\begin{resultbox}
\small
\textbf{Central equivalence} (source 10). For a computable binary-controlled system,
there is a recurrent run with a computable progress deadline if and only if
its accepting schedules contain a nonempty effectively closed subset,
if and only if it has an accepting scheduler of hyperimmune-free degree.

\textbf{Sharp classification} (sources 10, 03 and 11). Finite reachability, a fixed recurrence
deadline, some bounded-gap guarantee, some computable deadline, and
unrestricted existential recurrence have respective worst-case complexities
$\sig1$, $\ppi1$, $\sig2$, $\sig3$, $\an$; eventual permanence, universal recurrence and universal
eventual permanence add $\sig2$, $\ppi2$ and $\Pi^1_1$ (source 11).
Computable runs, computable deadlines and recurrence are strictly nested.

\textbf{Low-degree laws} (sources 03 and 11). One auxiliary-free polynomial of degree two
gives the labelled transition relation of a counter machine, with coefficient height at
most eight (source 03's control-vector encoding) or six (source 11's incoming-edge
encoding); a fixed such law already has $\an$-complete recurrence, and degree one does not suffice.

\textbf{Clock-degree spectra} (source 12, Part~VI). Every $\Delta^0_2$ degree is a least clock
degree; spectra with no least degree exist; a least clock degree is hyperarithmetic.
\end{resultbox}
\vfill
\small
\textbf{Status.} \wtag{} A merge of four AI-assisted, unrefereed research manuscripts, two
delivered in batch~62, one in batch~63 and one, printed as Part~VI, in batch~79 of ProveIt's
incoming reports (\cref{lbh:sec:provenance}).
The theorems carry conventional mathematical proofs; the accompanying Python programs perform
finite exact checks and are not proofs. No statement of this report is formalized in Lean or Rocq,
although the report sits beside ProveIt's formal Hilbert's-tenth-problem project, whose MRDP
interface it cites. Classical ingredients (MRDP, the low and hyperimmune-free basis theorems,
$\an$-hardness of recurrence, recursive-tree index sets) are credited; literature-wide priority is
claimed for nothing; no finite-fold or single-fold MRDP question is resolved. Every manuscript's own
status statement is printed in \cref{lbh:sec:manuscripts}.
\end{titlepage}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}
\setcounter{page}{1}

\begin{abstract}
\wtag{} The MRDP theorem represents finite successful computations by existential polynomial
equations; it does not represent every temporal property of a universal substrate. This report
merges three manuscripts that locate that boundary for \emph{recurrence}: the existence of a run
that visits an accepting region infinitely often.

A shared \emph{progress deadline} turns recurrence into an effectively closed condition, while
checking each finite horizon separately does not (three independent proofs and three explicit
counterexample machines). A computable deadline exists exactly when the accepting schedules
contain a nonempty effectively closed class, exactly when an accepting schedule of
hyperimmune-free degree exists; every c.e.\ degree is an exact deadline spectrum (source~10).
The resulting hierarchy---$\sig1$ reachability, $\ppi1$ fixed deadlines, $\sig2$ bounded gaps,
$\sig3$ computable deadlines and computable runs, $\an$ recurrence, with strict separations
inside one fixed system---is proved three times: for a fixed two-stack machine (source~10), for
a fixed degree-two counter graph (source~03), and for a degree-two incoming-edge law (source~11,
whose $\sig3$ rows range over effectively supplied machines); source~11 adds the temporal rows
$\sig2$, $\ppi2$ and $\Pi^1_1$.

On the certificate side, a counter network's labelled transition relation is the natural zero set
of one auxiliary-free polynomial of degree two (coefficient height at most~8 for a
control-vector encoding, source~03; at most~6 for an incoming-edge encoding with a
sum-of-squares quartic companion, source~11), and a single affine law cannot carry
$\an$-complete recurrence. A two-stack quartic has natural zeros in bijection with rule-labelled
runs meeting prescribed deadlines, with exactly $H(3+R+G)+k$ variables (source~10); for counter
machines the deadline certificate can be kept at degree two (source~11). Exact rational
piecewise-affine realizations exist on $[0,1]^3$ (source~10) and in a planar strip (source~11);
faithful block simulations preserve every recurrence notion; and no total computable reduction
maps any of these liveness properties to finite polynomial solvability.

\wtag{} Part~VI (source~12, added in batch~79) answers this report's question on deadline spectra
beyond c.e.\ cones in part. Clock problems are, up to uniform computable transformations, the upward
effectively closed mass problems of Baire space; every $\Delta^0_2$ degree and every degree of a
unique path through a recursive tree, including $\mathbf0^{(r)}$ and $\mathbf0^{(\omega)}$, is a
least clock degree; unions of incomparable cones have no least degree; and a least clock degree
contains a self-modulus and is hyperarithmetic. Its two-stack compiler reaches degree two with
$3(T+1)+9mT+k$ natural coordinates, a degree-two form of the quartic above.
\end{abstract}

\section{The research question and the contribution boundary}\label{lbh:sec:intro}

\subsection{What follows from universality, and what does not}
Suppose a computational model has finitely encoded configurations, an
effective finite execution semantics, and a finite event recognized by a
semidecision procedure. Its successful finite executions form a computably
enumerable relation. MRDP \cite{matiyasevich1970} then yields a fixed finite polynomial with natural
witnesses for that relation. This reasoning is independent of whether the
model is a Turing machine, a combinator system, a graph-rewriting machine,
or an exact geometric simulator.

There is an important limit. ``Some computation halts'' and ``some computation
returns to an accepting region infinitely often'' are different predicates.
An infinite execution is not a natural number unless extra restrictions make
it effectively describable, and existential quantification over all schedules
cannot simply be replaced by existential quantification over program indices.
This article asks three precise questions.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.},leftmargin=2.3em]
\item Which finite progress requirements allow the compactness argument that
turns success at every finite horizon into one successful infinite run?
\item Exactly what computational strength is needed for a recurrence schedule
when a computable clock exists?
\item Which of these temporal properties admit ordinary Diophantine
representations, which need alternating number quantifiers, and which need
an infinite witness?
\end{enumerate}

The answers are not merely the observation that every computably enumerable
relation is Diophantine. They identify the semantic obstruction, characterize
its degree-theoretic boundary, and connect it to a concrete polynomial
compiler and an unconventional geometric realization.

\subsection{Repository anchor and what was inspected}
The ProveIt repository contains a natural-number MRDP interface
\code{Diophantine.mrdp}, its converse \code{Diophantine.mrdp\_iff}, and an
adapter to Mathlib's \code{Dioph} predicate. The inspected theorem has the form
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:mrdp}
 S\text{ is c.e.}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists r\ \exists P\in\Z[X,Y_1,\ldots,Y_r]\ 
 \forall n\in\N:\quad n\in S\leftrightarrow\exists\vec y\in\N^r\ P(n,\vec y)=0.
\end{equation}
Both the polynomial and witness dimension are chosen before the input.
The repository's documentation explicitly separates this existence theorem
from numerical degree bounds and from an executable polynomial-extraction
algorithm \cite{proveit-mrdp,proveit-guide}.

For reproducibility, the actual public MRDP source was inspected at commit
\begin{center}
\small\code{b6bf6406a4c49017fedc6fba068b36c634987150}.
\end{center}
The MRDP guide and the Turing-degree project README were also inspected at
revision \code{b998f70c6886e6a00339a6f4a02ed8625324d357}.
These are source inspections, not a fresh build or an independent audit of
every repository theorem. The present proofs use ordinary MRDP as a classical
backend and do not rely on unrelated repository claims. The Turing-degree
README distinguishes its basic Lean theory from deeper Rocq developments and
from the separately conditional coarse-degree library \cite{proveit-degrees}.
No conditional coarse-degree theorem is used here.

\subsection{Prior work and the novelty ledger}
Recurrent computations were already used to obtain analytical-hierarchy
hardness in the work of Harel, Pnueli, and Stavi; their recurrence problem is
also explicitly recalled by Esparza and Krasotin \cite{hps,ek2025}.
Thus the $\an$ endpoint below is a classical phenomenon, not a new
undecidability breakthrough. Jockusch and Soare's low and hyperimmune-free
basis theorems are likewise classical \cite{js1972}; domination and its
interaction with effectively closed classes are studied further in
\cite{ng2013}. We use these results with explicit hypotheses.

Finitary acceptance asks for an unknown finite response bound.
Chatterjee and Fijalkow investigate this in an automata-theoretic setting
\cite{cf2011}. Their Borel classifications concern sets of infinite words;
our hierarchy classifies \emph{sets of finite input indices}. These are
not the same hierarchy. Moore's work supplies early dynamical universality
precedent \cite{moore1990}. Our geometric section is an elementary exact
construction for controlled guarded affine maps; it asserts neither
continuity nor noise-robust physical computation.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.31\linewidth}p{0.62\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Result or component & Status in this article\\
\midrule
MRDP; low and HIF basis theorems & Classical ingredients, cited and separated from the new construction.\\
Analytical recurrence hardness & Classical endpoint, with a self-contained reduction supplied.\\
Clock/closed-class/HIF equivalence & Derived here in one operational formulation; no priority claim.\\
The full clock hierarchy & Complete proofs and timing-safe universal realizations supplied here.\\
C.e.-degree clock spectra & Explicit uniform realization of every c.e. degree; proof supplied here.\\
Exact two-stack quartic compiler & Explicit construction, exact counts, conventional proof, finite tested implementation.\\
Geometric and parameter regimes & Constructive consequences with the quantified initial-data domain stated explicitly.\\
Lean certification of these additions & Not performed; an integration plan is given.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The substantive outcome is a unified theorem package and an implementable
arithmetization boundary, rather than an unsupported assertion that a
longstanding named conjecture has been settled. Independent mathematical
review and a wider priority search remain appropriate.
\subsection{How this report is organized}\label{lbh:sec:organization}
\wtag{} This report merges three manuscripts written independently on the same subject,
recurrence beyond halting and its Diophantine boundary. Two were delivered in batch~62 of
ProveIt's incoming reports (manuscripts~10 and~03 of that batch), the third in batch~63
(manuscript~01 of that batch). They are called \emph{source~10}, \emph{source~03} and
\emph{source~11} here, after the file prefixes of their shipped programs and data; source~11's
number continues this report's own sequence and is not a batch number
(\cref{lbh:sec:provenance}). Source~10, whose introduction the preceding subsections
reproduce, is the base: it treats arbitrary computable finitely branching (binary-controlled)
systems and proves the strongest conclusions (the hyperimmune-free characterization, exact
c.e.\ deadline spectra, the bounded-gap row). Sources~03 and~11 prove the same hierarchy by
other routes, through degree-two counter-machine transition laws of their own. The three titles,
abstracts, status statements and the introductions of sources~03 and~11 are printed in
\cref{lbh:sec:manuscripts}. A fourth manuscript, delivered in batch~79 (manuscript~04 of that
batch) and called \emph{source~12}, was written as an answer to \cref{lbh:q:spectra}; it was added
in the batch-79 write phase as Part~VI, after the existing Parts, without renumbering them
(\cref{lbh:cs:sec:source}).

After the conventions (\cref{lbh:conv:sec}) the material is arranged by subject in six Parts:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lXl@{}}
\toprule
Part & Subject & Sources\\
\midrule
I & Progress deadlines, compactness and the recurrence hierarchy (\cref{lbh:part:hierarchy}) & 10, 03, 11\\
II & Scheduler degrees, explicit separations and the deterministic case (\cref{lbh:part:degrees}) & 10, 03, 11\\
III & Low-degree transition laws and finite certificates (\cref{lbh:part:certificates}) & 03, 11, 10\\
IV & Transport, fairness and exact geometric realizations (\cref{lbh:part:realizations}) & 10, 03, 11\\
V & Fixed-arity signatures, no-go theorems, implementation, formalization and questions (\cref{lbh:part:limits}) & 10, 03, 11\\
VI & Clock-degree spectra beyond c.e.\ cones and quadratic progress certificates (\cref{lbh:part:spectra}) & 12\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
A result proved by several sources is printed once and credits each source; a genuinely
different proof is kept as a marked second (or third) route. The rule for which statements are
merged is stated in \cref{lbh:conv:union}.
\section{Conventions of the merged report}\label{lbh:conv:sec}

\subsection{Sources, tags and labels}
\wtag{} Text without a source marker is source~10's. Sections and subsections taken from the
other sources carry a marker such as \srctag{03} in their headings, and every theorem-like
statement they contribute lies inside such a section or names its source in its title. Text
written in the merge is marked \wtag. Inside printed source text, ``this article'', ``this
paper'', ``this report'', ``this manuscript'' and ``below'' refer to that source; theorem,
section and equation numbers are this report's. File names inside printed source text are the
delivered names; \cref{lbh:sec:provenance} and the README map them to the shipped names, and
displayed command listings keep the delivered layout, because the programs import one another by
their delivered names. The README gives rerun commands that work on a copy.

Every label carries the prefix \texttt{lbh:}. Source~10's labels are \texttt{lbh:}$+$its own
name, source~03's are \texttt{lbh:qd:}$+$its own name, and source~11's are
\texttt{lbh:ql:}$+$its own name; labels written in the merge use \texttt{lbh:conv:},
\texttt{lbh:part:}, \texttt{lbh:sec:} and \texttt{lbh:q:}. No source label was dropped.
Where a statement of source~03 or~11 is merged into a statement of source~10, its label sits on
the printed statement. Source~12 (Part~VI) has its own conventions, stated in
\cref{lbh:cs:sec:source}: its sections carry \srctag{12}, its labels are \texttt{lbh:cs:}$+$its own
name, and labels written for it in the batch-79 write use \texttt{lbh:cs:} and
\texttt{lbh:part:spectra}.

\subsection{What is printed once}\label{lbh:conv:union}
\wtag{} The three sources prove one hierarchy, but for different systems. A theorem about
\emph{all} systems of a class is the same theorem in each source, and is printed once:
deadline compactness (\cref{lbh:thm:compact}), the recursive-tree normal form
(\cref{lbh:lem:analytic-tree}), the diagonal-tree lemma (\cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc}), the
deterministic boundary (\cref{lbh:thm:deterministic}) and the $\Delta^0_2$ consequence of a
computable deadline (\cref{lbh:thm:core}). The other sources' proofs follow the printed statement
as marked second and third proofs, after a sentence recording any difference of hypotheses.
A completeness theorem, by contrast, is a statement about one fixed universal system; source~10
proves it for a two-stack machine, source~03 for its fixed quadratic counter graph~$E_U$, and
source~11 for its fixed quadratic law~$P_2$ (and, for the $\sig3$ rows, over effectively
supplied machines). Completeness for one fixed system does not imply it for another, so these
statements are each printed once for their own system, in sections marked as second and third
routes, and \cref{lbh:sec:onetable} collects all of them in one table. The same applies to the
separating examples, the transport theorems and the finite-certificate obstructions. Nothing of
any source was omitted: every result, proof, example, remark, limitation and question is
printed.

\subsection{Notions and symbols across the three sources}\label{lbh:conv:dictionary}
\wtag{} Each source keeps its own names inside its own text; no mathematical symbol of any source
was renamed. The dictionary below matches them; source~12's notation is matched against it in
\cref{lbh:cs:sec:notation}.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.2\textwidth}p{0.2\textwidth}p{0.23\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Notion & Source 10 & Source 03 & Source 11\\
\midrule
\endhead
some run is recurrent & $\Buchi(e)$, $\cB_e\neq\varnothing$ & $\REC(n)$ & $\Rec(\M,a)$, $\EGF F$\\
some run meets some computable deadline & $\Clock(e)$ & $\CB(n)$ & $\DRec(\M,a)$\\
some computable run is recurrent & $\Comp(e)$ & $\CP(n)$ & $\CRec(\M,a)$\\
some run meets a fixed deadline & $\Fixed_d(e)$ & $\BH_h(n)$ & $\forall K\,W_g(K)$\\
some finite accepting visit & $\Reach(e)$ & $\HIT(n)$ & $\EF F$\\
some infinite run & --- & $\INF(n)$ & $\EG F$ with $F$ everything\\
some run with bounded gaps & $\Gap(e)$ & --- & ---\\
deadline & $d(k)$, strictly increasing & $h(n,k)$, strictly increasing in $k$, $h\ge k$ & $g(k)$, nondecreasing, $g(k)\ge k$\\
visit count & $N_{e,f}(t)$, times $1,\ldots,t$ & $V_s(T)$, times $1,\ldots,T$ & $\tau_k(\rho)$; $R_{g,K}$ sums from $t=0$\\
$k$th visit time & $\tau_{e,f}(k)$ & $\tau_s(k)$ & $\tau_k(\rho)$\\
substrate & binary-controlled system; two-stack machine $\mathcal M$ & counter network $M$; universal $U$ & unit-counter machine $\M$\\
state & $(q,x,y)$, stack codes $\nu$ & $(p,x,h)$: one-hot control, counters, last-edge tag; $D=q+r+m$ & $(x,b)$: counters, incoming-edge tag; $d+m$\\
transition polynomial & $Q$, a quartic on a trace & $E_M$, degree $2$, height $\le8$ & $P_2$, degree $2$, height $\le6$; $P_4$, SOS quartic\\

\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The letters below change meaning across the sources; the tempting false reading is to carry a
letter across a section boundary.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Deadlines.} Source~10's $d$ and source~03's $h(n,k)$ are strictly increasing;
source~11's $g$ is only nondecreasing with $g(k)\ge k$ (so $g$ may repeat values). Source~10's
compactness proof applies verbatim to nondecreasing unbounded deadlines, and source~11 proves
that case separately (\cref{lbh:thm:compact}). The letter $g$ also names, in source~10, a path of
a tree (\cref{lbh:sec:examples}), the expansion bound $g_e$ of \cref{lbh:thm:transfer} and the
bounds $g_e$ of \cref{lbh:lem:hifbasis}; none of these is a deadline.
\item \textbf{$h$.} Source~10: a computable bound (\cref{lbh:thm:core}). Source~03: both the
last-edge tag in states $(p,x,h)$ and the deadline $h(n,k)$. Source~11: the halting time
$h(e)$ of $\varphi_e(e)$ (\cref{lbh:ql:eq:halttree}), and the homogenizing variable of
\cref{lbh:ql:sec:compiler}.
\item \textbf{$H$.} Source~10: the horizon of the quartic compiler. Source~03: a halting control
label in \cref{lbh:qd:eq:small}. Source~11: the history polynomial $H_T$ and a control label.
\item \textbf{$D$.} Source~10's $\Fixed=\mathsf D$ is a sans-serif predicate; source~03's and
source~11's italic $D$ is a state dimension; source~03's $T_D$ is its diagonal tree.
\item \textbf{$E$, $R$, $Q$, $A$, $T$.} $E$: source~10's number of empty guards, source~03's energy
$E_M$ and source~11's edge set. $R$: source~10's number of rules, a decidable relation in all
three sources, and source~11's certificate $R_{g,K}$. $Q$: source~10's trace quartic, and the set
of control labels in sources~03 and~11 (the rationals are $\Q$). $A$: the accepting predicate or
marker in all three sources (source~11's $A(b)$ is a linear form on tags), a control label in
the examples of sources~03 and~11, and a c.e.\ set in \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}. $T$: a tree or
a horizon in every source.
\item \textbf{Stack codes.} Source~10 uses the natural code $\nu$ (\cref{lbh:eq:stackcode}, base
four with a sentinel) and the rational code $\rho(w)=\sum_i d_i4^{-i}$, $d_i\in\{1,3\}$
(\cref{lbh:eq:rational-stack}). Source~11's rational code $\eta(w)=\sum_j(1+2a_j)4^{-j}$ with
$a_j\in\{0,1\}$ (\cref{lbh:ql:eq:stackcode}) \emph{is} source~10's $\rho$ after the digit
relabelling $d=1+2a$, and source~11's integer code $N(w)$ is base three. Source~10's $\eta$ is
something else: the real code of a whole schedule (\cref{lbh:eq:eta}). The letter $\rho$ also
names a run in source~11.
\item \textbf{Visit times.} Sources~10 and~03 count accepting visits at times $t\ge1$.
Source~11's $\tau_k(\rho)$ counts positions, and its certificate $R_{g,K}$ sums the marker from
$t=0$. The difference is harmless: in every construction of the three sources the initial
configuration is unmarked (source~11's initial state carries the dummy incoming edge~$e_0$,
whose target is the start label, and its start labels are unmarked).
\item \textbf{Heights $8$ and $6$.} Source~03's bound $\htp(E_M)\le8$ and source~11's
$\htp(P_2),\htp(P_4)\le6$ concern \emph{different encodings}: source~03 keeps a one-hot
control vector and separate source and target residuals, source~11 drops the control vector and
merges both into one flow residual. Each bound is sharp for its own compiler
(\cref{lbh:qd:thm:height}, \cref{lbh:ql:sec:height}). The tempting false reading ``source~11
improves source~03's sharp height~$8$ to~$6$'' is wrong; neither contradicts the other's
sharpness (\cref{lbh:sec:threecompilers}).
\end{itemize}
\section{The three manuscripts}\label{lbh:sec:manuscripts}
\wtag{} Each subsection gives one manuscript's title and date as delivered, its abstract and
status statement verbatim, and, for sources~03 and~11, its own introduction; source~10's
introduction is \cref{lbh:sec:intro}. (Source~12, the manuscript of Part~VI, is introduced in
\cref{lbh:cs:sec:ms12}.) The delivered author lines named the assistant used or the
addressee of the manuscript; they are replaced here by the neutral description ``AI-assisted
research manuscript'' (\cref{lbh:sec:provenance}).

\subsection{Source 10: Beyond Halting}\label{lbh:sec:ms10}
\wtag{} \emph{Beyond Halting: A Progress-Modulus Hierarchy for Diophantine Liveness. Effective
compactness, scheduler degrees, and rational piecewise-affine computation.} Research article and
exact certificate implementation; AI-assisted research manuscript, dated September 30, 2026.
Batch~62, manuscript~10; base of this report. Its title-page summary box read as follows.
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Central equivalence.} For a computable binary-controlled system,
there is a recurrent run with a computable progress deadline if and only if
its accepting schedules contain a nonempty effectively closed subset,
if and only if it has an accepting scheduler of hyperimmune-free degree.

\textbf{Sharp classification.} Finite reachability, a fixed recurrence
deadline, some bounded-gap guarantee, some computable deadline, and
unrestricted existential recurrence have respective worst-case complexities
\[
 \sig1,\qquad \ppi1,\qquad \sig2,\qquad \sig3,\qquad \an.
\]
All five are realized by one fixed two-stack system and its exact rational
piecewise-affine realization.
\end{quote}
Its status statement:
\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{Status.} This is a research manuscript with detailed mathematical proofs
and a tested finite-horizon compiler, not a claim of a new proof of MRDP,
a solution of the finite-fold conjecture, or established publication priority.
The basis theorems and classical recurrence hardness are explicitly attributed.
The integrated progress-clock formulation, exact compiler, and applications
are developed here. No new Lean formalization is included or claimed.
\end{quote}
Its abstract:
\begin{quote}\small
The Matiyasevich--Robinson--Davis--Putnam theorem represents finite successful
computations by existential polynomial equations. It does not represent every
property of a universal computational substrate: the temporal predicate and
its quantifiers matter. We analyze existential recurrent reachability in
computable, finitely branching systems through \emph{progress deadlines}.
A shared deadline converts recurrence into an effectively closed condition;
checking each finite horizon independently without retaining earlier deadlines
does not. We prove a three-way equivalence between a computable deadline,
a nonempty effectively closed family of accepting schedules, and an accepting
schedule of hyperimmune-free Turing degree. We give full upper-bound and
hardness constructions for the resulting arithmetical/analytical hierarchy,
separate computable clocks from computable schedulers, and construct a system
with recurrent runs but no computable deadline. More generally, every c.e.
Turing degree is realized as the exact minimum oracle degree needed to
compute a successful deadline.

An explicit two-stack compiler produces a nonnegative quartic polynomial
whose natural zeros correspond bijectively to rule-labelled finite runs
satisfying all prescribed deadlines. With horizon $H$, $R$ rules, $G$ top
guards, $E$ empty guards, and $k$ deadlines, it uses exactly
$H(3+R+G)+k$ variables and $H(4R+E+2G+2)+k$ quadratic residuals.
The same semantics are realized by a fixed guarded rational piecewise-affine
relation on a compact three-dimensional cube. A fourth coordinate internalizes
the scheduler as an initial real parameter, yielding a deterministic map.
We explain the different classifications for rational, computable, and
unrestricted initial parameters, derive fixed-arity polynomial quantifier
normal forms using ProveIt's MRDP interface, and specify concrete subsequent
formalization and research tasks. The accompanying dependency-free Python
implementation includes exact arithmetic tests and an exported sample
certificate.
\end{quote}

\subsection{Source 03: Quadratic Diophantine Dynamics}\label{lbh:sec:ms03}
\wtag{} \emph{Quadratic Diophantine Dynamics: Recurrence, computable schedules, and the limits of
finite certificates.} Computability / Diophantine representation; AI-assisted research
manuscript, dated 30 September 2026. Batch~62, manuscript~03. Its abstract:
\begin{quote}\small
A finite counter network can be compiled into a single integer polynomial
$E(s,s')$ of degree two whose natural zero set is exactly its labelled
transition relation. The compiler uses no existential auxiliary coordinates:
its state dimension is $q+r+m$ for $q$ controls, $r$ counters and $m$ edges,
and every expanded coefficient has absolute value at most eight. A mixed
nonnegative zero-test term, rather than a squared zero-test equation, is the
key to retaining quadratic degree. Binary branching is preserved globally.

This normal form supports a sharp study of infinite computation. For one fixed
universal polynomial, existence of a recurrent path is $\Sigma^1_1$-complete;
existence of a recurrent path with some total computable return-time bound is
$\Sigma^0_3$-complete; and existence of a computable recurrent path is also
$\Sigma^0_3$-complete. Nevertheless, the last two properties are distinct, and
both are strictly weaker than unrestricted recurrence. Explicit recursive-tree
constructions separate them. A nine-coordinate example refutes the tempting
inference from arbitrarily many finite visits to one recurrent path. A
matching affine obstruction proves that degree two is minimal for this
uniformly bounded-branching recurrence universality. We also give an explicit
weak-fairness translation, finite-certificate boundaries, executable compiler
checks, and a proposed ProveIt integration plan.
\end{quote}
Its status statement:
\begin{quote}\small
\noindent\textbf{Status.} Conventional proofs and executed finite tests; not Lean- or
Rocq-verified, independently refereed, or claimed to solve a named published
open problem. Classical recurrence and fairness undecidability are credited
as background. The exact normal form, quantitative bounds, and combined
separation results are the contributions developed here; literature-wide
priority remains unverified.
\end{quote}
Its introduction follows.
\subsection{The research question and the resulting contribution\srctag{03}}

The finite computational behavior of an effective machine has a familiar
arithmetic description: a successful execution is a finite object, and a
checker can verify it. The more delicate question is whether the same
representation remains possible for a machine that must keep succeeding
forever. The issue is not the exotic appearance of the substrate. It is the
quantification over an entire infinite trajectory.

ProveIt's inspected Diophantine library already provides exact finite
iteration and finite reachability interfaces, as well as MRDP in both
directions~\cite{proveit-trace,qd-proveit-readme}. We therefore investigate the next
semantic boundary instead of merely applying MRDP again to another effective
halting predicate. The central questions formulated in this manuscript are:
can one obtain a particularly small-degree local arithmetic dynamics;
what infinite properties survive as ordinary Diophantine sets; and what
changes when the times of recurrent events admit a computable bound?

\subsubsection{A quadratic, not quartic, local representation}

The construction uses an energy of the form
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:informalenergy}
 E=\text{sum of squares of affine residuals}
       +\text{sum of nonnegative selector--counter products}.
\end{equation}
The second part enforces zero tests. Squaring those products would raise the
degree to four, but is unnecessary on the nonnegative orthant. Integrality of
a selector vector whose coordinates sum to one chooses exactly one edge.
The selected edge is written into the successor state, so two labelled edges
remain distinct without existential choices hidden behind a transition.

The resulting substrate is a discrete zero-energy relation, not iteration of
a polynomial map and not a continuous differential equation. This distinction
is essential. The relation can branch, its coordinates are natural numbers,
and its zero tests are complementarity constraints. Integrality supplies
the discrete choice.

\subsubsection{The main theorem package}

The following overview refers to precise statements proved below. All
completeness assertions use computable many-one reductions.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.57\textwidth}p{0.35\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Result & Quantitative or logical conclusion\\
\midrule
Local counter-network compiler & Degree $2$; height $\leq8$; dimension
$q+r+m$; no existential auxiliaries\\
Affine obstruction & Degree $1$ cannot have the same recurrent universality
with a uniform finite branching bound\\
Finite hit in a fixed universal system & $\Sigma^0_1$-complete; ordinary
Diophantine\\
Existence of an infinite path & $\Pi^0_1$-complete\\
Unrestricted recurrent path & $\Sigma^1_1$-complete\\
A prescribed computable return-time bound & $\Pi^0_1$ upper bound; sharp
for suitable uniformly specified families\\
Some computable return-time bound & $\Sigma^0_3$-complete\\
Some computable recurrent path & $\Sigma^0_3$-complete, but strictly stronger
than the preceding existence property\\
One always-enabled weak-fairness obligation & $\Sigma^1_1$-complete;
branching at most $3$ after translation\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Here ``some computable return-time bound'' means that a bound exists for one
path, not that an algorithm must compute a bound from the input. It also does
not mean that the bounded path itself is computable. These two distinctions
are responsible for the middle part of the classification.

\subsubsection{What is and is not new}

Counter-machine universality is classical~\cite{shepherdson-sturgis}.
Highly undecidable recurrence and its relation to infinite trees and fairness
are also classical: Harel's work is directly relevant~\cite{harel1985,harel1986}.
Finkel gives related analytical-hierarchy classifications for infinite tiling
systems~\cite{finkel2008}. We do not present the general phenomenon of
$\Sigma^1_1$ recurrence as a discovery.

The research contribution here is the explicit conjunction of a quadratic,
height-eight, auxiliary-free transition normal form; a degree-one lower
obstruction under the same global branching requirement; the exact
computable-deadline classification; and concrete strict-separation
constructions that survive this arithmetic compilation. The proofs are given
in full except for explicitly identified standard computability background.
No claim of literature-wide priority is made. The construction for a fixed
universal system is effective but its full low-level universal interpreter is
not numerically instantiated in the delivered code. The code does instantiate
and test the local compiler and the small counterexamples.
\subsection{Source 11: Quadratic Laws, Infinite Computation}\label{lbh:sec:ms11}
\wtag{} \emph{Quadratic Laws, Infinite Computation: Exact Diophantine Dynamics, Planar Stack
Machines, and the Boundary Created by Recurrence Deadlines.} AI-assisted research report,
presented by its author line as an extension of the Diophantine and computability program in
\repo; dated September 30, 2026. Batch~63, manuscript~01; its file prefix is~\texttt{11-}. Its
abstract (source~11 has no separate status statement; its status is stated in its abstract, in
\cref{lbh:ql:sec:repository} and in its theorem-status ledger):
\begin{quote}\small
Finite computation and infinite behavior have sharply different arithmetic
content, even when they obey the same low-degree local law. We construct, from
any binary-branching unit-counter machine with $d$ counters and $m$ edges
including one initialization edge, a polynomial
$P_2(X,Y)\in\Z[X,Y]$ in $2(d+m)$ variables. It has degree at most two,
coefficient height at most six, and is nonnegative on the entire nonnegative
real orthant. Its natural-number zeros are exactly the machine steps, without
existential transition witnesses. A companion polynomial $P_4$ has the same
natural zeros, the same coefficient bound and dimension, and is globally a sum
of squares of degree at most four. Both preserve edge-labelled finite and
infinite computations exactly.

A fixed instance of this construction has a $\Sigma^1_1$-complete recurrent
reachability problem, although its one-step graph is semilinear and has
out-degree at most two. We then isolate a finer boundary: existence of a
recurrent run with a computable bound on its $k$th marked visit is
$\Sigma^0_3$-complete. Existence of a computable recurrent run is also
$\Sigma^0_3$-complete, but is a strictly stronger property. Two explicit
diagonal constructions separate these notions from unrestricted recurrence.
A prescribed total computable deadline restores an effective compactness
criterion, expressed by compatible finite quadratic certificates.

The same distinctions transfer to exact rational piecewise-affine dynamics in
a bounded planar region through an explicit two-stack coding. The article
includes proofs, a coefficient audit, an executable compiler, exact finite
tests, a repository integration plan, and further research questions. The
classical high-undecidability results are credited; the coefficient-controlled
normal form and deadline refinements are presented as proved results of this
report, not as an established claim of literature priority.
\end{quote}
Its keywords: Diophantine representation; counter machines; B\"uchi recurrence; effective
compactness; computable deadlines; piecewise-affine computation; analytic hierarchy. Its
introduction follows.
\subsection{The research question and its scope\srctag{11}}

\subsubsection{Why another compilation theorem is not enough}
The MRDP theorem identifies the computably enumerable subsets of $\N$ with
sets of the form
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:mrdp}
 n\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists w\in\N^r\;H(n,w)=0,
 \qquad H\in\Z[n,w_1,\ldots,w_r].
\end{equation}
The polynomial and its finite number of witnesses are fixed before $n$ is
chosen. The repository exposes precisely this interface, including input zero,
and proves its converse as well \cite{ql-repo-mrdp}. Thus finite acceptance in any
effectively presented Turing-complete substrate is already Diophantine at the
level of existence. Merely changing the substrate does not by itself improve
that theorem.

A more demanding question is what the same arithmetic representation says
about a computation that must continue forever, repeatedly make progress,
or obey a schedule. These properties occur in interactive computation,
reactive systems, nondeterministic verification, infinite tilings, and exact
dynamical simulations. A finite accepting history is not a certificate that
an infinite history exists with infinitely many marked events.

This report addresses three concrete questions.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.},leftmargin=3em]
\item How simple can a single polynomial local transition law be while its
infinite behavior has genuinely non-arithmetical complexity?
\item Which additional hypothesis makes finite-prefix reasoning valid for
recurrence, and what is the exact complexity of existentially supplying that
hypothesis as an algorithm?
\item Do these distinctions survive translation to an unconventional,
low-dimensional computational substrate without relying on arbitrary real
oracles or hidden infinite-precision input advice?
\end{enumerate}
The answers are a nonnegative quadratic local law, a computable recurrence
deadline, and an exact rational planar stack realization, respectively.

\subsubsection{A statement of the principal conclusions}
\begin{theorem}[Summary of the proved results]\label{lbh:ql:thm:summary}
The following assertions hold.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item Every finite binary-branching unit-counter machine has the explicit
witness-free quadratic and quartic presentations of
\cref{lbh:ql:thm:compiler}, with coefficient height at most six and state dimension
$d+m$.
\item For one fixed quadratic presentation, the set of effectively encoded
initial states admitting a run with infinitely many marked visits is
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete. Its one-step natural relation is nevertheless
semilinear and has out-degree at most two.
\item Over effectively presented finite counter machines, both existence of
a computable recurrent run and existence of a recurrent run with some total
computable deadline are $\Sigma^0_3$-complete. The former implies the latter,
but not conversely. Unrestricted recurrence is strictly more general than
both.
\item For a prescribed total computable deadline, recurrence is equivalent
to the satisfiability of every member of a compatible family of finite
quadratic systems. Each system has unique slack variables once the finite
path is fixed.
\item The qualitative distinctions in (ii)--(iv) are realizable by exact
rational piecewise-affine two-stack dynamics in the plane.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
These are statements about ordinary standard natural numbers and actual
infinite sequences. They are not claims about nonstandard finite runs or
formal satisfiability inside an arbitrary model of arithmetic.

\subsubsection{What is classical, what is developed here}
The analytic completeness of recurrent nondeterministic counter computation
is classical, associated in particular with Harel's work on infinite trees,
dominoes, and fairness \cite{harel1986}. A recent primary source explicitly records
the two-counter formulation \cite[Appendix A]{chodil-kucera}. Generalized shifts
and computational dynamics likewise have a substantial literature
\cite{moore1991}. We do not rename these established results as new discoveries.

The work developed here is the exact incoming-edge quadratic normal form,
its uniform coefficient audit, the compatible deadline certificates, the
explicit strict witness separations, and their integration into one
substrate-preserving framework. The $\Sigma^0_3$ arguments are proved directly
rather than asserted by analogy. Related recursive-tree index-set techniques
are also classical. In particular, the $\Sigma^0_3$ classification of trees
having a computable path appears in \cite[Theorem 2.10(e)]{cenzer-marek-remmel};
our proof specializes this machinery to recurrent runs and deadlines. A comprehensive priority check
for the precise combination of refinements in this report remains necessary.
No named long-standing open conjecture is claimed resolved, and no new Lean
or Rocq verification is claimed.
\part{Progress deadlines, compactness and the recurrence hierarchy}\label{lbh:part:hierarchy}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Part~I prints source~10's semantics, compactness theorem and hierarchy, the
conventions of sources~03 and~11, the three counterexamples to naive compactness, and the
second and third routes to the hierarchy through the fixed quadratic systems of sources~03
and~11. The degree-theoretic characterization of computable clocks is in Part~II.}
\section{Operational semantics and progress clocks}\label{lbh:sec:model}

\subsection{A fixed binary-controlled effective system}
\begin{definition}\label[definition]{lbh:def:system}
A \emph{computable binary-controlled system} consists of natural-number-coded
configurations $c$, a total computable transition function
$\delta(c,b)$ for $b\in\B$, a computable initial configuration $i(e)$ for
finite input $e\in\N$, and a decidable accepting predicate $A(c)$.
A schedule $f\in\Cantor$ determines
\[
 c_0=i(e),\qquad c_{t+1}=\delta(c_t,f(t)).
\]
Halting, disabled actions, or invalid configurations can be completed by an
absorbing rejecting sink. A schedule is an arbitrary infinite bit sequence,
not necessarily computable.
\end{definition}

The transition computation is total; therefore the finite run determined
by a finite bit string can be computed completely. Deterministic steps may
ignore their action bit. More general effective finite branching can be
encoded by finite action alphabets, but the binary formulation makes the
compactness and degree arguments transparent. For a rule-based compiler,
we retain the rule label as part of the finite trace.

We count accepting visits at times $1,2,\ldots$, excluding the initial time:
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:count}
 N_{e,f}(t)=\sum_{u=1}^{t}\ind_{A(c_u)},\qquad
 \tau_{e,f}(k)=\min\{t:N_{e,f}(t)\ge k\}.
\end{equation}
The second expression is defined only when the $k$th visit occurs. We often
suppress $e$ in the notation. The set of accepting schedules is
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:buchi-class}
 \cB_e=\{f:\forall k\ge1\ \exists t\ N_{e,f}(t)\ge k\}.
\end{equation}
Thus acceptance is the B\"uchi condition of infinitely many visits.

\begin{definition}\label[definition]{lbh:def:deadline}
A \emph{deadline} is a strictly increasing function
$d:\N_{>0}\to\N_{>0}$. In particular $d(k)\ge k$.
A schedule meets $d$ when $N_{e,f}(d(k))\ge k$ for every $k\ge1$.
Write
\[
 \cK_{e,d}=\{f:\forall k\ge1\ N_{e,f}(d(k))\ge k\}.
\]
For a finite deadline vector $\vec d=(d(1),\ldots,d(k))$, let
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:finite-feasibility}
 \Fin(e,\vec d)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\sigma\in\B^{d(k)}\ \forall j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}:\quad
 N_{e,\sigma}(d(j))\ge j.
\end{equation}
The empty vector is feasible. Every earlier deadline is retained.
\end{definition}

For a finite explicit vector, $\Fin$ is decidable: enumerate its finitely
many binary schedules and compute their finite runs. The procedure can be
very expensive, but the hierarchy requires decidability, not an efficient
bound. When $d$ is computable, $(e,k)\mapsto\Fin(e,d\pref k)$ is decidable.

\subsection{Four recurrence predicates and one finite predicate}
For a fixed computable deadline $d$, define
\begin{align}
 \Fixed_d(e)&\iff\cK_{e,d}\ne\varnothing,\label{lbh:eq:D}\\
 \Gap(e)&\iff\exists f\ \exists b\ge1\ \forall t\ge0:
       N_{e,f}(t+b)-N_{e,f}(t)\ge1,\label{lbh:eq:BG}\\
 \Clock(e)&\iff\exists\text{ a total computable deadline }d:
       \cK_{e,d}\ne\varnothing,\label{lbh:eq:CD}\\
 \Buchi(e)&\iff\cB_e\ne\varnothing,\label{lbh:eq:B}\\
 \Reach(e)&\iff\exists f\ \exists t\ge1:\ A(c_t).\label{lbh:eq:reach}
\end{align}
The gap condition includes the initial delay: the first visit occurs by $b$,
and successive visits are at most $b$ steps apart. Requiring a bound only
after some finite prefix gives the same \emph{existence} predicate, because
a finite prefix and finitely many initial gaps can be absorbed into a larger
bound. However, a linear bound $\tau_f(k)\le bk$ is not the same as a bound
on every individual gap. We never use those two notions interchangeably.

There are immediate inclusions
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:inclusions}
 \Fixed_{\mathrm{id}}\ \subseteq\ \Gap\ \subseteq\ \Clock\ \subseteq\ \Buchi,
 \qquad\text{where }\mathrm{id}(k)=k.
\end{equation}
The second inclusion follows by taking $d(k)=bk$.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The next subsection prints source~03's conventions (its Section~2). Its
predicates $\HIT,\REC,\CB,\CP$ are source~10's $\Reach,\Buchi,\Clock,\Comp$
(\cref{lbh:conv:dictionary}); its systems are effectively finitely branching rather than
binary-controlled.}
\subsection{Conventions and logical prerequisites\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:definitions}

Throughout, $\N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. All polynomial coefficients belong to $\Z$;
all state and witness coordinates belong to $\N$ unless another domain is
explicitly specified. For a polynomial $P$, its coefficient height $\htp(P)$
is the maximum absolute value of an expanded coefficient. This is unrelated
to the size of a state or the duration of a computation.

\begin{definition}[Effective transition system]
An effectively finitely branching system consists of a decidable state space
with an algorithm that, on every state, returns its complete finite list of
successors. In our applications the state space is $\N^D$, the list has length
at most two, the initial state $c(n)$ is computable from an input $n$, and a
marked-state predicate $A(s)$ is decidable.
\end{definition}

The complete-list requirement is stronger than saying that the edge relation
is decidable and every vertex has at most two successors. Merely enumerating
possible successors does not in general tell us when that enumeration is
finished. Our compiler supplies the complete-list algorithm directly from
the finite edge table.

A finite path of length $T$ has states $s_0,\ldots,s_T$. An infinite path has
states $s_t$ for every $t\in\N$. A path starts at $c(n)$ when $s_0=c(n)$.
We count marked visits at positive times; thus
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:visits}
 V_s(T)=\#\{t:1\leq t\leq T,\ A(s_t)\}.
\end{equation}
Omitting a possible visit at time zero does not change recurrence. A recurrent
path is an infinite path with unbounded $V_s(T)$. Its $k$th marked time, for
$k\geq1$, is $\tau_s(k)=\min\{T:V_s(T)\geq k\}$.

\begin{definition}[Four existence predicates]
For a fixed system and initial-state map, let
\begin{align*}
 \HIT(n)&:\quad \text{some finite path reaches a marked state},\\
 \REC(n)&:\quad \text{some infinite path is recurrent},\\
 \CB(n)&:\quad \text{some recurrent path has a total computable bound on }
                         \tau_s(k),\\
 \CP(n)&:\quad \text{some recurrent path }t\mapsto s_t\text{ is computable}.
\end{align*}
The definition of $\CB$ permits an arbitrary, possibly noncomputable path.
Only its numerical upper bound must be computable.
\end{definition}

A bound can always be replaced by a strictly increasing bound $h$ with
$h(k)\geq k$: replace $g(k)$ by $k+\max_{1\leq j\leq k}g(j)$. Consequently
we impose these two conditions on deadline functions without loss of
generality when existentially quantifying over them.

\subsubsection{Arithmetic and analytic complexity}

A predicate is $\Sigma^0_1$ when it has the form $\exists u\,R(n,u)$ with
computable $R$; it is $\Pi^0_1$ when it has the form $\forall u\,R(n,u)$.
The pattern $\exists a\forall b\exists c\,R(n,a,b,c)$ defines
$\Sigma^0_3$. Finite blocks of consecutive quantifiers can be coded by single
natural numbers. A $\Sigma^1_1$ predicate permits an existential function
quantifier, followed by an arithmetic predicate:
\[
 n\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists f\in\N^\N\;\theta(n,f).
\]
A complete set for a class is one to which every set in that class reduces
computably. We use the standard strictness of the arithmetic and analytical
hierarchies, and in particular that $\Sigma^1_1$-complete sets are not
arithmetic~\cite{finkel2008} (\wtag{} a textbook reference is \cite{rogers1967}).

\begin{definition}[Ordinary Diophantine representation]
A set $S\subseteq\N$ has an ordinary Diophantine representation when there
exist a fixed finite $v$ and $P\in\Z[X,Y_1,\ldots,Y_v]$ such that
\[
 n\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists y_1,\ldots,y_v\in\N\;P(n,y_1,\ldots,y_v)=0.
\]
\end{definition}

Every such set is computably enumerable, by exhaustive enumeration of finite
witness tuples. The converse is MRDP, which is available in the inspected
ProveIt library~\cite{qd-proveit-readme}. An infinite list of polynomial equations
with an existentially quantified infinite sequence is \emph{not} an ordinary
Diophantine representation in this sense.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The next subsection prints source~11's semantics (its Section~2). Its
$\Rec,\CRec,\DRec$ are source~10's $\Buchi,\Comp,\Clock$; its deadlines are nondecreasing
(\cref{lbh:conv:dictionary}).}
\subsection{Semantics before equations\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:semantics}

\subsubsection{Counter machines with at most two choices}
A \emph{unit-counter machine} consists of a finite set $Q$ of control labels,
a start label $q_0$, a marked subset $F\subseteq Q$, and $d\geq1$ natural
counters. Its edges are of four types:
\[
 \mathsf{inc}(i),\qquad \mathsf{dec}(i),\qquad
 \mathsf{zero}(i),\qquad \mathsf{nop}.
\]
An increment adds one to counter $i$. A decrement is enabled only when that
counter is positive and subtracts one. A zero edge is enabled only when the
counter is zero and changes no counter. A no-op is always enabled and changes
no counter. Every ordinary control label has at most two outgoing edges.
Different edges may have identical endpoints and effects; they remain distinct
choices.

The usual zero-test/decrement instruction has one zero edge and one decrement
edge. A nondeterministic binary choice has two no-op edges. Standard
counter-machine programs can therefore be put into this form by inserting
finitely many control labels. Marker visits are assigned only to the intended
instruction boundaries, not to every internal simulation state.

A configuration is $(q,x)$ with $x\in\N^d$. A finite path of length $T$ contains
$T$ edges and $T+1$ configurations. An infinite run has one configuration at
each $t\in\N$. Deadlocks have no successors. All temporal properties below
quantify over genuine infinite runs unless explicitly stated otherwise.

\subsubsection{Three different recurrent witnesses}
For a marked run $\rho$, let $\tau_k(\rho)$ be the time of its $k$th marked
configuration, where $k=1,2,\ldots$. Visits count time positions, not distinct
marked configurations. Define:
\begin{align*}
 \Rec(\M,a)
 &\iff \exists\rho\text{ from }a\;\forall k\;\tau_k(\rho)\text{ exists},\\
 \CRec(\M,a)
 &\iff \exists\rho\text{ total computable from }a\;
                   \forall k\;\tau_k(\rho)\text{ exists},\\
 \DRec(\M,a)
 &\iff \exists g\text{ total computable}\;\exists\rho\text{ from }a\;
                   \forall k\;\tau_k(\rho)\leq g(k).
\end{align*}
A computable run means that the complete encoded state at time $t$ can be
computed from $t$. The deadline $g$ is allowed to depend on the instance.
There is no requirement that an algorithm find $g$ from the instance.
We normalize deadlines to be nondecreasing with $g(k)\geq k$; replacing a
bound by $\max\{k,g(1),\ldots,g(k)\}$ preserves its meaning.

The quantifier order in $\DRec$ is important. The same computable function
must bound all marked visits of one run. The run itself need not be computable.
An arbitrary recurrent run always has a deadline as a set-theoretic function,
namely its visit-time function. That function need not be computable or
computably dominated.

\subsubsection{Effective finite branching}
In the machines above, the complete finite successor list of any state is
computable. This is stronger than merely knowing that a decidable relation has
finite fibers. Our finite-tree searches always use the complete successor
list. The constructions below preserve this property and preserve the bound
of two successors.
\section{A shared deadline restores compactness}\label{lbh:sec:compact}\label{lbh:qd:sec:deadlines}\label{lbh:ql:sec:deadlines}

\begin{theorem}[Coherent deadline compactification]\label{lbh:thm:compact}\label{lbh:qd:thm:deadline}\label{lbh:ql:thm:deadlinecompact}
For every system, input $e$, and deadline $d$, computable or not,
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:compact-equivalence}
 \cK_{e,d}\ne\varnothing
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall k\ge1\ \Fin(e,d\pref k).
\end{equation}
When $d$ is computable, $\cK_{e,d}$ is an effectively closed subset of Cantor
space, given uniformly by a computable binary tree.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For each $j$, the condition $N_{e,f}(d(j))\ge j$ depends only on the first
$d(j)$ bits of $f$. Its solution set $C_j$ is therefore clopen. Feasibility
of the first $k$ deadlines says that
$C_1\cap\cdots\cap C_k$ is nonempty. These intersections are nested compact
sets. Compactness of Cantor space gives a point in their intersection,
which is exactly $\cK_{e,d}$. The reverse implication follows by truncation.

For effectiveness, put a finite string $\sigma$ into $T_{e,d}$ precisely
when it satisfies every deadline $d(j)\le|\sigma|$ that is already due.
Because $d$ is increasing, compute its values until the first one exceeds
$|\sigma|$, then check the finite list of requirements. This is a total
decision procedure, and the tree is prefix closed. Its infinite paths are
exactly $\cK_{e,d}$. The assertion also follows directly from K\"onig's
lemma applied to this finitely branching tree.
\end{proof}
\paragraph{Second route (source 03).}
\wtag{} Source~03 states \cref{lbh:thm:compact} for effectively finitely branching systems with
an input-dependent deadline prescription (its Section ``Prescribed deadlines restore effective
compactness''). It fixes a total computable function $h(n,k)$, $k\geq1$, strictly increasing in
$k$ and satisfying $h(n,k)\geq k$, and, for a fixed input $n$, defines
\[
 \BH_h(n):\quad\exists\text{ infinite path }s\text{ from }c(n)
       \ \forall k\geq1\;V_s(h(n,k))\geq k.
\]
This asks for a path meeting every prescribed deadline, not merely a path that eventually
reaches each finite visit count. Its theorem (``Deadline compactness'') states that in an
effectively finitely branching system $\BH_h(n)$ is equivalent to
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:deadlinefinite}
 \forall k\geq1\ \exists\text{ a path of length }h(n,k)
 \text{ meeting every deadline }h(n,j),\ 1\leq j\leq k.
\end{equation}
For each $n,k$ the inner finite-path condition is decidable. In particular,
$\BH_h$ is $\Pi^0_1$.
\begin{proof}[Second proof of \cref{lbh:qd:thm:deadline} (source 03)]
An infinite witnessing path supplies each finite prefix in
\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:deadlinefinite}. Conversely, form the tree of finite paths that
respect every deadline that has already elapsed. More precisely, a prefix
of length $T$ belongs to this tree if
\[
 \forall j\text{ with }h(n,j)\leq T:\quad V_s(h(n,j))\geq j.
\]
This tree is prefix closed. It is finitely branching because it is a subtree
of the original finite-path tree. A path of length $h(n,k)$ meeting the first
$k$ deadlines belongs to it: strict increase ensures that these are exactly
the deadlines that have elapsed. By~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:deadlinefinite} and the
unboundedness of $h(n,k)$, this tree has arbitrarily long paths. K\"onig's
lemma gives an infinite branch. Each fixed deadline eventually lies inside
a prefix, so the branch meets all deadlines.

Compute $h(n,1),\ldots,h(n,k)$, enumerate the complete finite successor tree
through depth $h(n,k)$, and check the visits at each required time. This is
a terminating decision procedure for the inner predicate, regardless of
how large the tree is. Universal quantification over $k$ gives a
$\Pi^0_1$ definition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[All earlier deadlines must be checked]
Replacing the inner condition by ``some prefix has $k$ visits by time
$h(n,k)$'' can lose constraints on the first visits. The proof uses the
closed tree of prefixes satisfying \emph{every elapsed deadline}. It does
not permit an unrelated choice of early deadlines for each $k$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Sharpness of the deadline upper bound]\label[proposition]{lbh:qd:prop:deadline-sharp}
The $\Pi^0_1$ upper bound is sharp for uniformly coded effective systems with
a fixed computable deadline prescription. It is also attained by a suitable
input-dependent total computable deadline function on the fixed interpreter
$U$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the first assertion, take a deterministic effective simulation of a
machine, mark every live state, and let halting have no continuation. With
$h(k)=k$, meeting every deadline is exactly nonhalting. The next-state
algorithm can compute a simulated tape step as one transition, so this
already proves the upper bound cannot generally be improved.

For the fixed counter interpreter, use a program that simulates one source
step and then emits, repeating until the source machine halts. Put these
wrapper programs in a decidably tagged input subfamily. For such an input
$n$ and integer $k$, simulate the actual counter run until it either halts
or completes its $k$th emit. This always terminates: at most $k$ source
steps are required, and each implemented source step has finite duration.
Measure the elapsed counter time, and monotonize the resulting function
in $k$. Call it $h_0(n,k)$; outside the tagged subfamily, set $h_0(n,k)=k$.
It is total computable. On a nonhalting wrapped input, the actual first $k$
emits occur within the bound. On a halting input, the actual program has
only finitely many emits and therefore fails some deadline. Thus
$\BH_{h_0}$ is $\Pi^0_1$-hard on this coded subfamily. \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:deadline}
gives membership.
\end{proof}

No fixed-deadline computable selection of a path has been proved. Compactness
establishes the existence of a compatible infinite branch; it need not
supply a computable branch. \Cref{lbh:qd:sec:separations} makes this failure
explicit.
\paragraph{Third route (source 11).}
\wtag{} Source~11 proves the same theorem (its ``Deadline compactness'') for effectively
finitely branching machines and one fixed total computable deadline $g$ normalized only to be
nondecreasing with $g(k)\geq k$. It lets $W_g(K)$ mean that there is a path of length $g(K)$
from $a$ whose first $j$ marked visits occur by $g(j)$, simultaneously for every $1\leq j\leq K$,
and proves
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:deadlinecompact}
 \exists\rho\;\forall k\;\tau_k(\rho)\leq g(k)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall K\;W_g(K).
\end{equation}
For fixed total computable $g$, the right-hand predicate is $\Pi^0_1$ in the
machine and its initial state.
\begin{proof}[Third proof of \cref{lbh:ql:thm:deadlinecompact} (source 11)]
An infinite witness gives all finite witnesses by restriction. Conversely,
consider the tree of finite paths that have met every deadline already due
within that path. It is prefix-closed. Fix any length $n$ and take a successful
witness for $W_g(n+1)$. Its length is at least $n+1$, since $g(k)\geq k$.
Restrict it to length $n$. Any deadline $g(j)\leq n$ has $j\leq n$, so that
constraint was already enforced in the witness and remains satisfied after
restriction. Thus the restricted path is in the compatible tree. This works
for every $n$, including when $g$ has repeated values, so the tree has nodes
at every depth.

The tree is finitely branching, so K\"onig's lemma yields an infinite branch.
At time $g(k)$, its prefix meets the $k$th deadline, proving the forward
condition. Each $W_g(K)$ is decidable by finite exploration after computing
$g(1),\ldots,g(K)$. Searching for a failed $W_g(K)$ semidecides the
complement, giving the $\Pi^0_1$ upper bound.
\end{proof}
The qualification ``fixed total computable'' is not cosmetic. Given an
arbitrary program index for $g$, totality itself is a $\Pi^0_2$ requirement.
One cannot silently treat a partial proposed clock as a decidable deadline.
\begin{example}[The incoherent-prefix trap]\label[example]{lbh:ex:burst}
Consider a machine that first chooses a natural number $n$ in unary:
$n$ continue steps followed by one stop step. It then emits exactly $n$
accepting pulses, one per step, and enters a rejecting sink. The path that
continues forever emits no pulses. Every run has only finitely many
accepting visits.

Nevertheless, for each $k\ge1$, choosing $n=k$ gives $k$ visits by time
$2k+1$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:wrong}
 \forall k\ \exists\sigma\in\B^{2k+1}:\quad N_{e,\sigma}(2k+1)\ge k
\end{equation}
is true, while $\Buchi(e)$ is false. With $d(j)=2j+1$, meeting the first
deadline forces $n=1$: the first pulse is at $n+2$, and $n=0$ has no pulse.
That choice cannot meet the second deadline. In particular,
$\Fin(e,(3,5))$ is false.
\end{example}

This example locates the exact error in an attractive but invalid proof:
one cannot interchange the finite witnesses for different requirements
without checking that they belong to a nested family. B\"uchi acceptance
is generally an effective $G_\delta$ condition,
\[
 \cB_e=\bigcap_{k\ge1}\ \bigcup_{t\ge1}
       \{f:N_{e,f}(t)\ge k\},
\]
not a closed condition. A chosen clock replaces each open union by one
finite-prefix requirement.

\begin{proposition}[Closed subsets yield computable clocks]\label[proposition]{lbh:prop:closed-clock}
If $P$ is a nonempty effectively closed subset of $\cB_e$, then there is a
computable deadline $d$ with $P\subseteq\cK_{e,d}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $P=[T]$ for a computable binary tree. For a fixed $k$, search for a
level $n$ such that every node $\sigma\in T\cap\B^n$ has
$N_{e,\sigma}(n)\ge k$. Each level test is decidable. If the search never
succeeded, the nodes of $T$ with fewer than $k$ accepting visits would form
an infinite finitely branching subtree. K\"onig's lemma would provide a
path in $P$ with fewer than $k$ visits, contradicting $P\subseteq\cB_e$.
Thus the search terminates. Starting the search above $d(k-1)$ makes the
result strictly increasing, with $d(0)=0$. The finite program implementing
this search computes a deadline valid for every path in $P$.
\end{proof}

The algorithm in this proof uses the promise $\varnothing\ne P\subseteq\cB_e$.
It is not a decision procedure for that promise. This distinction matters
when a proposed compiler tries to synthesize a clock from an unverified
invariant.
\subsection{The same trap in sources 03 and 11}\label{lbh:sec:traps}
\wtag{} Sources~03 and~11 refute the same tempting inference with machines of their own:
source~03 with a nine-coordinate quadratic graph, source~11 with a one-counter ``budget''
machine whose compiled polynomial has dimension eight. The three examples (with
\cref{lbh:ex:burst}) share one mechanism: an unmarked initial guessing loop that can be
prolonged forever. The polynomials of both examples use the compilers of Part~III
(\cref{lbh:qd:eq:energy} and \cref{lbh:ql:eq:P2}); source~11's explicit residual form is in
\cref{lbh:ql:app:example}, and source~11's illustration of the deadline $g(k)=4k$ on its machine
follows \cref{lbh:ql:cor:deadlinepoly}.
\subsubsection{A nine-coordinate failure of naive compactness\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:counterexample}

The following completely explicit example is independent of universality
or hierarchy theory. It demonstrates the missing compatibility in a common
finite-witness argument.

Take three controls $G,A,H$, one counter $x$, and five edges:
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,node distance=38mm,on grid,auto]
\node[state] (G) {$G$};
\node[state,accepting,right=of G] (A) {$A$};
\node[state,right=of A] (H) {$H$};
\path[->]
(G) edge[loop above] node {$a_0:\ x{+}{+}$} (G)
(G) edge node {$a_1:\ \mathsf{nop}$} (A)
(A) edge[loop above] node {$a_2:\ x{>}0,\ x{-}{-}$} (A)
(A) edge node {$a_3:\ x=0$} (H)
(H) edge[loop above] node {$a_4:\ \mathsf{nop}$} (H);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Only $A$ is marked. Initially the control is $G$, the counter is zero,
and the old tag is zero. The state dimension is $D=3+1+5=9$, and
$e=(e_0,\ldots,e_4)$ denotes the next tag.
\begin{align}
 E={}&(e_0+e_1+e_2+e_3+e_4-1)^2\nonumber\\
 &+(p_G-e_0-e_1)^2+(p_A-e_2-e_3)^2+(p_H-e_4)^2\nonumber\\
 &+(p_G'-e_0)^2+(p_A'-e_1-e_2)^2+(p_H'-e_3-e_4)^2\label{lbh:qd:eq:small}\\
 &+(x'-x-e_0+e_2)^2+e_3x.\nonumber
\end{align}
There are eighteen source-and-target coordinate slots, five of which are
unused old tags. Its expanded form has forty-four nonzero monomials and
coefficient height four, as independently computed by the delivered compiler.

\begin{proposition}[Unbounded finite visits without recurrence]\label[proposition]{lbh:qd:prop:falsecompact}
For every $k\geq1$, this system has a finite path with at least $k$ marked
visits, and every finite path can be continued to an infinite path.
Nevertheless, no infinite path is recurrent.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose $n$ increment loops at $G$, then move to $A$. Arrival at $A$ gives one
visit. Exactly $n$ decrement loops are then possible before the counter
becomes zero; each gives another visit. The zero edge moves to $H$, which
loops forever. This path has exactly $n+1$ marked visits. Taking $n=k-1$
gives the required finite prefix and infinite extension.

Every infinite path either stays at $G$ forever, in which case it has no
marked visits, or leaves $G$ after a finite number $n$ of loops. In the second
case it has precisely the finite behavior just described and then remains
at $H$. No path has infinitely many marked visits. There are no dead ends,
so every finite path has an infinite continuation.
\end{proof}

Thus the implication
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:badexchange}
 \bigl[\forall k\ \exists\text{ finite path with }k\text{ marked visits}\bigr]
 \ \Longrightarrow\
 \bigl[\exists\text{ path with infinitely many marked visits}\bigr]
\end{equation}
is false even for a globally binary quadratic graph. The witnesses on the
left choose different lengths of the initial guessing segment. Their limit
may stay in that segment forever. The topology of a finite-branching path
space is compact, but a requirement of infinitely many visits is not a
closed finite-prefix condition. Deadlines repair precisely this defect.
\subsubsection{Why arbitrarily many finite visits do not imply recurrence\srctag{11}}
\label{lbh:ql:sec:falsecompactness}
One tempting assertion is
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:wrong}
 \forall k\;\exists\text{ finite path with at least }k\text{ marked visits}
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \Rec.
\end{equation}
It is false even for a one-counter binary-branching machine.

\begin{example}[Choose a budget, then spend it]\label[example]{lbh:ql:ex:budget}
Use labels $G,I,D,A,H$, one counter $x$, and mark only $A$. The edges are
\[
 \begin{array}{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}
 G\xrightarrow{\mathsf{nop}}I,&
 G\xrightarrow{\mathsf{nop}}D,&
 I\xrightarrow{\mathsf{inc}}G,\\[2pt]
 D\xrightarrow{\mathsf{dec}}A,&
 D\xrightarrow{\mathsf{zero}}H,&
 A\xrightarrow{\mathsf{nop}}D.
 \end{array}
\]
Start at $(G,0)$, and give $H$ no outgoing edges. Repeating $G,I,G$ exactly
$n$ times and then entering $D$ produces exactly $n$ marked visits before
halting. The only infinite behavior is to keep increasing the budget in the
$G,I$ loop forever. That behavior never reaches $A$.
\end{example}
Consequently the left side of \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:wrong} holds and its right side fails.
This is not a violation of K\"onig's lemma. The full tree of finite runs has
an infinite branch, namely the unmarked choosing branch. Having many marks
somewhere in the tree is not a closed, prefix-compatible constraint forcing
marks onto that branch.

The compiled example has state dimension eight and 88 expanded monomials in
each of $P_2$ and $P_4$. Both have height four. The package exports both
polynomials exactly, not just their numerical evaluations.
\section{The sharp progress-modulus hierarchy}\label{lbh:sec:hierarchy}

\subsection{Statement and conventions}
Completeness below means computable many-one completeness for sets of natural
input codes. For a fixed system, the unknown is $e$; neither the transition
function nor the accepting region is changed with $e$. We use the standard
arithmetical normal forms $\exists m\forall k R(e,m,k)$ and
$\exists m\forall k\exists s R(e,m,k,s)$, with total decidable $R$.

\begin{theorem}[Progress hierarchy]\label{lbh:thm:hierarchy}
Every computable binary-controlled system has the upper bounds in the middle
column of the following table. There is a single fixed universal system for
which every bound is complete, and which can be implemented as a finite
binary-controlled two-stack machine.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.52\linewidth}cc@{}}
\toprule
Finite-input property & Complexity & Defining pattern\\
\midrule
Some finite accepting visit, $\Reach$ & $\sig1$ & $\exists n$\\
Some run meets a fixed computable $d$, $\Fixed_d$ & $\ppi1$ & $\forall k$\\
Some run has bounded gaps, $\Gap$ & $\sig2$ & $\exists b\forall n$\\
Some run has a computable deadline, $\Clock$ & $\sig3$ & $\exists m\forall k\exists s$\\
Some run is recurrent, $\Buchi$ & $\an$ & $\exists f\forall k\exists t$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the fixed-deadline row, completeness holds for every fixed computable
strictly increasing $d$, including $d(k)=k$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Finite reachability and a fixed clock}
For $\Reach$, enumerate finite schedules and simulate them. This semidecides
an accepting visit. To reduce halting, simulate an ordinary program without
accepting and enter an accepting state exactly when it halts. The finite
input encodes the simulated program, so one interpreter handles the entire
reduction.

For $\Fixed_d$, \cref{lbh:thm:compact} gives the $\ppi1$ definition
$\forall k\,\Fin(e,d\pref k)$. For hardness, simulate a program while
marking every nonhalting interpreter microstep accepting. If it halts,
enter a rejecting sink. Nonhalting produces one accepting visit at every
time and therefore satisfies every $d(k)\ge k$. Halting produces only
finitely many accepting visits and satisfies no deadline. This reduces
the complement of the halting problem to $\Fixed_d$.

\subsection{An unknown bounded-gap guarantee}
Fix $b\ge1$. A finite schedule of length $n$ is $b$-admissible when every
window $(t,t+b]$ contained in that finite prefix contains an accepting
visit. Checking that a $b$-admissible schedule of length $n$ exists is
decidable. These conditions define a prefix-closed binary tree; K\"onig's
lemma gives
\[
 \exists f\ \forall t\ge0:\ N_f(t+b)-N_f(t)\ge1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall n\ \exists\text{ a $b$-admissible prefix of length }n.
\]
The finite existential search is part of the decidable matrix. Quantifying
$b$ gives a $\sig2$ upper bound.

For hardness, let $S(e)\leftrightarrow\exists m\forall k R(e,m,k)$, with
$R$ decidable. The system first guesses $m$ in unary without accepting.
After the guess ends, it deterministically checks
$R(e,m,0),R(e,m,1),\ldots$. All computation steps during these checks are
accepting. On the first false test it enters the rejecting sink.

If some $m$ works, the finite guessing prefix is followed by an accepting
visit at every step, so some gap bound holds. If no $m$ works, every completed
guess eventually reaches the sink. A schedule that never finishes guessing
has no accepting visits. Thus $\Gap$ holds exactly when $S(e)$ holds.
The acceptance flag during verification is essential: the computation times
of the individual decidable tests need not have a uniform bound.

\subsection{An unknown total computable deadline}
Fix an effective enumeration $(\varphi_m)$ of partial computable functions.
There is a decidable predicate $V(e,m,k,s)$ saying:
\begin{quote}
By computation stage $s$, all values $\varphi_m(1),\ldots,\varphi_m(k)$
have halted, are positive and strictly increasing, and their resulting
finite deadline vector is feasible.
\end{quote}
One may either include the finite feasibility computation in the stage bound
or run its total decision procedure after the bounded simulations; both
define decidable predicates with the same existential closure in $s$.
The condition for $k=0$ can be declared true. Then
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:CDsigma3}
 \Clock(e)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists m\ \forall k\ \exists s\ V(e,m,k,s).
\end{equation}
The same index $m$ must serve every $k$. If the right side holds, it defines
a single total computable deadline, and coherent compactification supplies
a run meeting it. This proves the $\sig3$ upper bound.

For hardness, start with
$S(e)\leftrightarrow\exists m\forall k\exists s R(e,m,k,s)$.
Guess $m$ in unary. For $k=0,1,2,\ldots$, search $s=0,1,2,\ldots$ until
$R(e,m,k,s)$ is true; then emit one accepting pulse and advance to $k+1$.
No search step is accepting. If $m$ satisfies the formula, the run obtained
by using the least successful $s$ at each stage is computable and recurrent.
Its $k$th accepting time is therefore a total computable function; strictify
it if necessary to obtain a deadline. If no $m$ satisfies the formula, every
completed guess eventually stalls on a stage with no witness. Infinite unary
guessing produces no pulses. There is then no recurrent run at all, hence
no computable-deadline run. This proves $\sig3$ hardness.

\subsection{Unrestricted recurrence and recursive trees}
The definition $\exists f\forall k\exists t\,N_f(t)\ge k$ is analytic,
so $\Buchi$ belongs to $\an$. For completeness, we spell out the standard
recursive-tree reduction behind this endpoint; the recurrent-machine
hardness itself is classical \cite{hps,ek2025}.

\begin{lemma}[Analytic tree normal form]\label[lemma]{lbh:lem:analytic-tree}\label[lemma]{lbh:qd:lem:tree}
For every $\an$ set $S\subseteq\N$, there is a uniformly primitive-recursive
family of prefix-closed trees $T_e\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ such that
\[
 e\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists g\in\N^\N\ \forall n:\ g\pref n\in T_e.
\]
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Normal-form argument]
Write an analytic definition as an existential function quantifier followed
by an arithmetic formula. Put the finite number-quantifier part in prenex
form and absorb all existential number choices into additional Skolem
functions, dependent on the preceding universal number variables. Encode
these functions and the original function into one $g$. This gives
$\exists g\forall n\,R(e,g,n)$, where $R$ is a total oracle-computable
Boolean test.

For a finite sequence $\sigma$ of length $l$, run the first $l$ tests for
$l$ steps, answering oracle questions within $\sigma$ and suspending a
test that asks outside it. Reject $\sigma$ exactly when a fully evaluated
test is false. This is a primitive-recursive bounded computation and is
prefix closed. A $g$ satisfying every test has all its prefixes in the
tree. Conversely, along an infinite tree path every particular test obtains
all its finitely many oracle answers and finishes at a sufficiently long
stage, so it cannot be false. This proves the equivalence. Standard coding
of the finitely many function components is effective.
\end{proof}

Given $e$, the recurrent machine maintains a finite sequence $\sigma$.
It guesses the next natural $n$ by unary binary choices, appends $n$, checks
$\sigma^\frown n\in T_e$, and emits one pulse exactly when the check
succeeds. A failed check leads to the rejecting sink. The tree check is
always a terminating finite computation. An infinite branch gives an
accepting schedule. Conversely, an accepting schedule must complete
infinitely many guesses and successful checks, so its guessed values form
an infinite branch. Remaining forever inside one unfinished unary guess
cannot create recurrence. This is the reduction.

\subsection{Why one fixed two-stack machine suffices}\label{lbh:sec:universal}
The reductions above describe a finite menu of interpreters with input
parameters: a halting simulator, an always-accepting safety simulator,
a unary-witness verifier, a pulse-on-success searcher, and a recursive-tree
checker. A single machine can include these modes in its finite control and
read a mode-tagged program from its initial finite storage.

A two-stack machine simulates an ordinary tape by storing the finite tape
on the two sides of the head, keeping the scanned symbol in finite control.
A fixed binary block code represents the finitely many tape symbols using
stack symbols $1$ and $3$. Reading or modifying a symbol requires finitely
many top tests, pops, and pushes; the fixed universal interpreter has only
finitely many such instruction types. External binary choices are read only
at the intended guessing steps and ignored elsewhere. Rejecting halts are
completed by a sink. This gives one finite rule table with effective finite
input encoding. No minimum rule count or explicit small universal table is
claimed.

The accepting flag is part of the finite control. In the $\ppi1$ and $\sig2$
safety modes it is set during every verification microstep. In the search
and tree modes it is set for exactly one microstep per successful stage.
These choices eliminate hidden assumptions about interpreter overhead.
The natural-number arguments of all normal forms are parameters to the
fixed interpreter, not infinitely many new control states.

The strictness of the arithmetical hierarchy and the existence of
nonarithmetical analytic sets imply that the inclusions in
\cref{lbh:eq:inclusions} are strict for such a universal system. We next give
concrete separations that explain their computational meaning.
\subsection{One hierarchy table for the three sources}\label{lbh:sec:onetable}
\wtag{} The table lists every temporal predicate classified in the three sources, with the
theorem that proves each classification. Completeness is for a fixed universal system unless
marked ``across machines'', which means over effectively supplied finite machines and initial
states (source~11's $\sig3$ rows; source~11 notes that a fixed universal interpreter can absorb
the program into the initial encoding where appropriate).
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.3\textwidth}cp{0.55\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Property & Class & Proved in\\
\midrule
\endhead
some finite accepting visit & $\sig1$ & 10: $\Reach$, \cref{lbh:thm:hierarchy}; 03: $\HIT$ for $E_U$, \cref{lbh:qd:thm:hitinf}; 11: $\EF F$, \cref{lbh:ql:thm:temporal}\\
some infinite run & $\ppi1$ & 03: $\INF$ for $E_U$, \cref{lbh:qd:thm:hitinf}; 11: $\EG F$, \cref{lbh:ql:thm:temporal}\\
some run marked at every step & $\ppi1$ & 10: $\Fixed_{\mathrm{id}}$, \cref{lbh:thm:hierarchy} (times $t\ge1$); 11: $\EG F$, \cref{lbh:ql:thm:temporal}\\
some run meets a fixed computable deadline & $\ppi1$ & 10: $\Fixed_d$, complete for \emph{every} fixed $d$, \cref{lbh:thm:hierarchy}; 03: $\BH_h$, sharp for suitable families, \cref{lbh:qd:prop:deadline-sharp}; 11: upper bound, \cref{lbh:thm:compact}\\
