\wtag{} Source~03's summary ``$\ppi1$ upper bound; sharp for suitable uniformly specified

are compatible: source~10's reduction marks every live interpreter microstep accepting, so every
deadline $d(k)\ge k$ is met exactly on nonhalting inputs, while source~03's second construction
(\cref{lbh:qd:prop:deadline-sharp}) marks only emits and therefore needs an input-dependent
deadline. Source~11's temporal rows $\EFG$, $\AGF$ and $\AFG$ are new relative to the other two

systems. Report~B of this category (\path{probabilistic-quantum-and-continuous-computation}) has
the probabilistic counterpart of the deterministic $\ppi2$ row: almost-sure termination is
$\ppi2$ (its \texttt{pqc:eq:te} and theorem \texttt{pqc:thm:unconditionaltable}), by the same
totality-type argument. Report~A (\path{canonical-diophantine-certificates}) supplies the lowest

($\sig1$; its batch-62 priority-pumping Part).
\subsection{Second routes through source 03's quadratic graph}\label{lbh:sec:routes03}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~03 proves the rows $\HIT$, $\INF$, $\REC$, $\CB$ and $\CP$ for one fixed
degree-two counter graph $E_U$ (\cref{lbh:qd:cor:fixed}, Part~III). Its tree normal form is
\cref{lbh:lem:analytic-tree}, whose second proof is source~03's. Its statements and proofs
follow; they reuse only the event-reflecting interpreter of \cref{lbh:qd:lem:universal}.}
\begin{theorem}[Finite hit and plain infinite continuation]\label{lbh:qd:thm:hitinf}
For the fixed universal system, $\HIT$ is $\Sigma^0_1$-complete and has an
ordinary Diophantine representation. The predicate $\INF$ that some infinite
path exists is $\Pi^0_1$-complete.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Finite hit is computably enumerable: enumerate the finite successor tree
level by level until a marked vertex occurs. To reduce halting, interpret a
program that simulates the given machine and emits once precisely when it
halts. MRDP supplies the ordinary Diophantine representation.

For infinite continuation, existence of a path of any particular length
$T$ is decidable by complete finite branching. By K\"onig's lemma, an
infinite path exists if and only if paths of every length exist. This is
$\Pi^0_1$. For hardness, interpret a deterministic simulation that halts
exactly when the given machine halts. Positive finite simulation of each
source step ensures an infinite counter run exactly in the nonhalting case.
\end{proof}
\subsection{Unrestricted recurrence is analytical-complete\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:analytic}

A tree $T\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ is prefix closed. It is recursive when membership
of a finite sequence is decidable. An infinite branch is a function
$f\in\N^\N$ with $f\upharpoonright k\in T$ for every $k$. Notice that
such a tree is not required to be finitely branching.

\wtag{} Source~03's recursive-tree normal form (its Lemma ``Recursive-tree normal form'') is
\cref{lbh:lem:analytic-tree}, stated for primitive recursive trees $T_n\subseteq\N^{<\N}$, with
the added remark that infinite-branch existence for such trees is consequently
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\begin{proof}[Second proof of \cref{lbh:lem:analytic-tree} (source 03)]
The upper bound existentially quantifies over a branch and arithmetically
checks every finite prefix. For completeness, start from
$\exists f\,\theta(n,f)$, where $\theta$ is arithmetic. Skolemize the
existential number quantifiers inside $\theta$ by functions of the preceding
universal number variables, and combine the finitely many function variables
into one. This gives an equivalent form
\[
 \exists u\in\N^\N\ \forall z\ R(n,u,z),
\]
where $R$ can be checked by an oracle computation that uses finitely many
values of $u$ on each terminating computation. One can choose the matrix
checker total; alternatively, accepting computation transcripts provide
the needed finite evidence directly.

Let a branch code alternate entries $u(i)$ and finite accepting computation
transcripts $C_z$ for the checker of $R(n,u,z)$. For a finite prefix, verify
that each transcript already present is a syntactically valid accepting
computation, treating the recorded oracle answers as part of the transcript.
Whenever a queried value $u(i)$ is also present in the prefix, require that
the recorded answer equals it. Any coding inconsistencies cause rejection.
Finite computation checking and decoding finite natural-number codes are
primitive recursive. Only finitely many present entries and present
transcripts are checked, so membership in the resulting tree is primitive
recursive, uniformly in $n$.

The conditions are prefix closed: extending a prefix adds checks but never
removes an existing one. A witness $u$ together with its actual accepting
transcripts gives an infinite branch. Conversely, on an infinite branch,
every transcript is eventually present and every one of its finitely many
oracle answers is eventually compared with the corresponding $u(i)$. Thus
all transcripts are genuine accepting computations relative to the single
function $u$, and $R(n,u,z)$ holds for every $z$. This proves the normal form.
It is the standard tree mechanism underlying analytical recurrence
reductions; compare~\cite{harel1986}.
\end{proof}

\Needspace{9\baselineskip}
\begin{theorem}[Fixed quadratic recurrent universality]\label{lbh:qd:thm:analytic}
For the single polynomial $E_U$ and marker $p_{q_A}=1$,
\[
 \REC(n)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists s\in(\N^{D_U})^\N\left[
 s_0=c(n)\ \land\ \forall t\ E_U(s_t,s_{t+1})=0
 \ \land\ \forall k\exists t\geq k\ A(s_t)\right]
\]
is $\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The displayed formula gives membership in $\Sigma^1_1$, since a sequence of
finite-dimensional natural states is a single function after pairing.
For hardness, use the family $T_n$ from \Cref{lbh:qd:lem:tree}. Interpret the
following program, where each choice is binary:
\begin{lstlisting}
sigma := empty sequence
repeat:
    a := 0
    while choose(CONTINUE, STOP) == CONTINUE:
        a := a + 1
    sigma := sigma concatenated with [a]
    if sigma is not in T_n: halt
    EMIT
\end{lstlisting}
Membership tests terminate because the tree is primitive recursive. If
$f$ is an infinite branch of $T_n$, choose $f(k)$ continuations at stage
$k$ before stopping. Every test succeeds, so the resulting run emits
infinitely often.

Conversely, infinitely many emits require infinitely many completed stages.
A run that chooses CONTINUE forever in a single stage has no further emits;
it cannot be recurrent. Read the successive completed guesses from a
recurrent run. They form $f\in\N^\N$, and every finite prefix passes its
tree test. Hence $f$ is an infinite branch. The interpreter preserves and
reflects emits, and every completed source operation takes positive finite
time. Therefore exactly the same equivalence holds after compilation into
the fixed quadratic graph. The program code depends computably on $n$.
\end{proof}

The apparent paradox with finite branching is resolved by the infinite unary
guess. A countably branching tree is represented by a binary choice process
that may postpone completion forever. Recurrence discards those postponed
branches. K\"onig's lemma guarantees an infinite binary run but does not
guarantee that the run completes infinitely many guesses.
\subsection{Existential computable bounds and computable paths\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:sigma3}

The next theorem concerns one fixed system $E_U$, not an unbounded collection
of different-dimensional polynomials. Input codes select source programs
inside the fixed interpreter.

\begin{theorem}[Exact complexity of computable deadline existence]
\label{lbh:qd:thm:cb}
For the fixed universal quadratic system, $\CB$ is $\Sigma^0_3$-complete.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Enumerate partial computable functions $\varphi_e$ on positive integers.
For a candidate index $e$ and $k$, require that $\varphi_e(1),\ldots,
\varphi_e(k)$ all halt, produce an increasing sequence $h(1),\ldots,h(k)$
with $h(j)\geq j$, and that a finite path of length $h(k)$ meets all these
$k$ deadlines. The last condition is decidable after the finite values of
$h$ have been computed, by the complete successor-list algorithm.

Let $R(n,e,k,s)$ say that all these calculations and the finite-path search
finish successfully within a total computation budget $s$. This is a
computable predicate; it can also be implemented by bounded simulation.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:cbnormal}
 \CB(n)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists e\ \forall k\geq1\ \exists s\;R(n,e,k,s).
\end{equation}
For the right-to-left direction, the universal condition forces
$\varphi_e$ to be total and yields every finite deadline condition.
\Cref{lbh:qd:thm:deadline} gives a single infinite path meeting all of them.
The reverse direction follows by taking an index of the computable bound.
This proves the $\Sigma^0_3$ upper bound.

For hardness, let $S$ be any $\Sigma^0_3$ set and fix a decidable relation
$R_0$ with
\[
 n\in S\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists a\ \forall b\ \exists c\;R_0(n,a,b,c).
\]
Interpret the following program:
\begin{lstlisting}
a := a finite natural chosen by a unary CONTINUE/STOP loop
for b = 0, 1, 2, ...:
    search c = 0, 1, 2, ... until R_0(n,a,b,c) holds
    EMIT
\end{lstlisting}
An infinite unfinished initial guess produces no emits. After a completed
guess $a$, the rest of the run is deterministic. If $a$ witnesses $n\in S$,
every search terminates. The resulting recurrent path is computable: the
finite choice of $a$ can be built into its program, and each later step is
computed by simulation. In particular, its $k$th emit time is a total
computable function of $k$. Thus the corresponding input belongs to $\CB$.
If no $a$ is a witness, each completed choice eventually reaches a search
that never succeeds, and hence has only finitely many emits. There is no
recurrent path at all. The input code is computable from $n$, proving
$\Sigma^0_3$-hardness.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact complexity of computable recurrent paths]
\label{lbh:qd:thm:cp}
For the same fixed system, $\CP$ is $\Sigma^0_3$-complete, and
\[
 \CP\subseteq\CB\subseteq\REC.
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For the upper bound, existentially choose an index $e$ of a proposed state
sequence. For every $k$, require a finite computation budget $s$ within which
$e$ produces a prefix $s_0,\ldots,s_T$ with $T\geq k$, the prescribed initial
state, valid adjacent transitions, and at least $k$ marked visits. This
condition is decidable by bounded simulation and finite checking. The
formula has shape $\exists e\forall k\exists s$. Because every prefix uses
the same deterministic program $e$, the verified outputs are compatible.
Since $T\geq k$ for unbounded $k$, that program is defined on every time
index. It therefore describes one computable infinite recurrent path.
Conversely, every such path supplies the required finite computations.

The reduction used in \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:cb} already has a computable recurrent
path whenever it has any recurrent path, proving the same hardness for
$\CP$. Finally, from a computable recurrent path and a decidable marker,
search for successive marked times. Each search terminates, so
$k\mapsto\tau_s(k)$ is total computable and is a valid deadline function.
This proves $\CP\subseteq\CB$; the other inclusion is immediate.
\end{proof}

Equality of complexity classes does not imply equality of sets. In this
case $\CP$ and $\CB$ are both complete for the third arithmetic level, yet
there are explicit inputs belonging to one and not the other.
\subsection{Third routes through source 11's quadratic law}\label{lbh:sec:routes11}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11 classifies the temporal properties of its incoming-edge law
(Part~III) and proves the two $\sig3$ rows over effectively supplied machines. Its fixed-law
$\an$ theorem is \cref{lbh:ql:thm:fixed} in Part~III. Its sections follow.}
\subsection{The finite-branching temporal hierarchy\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:hierarchy}
The local equations remain unchanged while temporal specifications cross
several levels of definability. The following table gives sharp completeness
bounds over effectively supplied finite binary counter machines and initial
states. A fixed universal interpreter can also absorb the program into the
initial encoding where appropriate.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lXl@{}}
\toprule
Property & Meaning & Completeness\\
\midrule
$\EF F$ & Some finite path reaches a marked state & $\Sigma^0_1$\\
$\EG F$ & Some infinite run stays marked throughout & $\Pi^0_1$\\
$\EFG F$ & Some infinite run is eventually always marked & $\Sigma^0_2$\\
$\AGF F$ & Every infinite run is marked infinitely often & $\Pi^0_2$\\
$\EGF F$ & Some infinite run is marked infinitely often & $\Sigma^1_1$\\
$\AFG F$ & Every infinite run is eventually always marked & $\Pi^1_1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
Universal claims about infinite runs are vacuously true when there are no
infinite runs. This convention is part of the table.

\begin{theorem}[Temporal classifications]\label{lbh:ql:thm:temporal}
The completeness claims in the table hold under total computable many-one
reductions. They continue to hold after the witness-free polynomial
compilation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Finite reachability is computably enumerable by exploring finite paths;
halting gives hardness.

For $\EG F$, keep only marked states in the run tree. There is an infinite
run exactly when this effectively finitely branching tree has a node at every
depth. Each finite-level test is decidable, so the property is $\Pi^0_1$.
For hardness, simulate a deterministic computation with every nonhalting state
marked and deadlock on halting.

For $\EFG F$, existentially choose a finite prefix and then require an infinite
all-marked continuation. This is a $\Sigma^0_2$ condition. For hardness, put
a $\Sigma^0_2$ predicate in the form
$\exists n\,\forall k\,R(x,n,k)$ with recursive $R$. Guess $n$ in an
unmarked unary loop. After committing to $n$, enter marked states and check
$R(x,n,0),R(x,n,1),\ldots$ sequentially, halting upon failure. Every individual
check terminates. The resulting run is eventually always marked exactly when
some chosen $n$ passes all checks. An infinite uncommitted guessing run is
unmarked, so it cannot produce a false positive.

The identity $\AGF F=\neg\EFG(Q\setminus F)$ gives the $\Pi^0_2$ line,
including hardness. The $\Sigma^1_1$ recurrence line follows from
\cref{lbh:ql:thm:fixed}. Finally
$\AFG F=\neg\EGF(Q\setminus F)$ gives the $\Pi^1_1$ line. Compilation
preserves all labelled paths and marker visits, so it preserves these
properties.
\end{proof}
For a deterministic machine made total by an unmarked sink, recurrence is
$\Pi^0_2$ rather than $\Sigma^1_1$. Hardness follows by successively searching
for witnesses in a predicate $\forall k\,\exists s\,R(x,k,s)$ and marking
completion of each successful search. If one search never succeeds, only
finitely many marks occur. Every recurrent deterministic computable run also
has a computable deadline, obtained by simulating until its $k$th visit.
Nondeterminism is what permits the witness distinctions below.
\subsection{The exact cost of supplying a computable deadline\srctag{11}}
\label{lbh:ql:sec:sigma3}

\begin{theorem}[Two $\Sigma^0_3$-complete witness notions]
\label{lbh:ql:thm:sigma3}
The index sets $\DRec$ and $\CRec$, for effectively supplied finite
binary-branching counter machines and encoded initial states, are both
$\Sigma^0_3$-complete.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\emph{Upper bound for $\DRec$.} Existentially choose a program index $e$ for
a proposed deadline. A finite verification at stage $K$ can wait for the
program to return values on $1,\ldots,K$, check normalization, and exhaustively
test $W_g(K)$. The assertion that for every $K$ this finite verification
successfully finishes has the form
\[
 \exists e\,\forall K\,\exists s\;
 \mathsf{Verified}(\M,a,e,K,s),
\]
where the matrix is a decidable bounded-computation predicate. These conditions
also enforce totality of the proposed deadline. By
\cref{lbh:ql:thm:deadlinecompact}, they are equivalent to $\DRec$.

\emph{Upper bound for $\CRec$.} Existentially choose an index $e$ for the run.
For each $K$, require a finite computation witnessing a legal initial run
segment of length at least $K$ with at least $K$ marked positions, all of whose
states are returned by program $e$. As $K$ varies, this forces the program
to define a total legal run and forces infinitely many marks. It is again
an $\exists e\,\forall K\,\exists s$ condition.

\emph{Hardness for both.} Let $S$ be any $\Sigma^0_3$ set, and choose a
recursive matrix $R$ such that
\[
 x\in S\iff\exists n\,\forall k\,\exists s\;R(x,n,k,s).
\]
Construct a program which guesses $n$ by an unmarked unary loop, commits to
it, and then for $k=0,1,\ldots$ searches deterministically for an $s$ satisfying
$R(x,n,k,s)$. Each successful search emits exactly one marked checkpoint.
If $x\in S$, a witnessing finite $n$ gives a computable recurrent run.
Its visit-time function is total computable, so it also witnesses $\DRec$.
If $x\notin S$, every committed $n$ eventually reaches an unsuccessful search
that continues without further marks, while an uncommitted run has no marks.
There is no recurrent run at all. The construction is effective and converts
to the counter normal form, proving hardness of both properties.
\end{proof}
The quantifier $\exists n$ in the hardness proof is not uniformly computable
from a positive input $x$. That does not matter: a witnessing natural number
can be hard-coded into the program for that one run. Confusing a computable
witness with a uniform algorithm selecting witnesses would change the problem.

\begin{proposition}[Computable runs supply computable deadlines]
\label[proposition]{lbh:ql:prop:implication}
$\CRec\subseteq\DRec\subseteq\Rec$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Given a total computable recurrent run, compute successive states until the
$k$th mark appears. Recurrence ensures termination for each $k$, giving a
total computable visit-time function. Normalize it if necessary. The second
inclusion is immediate from the deadline condition.
\end{proof}
Completeness at the same arithmetical level does not imply equality of these
properties on individual machines. The next two constructions prove both
inclusions strict without appealing merely to different complexity classes.
\part{Scheduler degrees, explicit separations and the deterministic case}\label{lbh:part:degrees}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Part~II prints source~10's degree-theoretic characterization of computable
clocks, its separations and c.e.\ spectra, then source~03's and source~11's separations as second
and third routes, and the deterministic boundary with source~03's second proof.}
\section{Scheduler degrees: an exact characterization}\label{lbh:sec:degrees}

\subsection{Hyperimmune-free degrees and a basis argument}
\begin{definition}
A schedule $f$ has \emph{hyperimmune-free degree} when every total function
$g:\N\to\N$ with $g\le_T f$ is bounded by some total computable function.
Using eventual domination instead of pointwise domination gives the same
notion: finitely many exceptional values can be incorporated as constants
in a computable bound. A schedule is \emph{low} when $f'\le_T\varnothing'$.
\end{definition}

The hyperimmune-free basis theorem says that every nonempty effectively
closed subset of Cantor space contains a member of hyperimmune-free degree
\cite{js1972,ng2013}. We record its compactness mechanism because it is
precisely what is needed for clocks.

\begin{lemma}[Classical HIF basis theorem]\label[lemma]{lbh:lem:hifbasis}
Every nonempty effectively closed $P\subseteq\Cantor$ contains an HIF member.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Construct a nested sequence of nonempty effectively closed classes
$P=P_0\supseteq P_1\supseteq\cdots$, handling the $e$th oracle functional
$\Phi_e$ at stage $e$. If for some $n$ the class
\[
 P_e\cap\{f:\Phi_e^f(n)\uparrow\}
\]
is nonempty, choose such an $n$ and use this class as $P_{e+1}$. It is
effectively closed: nonconvergence is a closed finite-computation condition.
If no such $n$ exists, then $\Phi_e^f(n)$ converges for every $f\in P_e$ and
every $n$.

In the latter case fix a computable tree for $P_e$. For each input $n$, search
for a level $l$ at which every tree node of length $l$ gives a convergent
computation of $\Phi_e(n)$ within $l$ steps, using only the supplied $l$
oracle bits. Such a level exists; otherwise the nonconvergent finite
computations form an infinite subtree and yield a path on which the
functional diverges. The maximum of the finitely many outputs at the
successful level is a computable bound $g_e(n)$. Set $P_{e+1}=P_e$.

Compactness gives $f\in\bigcap_e P_e$. If $\Phi_e^f$ is total, the divergent
restriction could not have been chosen at stage $e$, so $g_e$ bounds it.
Therefore $f$ has HIF degree. The stage choices need not be effective; each
chosen finite tree index is a fixed constant in the corresponding program
for $g_e$. This is an existence proof, not an algorithm selecting an HIF
path from a tree index.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The main equivalence}
\begin{theorem}[Clock--closed-class--degree equivalence]\label{lbh:thm:core}\label{lbh:ql:prop:oraclepath}
For every input $e$ to a computable binary-controlled system, the following
are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)}]
\item $\Clock(e)$: some accepting schedule meets a computable deadline;
\item $\cB_e$ contains a nonempty effectively closed subset of Cantor space;
\item $\cB_e$ contains a schedule of hyperimmune-free Turing degree.
\end{enumerate}
Moreover, whenever these conditions hold, $\cB_e$ contains a low schedule.
The low schedule and the HIF schedule are not asserted to be the same.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For (i)$\Rightarrow$(ii), use the nonempty effectively closed set
$\cK_{e,d}$ from \cref{lbh:thm:compact}. The converse is
\cref{lbh:prop:closed-clock}. For (ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii), apply the HIF basis
theorem to the closed subclass.

For (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i), let $f\in\cB_e$ be HIF. Given oracle $f$, simulate
the run until the $k$th accepting visit. Because $f$ is accepting, this
algorithm computes the total function $k\mapsto\tau_{e,f}(k)$.
There is a computable bound $h$ for it. The function
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:strictify}
 d(k)=k+\max_{1\le j\le k}h(j)
\end{equation}
is computable and strictly increasing, and bounds $\tau_{e,f}(k)$.
Finally, the classical low basis theorem of Jockusch and Soare applied
to $\cK_{e,d}$ supplies a low accepting schedule \cite{js1972}.
\end{proof}
\wtag{} Source~11 proves the weaker $\Delta^0_2$ form of the last assertion by a direct path
selection (its proposition in a subsection ``What a computable deadline does give''):
\emph{every recurrent instance with a computable
deadline has a recurrent run computable in the halting oracle $0'$.} It follows from
\cref{lbh:thm:core}, since a low schedule is $\Delta^0_2$ and computes its run.
\begin{proof}[Second proof of the $\Delta^0_2$ form (source 11)]
The deadline-compatible tree in \cref{lbh:ql:thm:deadlinecompact} is decidable and
effectively finitely branching. Whether a node has arbitrarily long extensions
is $\Pi^0_1$, hence decidable by $0'$. Starting at the root, use that oracle to
choose a successor with arbitrarily long extensions, taking the first one in
the computable successor ordering. This constructs an infinite compatible
path. The deadline ensures recurrence.
\end{proof}

This theorem distinguishes two kinds of information. A computable clock
bounds how late progress may occur; it need not tell us which compatible
branch to choose. Conversely, an HIF schedule may contain noncomputable
information, but cannot use that information to produce a progress-time
function escaping every computable upper bound.

\begin{corollary}[Oracle-relative form]\label[corollary]{lbh:cor:relative}
Let $Z$ be any oracle, and allow the transition function, initial coding,
and acceptance predicate to be computable relative to $Z$.
There is a $Z$-computable deadline met by some accepting schedule if and
only if there is an accepting $f$ such that every total
$g\le_T f\oplus Z$ is dominated by a $Z$-computable function.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Relativize the effective closed-tree construction, the basis argument, and
the computation of $\tau_{e,f}$ in the preceding proof.
\end{proof}

For an ordinary computable system, the set of oracle degrees that can
compute some adequate deadline is upward closed. The relative theorem
characterizes it without confusing a schedule with a program that prints
that schedule. Determining finer properties of this deadline-degree
spectrum is one of the research questions in \cref{lbh:sec:questions}.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Batch~79: Part~VI (source~12) determines several of these properties; see the note
after \cref{lbh:q:spectra}.}
\section{Explicit separations and exact deadline-degree spectra}\label{lbh:sec:examples}

\subsection{A computable clock need not compute a scheduler}
Fix an effective enumeration $(\varphi_e)$ of partial computable
natural-number functions. Define a binary tree $T_{\mathrm{DNR}}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:dnr-tree}
 \sigma\in T_{\mathrm{DNR}},\quad |\sigma|=n
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall e<n:\quad
 \bigl[\varphi_e(e)\text{ halts within }n\text{ steps with }b\in\B\bigr]
 \Rightarrow \sigma(e)\ne b.
\end{equation}
Membership is decidable by bounded simulation, and the tree is prefix closed.
An infinite path exists: at each coordinate choose the opposite bit if the
diagonal computation eventually outputs a bit, and choose arbitrarily if it
does not. But no computable path exists. A total computable binary path
would equal $\varphi_j$ for some $j$, so at coordinate $j$ it would eventually
violate its own diagonal requirement.

A verifier requests one new bit at each stage, checks the resulting prefix
against \cref{lbh:eq:dnr-tree}, and emits a pulse after success. Every finite
verification terminates. For each fixed stage $k$, its worst-case computation
time over the finitely many possible bit prefixes is computably bounded:
enumerate those prefixes and run the terminating bounded checks. Adding
these bounds gives a computable deadline shared by all genuine infinite
paths. A computable accepting schedule would compute a path by recording
the bits requested at successive stages, which is impossible.

\begin{proposition}[The clock/controller distinction is strict]\label[proposition]{lbh:prop:noncomputable-scheduler}
There is a computable binary-controlled system with $\Clock(e)$ true and
with no computable accepting schedule. In fact, there is such a system with
$\Fixed_{\mathrm{id}}(e)$ true.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The pulse verifier proves the first assertion. For the stronger assertion,
mark every working microstep of the same verifier accepting, instead of
marking only successful stage completions. A failed check enters the
rejecting sink. Because every finite check terminates and exactly one bit
is requested per stage, an infinite nonsink computation determines an
infinite path of the tree. Therefore the schedules meeting $d(k)=k$ are
exactly the schedules that never fail a check. Such schedules exist, but a
computable one would compute an impossible computable DNR path.
\end{proof}

This example also explains why the compactness and basis arguments cannot
be silently turned into effective selection algorithms. It does not conflict
with the existence of low and HIF paths: those paths need not be computable,
and the two basis applications select potentially different paths.

\subsection{A recurrent computation that requires a halting oracle}
List ordinary programs $M_0,M_1,\ldots$ and let
\[
 B(i)=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup
      \{\text{halting time of }M_j\text{ on blank input}:j\le i\}
      \bigr).
\]
For a sequence $\sigma$ of length $n$, require that whenever a program
$M_j$ with $j\le i<n$ has halted within $n$ steps, its halting time is at most
$\sigma(i)$. This is a primitive-recursive, prefix-closed tree, and its
infinite paths are exactly the functions $g$ satisfying $g(i)\ge B(i)$ for
all $i$.

Run the unary-guess tree verifier of \cref{lbh:sec:hierarchy}. Choosing $g=B$
gives an accepting run. Every accepting schedule computes a path $g$, and
such a path decides halting: to test $M_i$, simulate it for $g(i)$ steps.
Also, guessing $g(i)$ in unary costs at least $g(i)$ microsteps before the
$(i+1)$st accepting pulse. Hence any successful deadline satisfies
\[
 B(i)\le g(i)\le\tau_f(i+1)\le d(i+1).
\]
A computable deadline would decide the halting problem. No such deadline
exists, although an accepting run does. The next theorem gives the exact
generalization to every c.e. degree.

\subsection{Realizing arbitrary c.e. deadline lower bounds}
Let $(A_s)_{s\in\N}$ be a computable increasing sequence of finite sets
with union $A$, so $A$ is computably enumerable. Fix a stage convention and
write $t_A(x)$ for the first stage at which $x$ enters $A$, when it does.
Define its finite-initial-segment enumeration modulus
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:enum-modulus}
 b_A(i)=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup\{t_A(x):x\le i,\ x\in A\}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The function $b_A$ is computable from $A$: ask which of $0,\ldots,i$ enter,
then enumerate until all those positive answers have appeared. Conversely,
any pointwise upper bound $h$ for $b_A$ computes $A$, since
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:decide-c.e.}
 x\in A\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad x\in A_{h(x)}.
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}
For a system input $e$, its \emph{deadline oracle spectrum} is
\[
 \operatorname{Spec}_{\mathrm{clk}}(e)=
 \{\deg_T(Z):\text{ some $Z$-computable deadline is met by a run on }e\}.
\]
This records oracle capabilities, not the degrees of every individual
schedule. In particular, the accepting schedule need not be computable
from the oracle that computes the deadline.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Exact realization of every c.e. degree]\label{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}
Uniformly from an index for a c.e. set $A$, one can construct an input $e_A$
to a fixed binary-controlled two-stack system such that
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:spectrum}
 \operatorname{Spec}_{\mathrm{clk}}(e_A)
   =\{\mathbf z:\deg_T(A)\le\mathbf z\}.
\end{equation}
Every accepting scheduler computes $A$, and an $A$-computable accepting
scheduler exists. Consequently its least accepting-scheduler degree and
its least deadline-oracle degree are both $\deg_T(A)$.
The same result holds for the fixed rational guarded affine realization
constructed in \cref{lbh:sec:geometry}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Define a tree $T_A$ on natural sequences. A sequence $\sigma$ of length $n$
is in $T_A$ when
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:c.e.-tree}
 \forall i<n\ \forall x\le i:\quad
 x\in A_n\ \Rightarrow\ t_A(x)\le\sigma(i).
\end{equation}
All entry stages needed in this formula can be computed by looking only at
the finite enumeration through stage $n$. Thus the tree is uniformly
computable, and under a bounded universal-enumeration convention it is
primitive recursive. It is prefix closed because shortening a sequence
only removes coordinates and reduces the enumeration stage inspected.
Its infinite paths are exactly the $g$ with $g(i)\ge b_A(i)$ for all $i$.
Indeed, an eventual enumeration at stage $s$ is enforced by all sufficiently
long prefixes $n\ge\max(s,i+1)$, and the reverse implication is immediate.

The fixed tree-verification interpreter takes the enumeration index as
part of its finite input. It guesses each $g(i)$ in unary, checks the next
prefix, and pulses on success. From an accepting schedule $f$, simulate the
run and recover each completed unary value; this computes $g$ from $f$.
Equation \eqref{lbh:eq:decide-c.e.} then computes $A$, so $A\le_T f$.
Taking $g=b_A$, which is $A$-computable, and using a default bit at every
ignored action step gives an $A$-computable accepting schedule.

Now suppose a $Z$-computable deadline $d$ is met by some accepting schedule,
not assumed $Z$-computable. Unary guessing gives
\[
 b_A(i)\le g(i)\le\tau_f(i+1)\le d(i+1).
\]
The $Z$-computable function $i\mapsto d(i+1)$ therefore decides $A$ by
\cref{lbh:eq:decide-c.e.}. Hence $A\le_T Z$.
Conversely, if $A\le_T Z$, use the $A$-computable schedule just constructed
and simulate its successive accepting times with oracle $Z$. They form a
$Z$-computable deadline. This proves \cref{lbh:eq:spectrum}, as well as the
least-degree assertions. The geometric realization is step-exact, so the
same inequalities and clocks are preserved there.
\end{proof}

This is a useful concrete form of the clock obstruction: it can have any
c.e. degree, not just the full halting degree. It also strengthens a purely
negative statement. The construction identifies exactly which oracles are
sufficient, and it supplies a matching least-degree accepting schedule.
The ingredients are enumeration moduli and recursive trees; the theorem
packages them as a uniform liveness and exact-substrate realization.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Batch~79, 2 October 2026: \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum} is the c.e.\ case of
source~12's \cref{lbh:cs:cor:delta} (Part~VI), which realizes every $\Delta^0_2$ degree as a least
clock degree by a first-matching-stage tree with a unique path, for source~12's own fixed two-stack
interpreter; the key inequality is the same (\cref{lbh:cs:eq:clock-bound}). Source~12 does not
restate the least-scheduler-degree clause (\cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation}).}

\begin{corollary}\label[corollary]{lbh:cor:halting-spectrum}
There is an input with recurrent runs but no computable deadline, whose
deadline oracle spectrum is exactly the upper cone of $\mathbf0'$.
There is a $\mathbf0'$-computable accepting schedule and a
$\mathbf0'$-computable deadline, but every accepting schedule computes
$\mathbf0'$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Take $A$ to be the halting set in \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Computable schedules are a different \texorpdfstring{$\sig3$}{Sigma-3} property}\label{lbh:sec:compclass}
Let $\Comp(e)$ mean that an accepting schedule is computable.
There is a $\sig3$ upper bound: existentially choose a program index,
then require that for every $k$ some finite simulation produces enough
schedule bits for $k$ accepting visits, while verifying totality on the
first $k$ inputs. A single larger finite stage combines these requirements.
The $\sig3$ hardness construction in \cref{lbh:sec:hierarchy} already produces
a computable accepting schedule in every positive instance and no accepting
schedule in any negative instance. Thus $\Comp$ is $\sig3$-complete for
the fixed universal system.

Every computable accepting schedule has computable accepting times, so
$\Comp\subseteq\Clock$. The DNR example makes this inclusion strict.
Equal completeness classifications do not identify the underlying
semantics. In particular, existential quantification over program indices
represents $\Comp$, not arbitrary $\Buchi$ acceptance.
\subsection{Second routes to the separations}\label{lbh:sec:seproutes}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~03 proves the two separations for its fixed quadratic graph $E_U$, with
the diagonal tree $T_D$ (source~10's $T_{\mathrm{DNR}}$, \cref{lbh:eq:dnr-tree}) and the
halting-time tree $T_H$. Its section ``Two explicit strict separations'' follows. Source~11's
diagonal lemma is \cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc}, whose third proof is source~11's
(\cref{lbh:ql:sec:separations}).}
\subsection{Two explicit strict separations\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:separations}

We fix an effective enumeration $(\varphi_e)_{e\in\N}$ of partial computable
unary functions. A halting time means the number of steps in this fixed
enumeration's simulation, counted from one. All tests bounded by a numerical
stage are decidable. The infinite paths constructed below need not be
computable; their existence is a classical mathematical statement.

\subsubsection{Computably bounded recurrence without a computable run}

For a binary string $\sigma$ of length $n$, define
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:dnctree}
 \sigma\in T_D\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall e<n:\left[
 \begin{array}{l}
 \varphi_e(e)\text{ halts within }n\text{ steps}\text{ with output }b\in\{0,1\}
 \end{array}\right]
 \Longrightarrow\ \sigma(e)\ne b.
\end{equation}
The implication is understood separately for each $e$.

\begin{lemma}\label[lemma]{lbh:qd:lem:dnc}\label[lemma]{lbh:ql:lem:dnr}
$T_D$ is a recursive binary tree with an infinite branch but no computable
infinite branch.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Only $n$ computations, each for $n$ steps, are needed to decide membership.
If a string satisfies these conditions at length $n$, a prefix of length
$m<n$ satisfies them as well: every computation checked at stage $m$ is
among those checked at stage $n$. Thus $T_D$ is a tree.

Define, without asserting computability,
\[
 f(e)=\begin{cases}
 1-b,&\varphi_e(e)\text{ eventually halts with }b\in\{0,1\},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}
\]
Every finite prefix of $f$ belongs to $T_D$, so a branch exists. If a branch
$f$ were computable, there would be an index $j$ with $\varphi_j=f$.
Then $\varphi_j(j)$ halts with bit $f(j)$. For every sufficiently large
$n>j$, membership of $f\upharpoonright n$ would require
$f(j)\ne\varphi_j(j)=f(j)$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[First strict inclusion]\label{lbh:qd:thm:cbnotcp}
There is an input to the fixed quadratic system in $\CB\setminus\CP$.
Moreover, its successful paths share one computable deadline bound.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Interpret a program that repeatedly chooses one bit, appends it to its
current string, tests membership in $T_D$, halts on failure, and emits on
success. Its recurrent paths correspond exactly to infinite branches of
$T_D$. Such branches exist by \Cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc}.

For any $k$, there are only $2^k$ bit strings of length $k$. Simulate the
counter implementation separately with each of these finite choice strings
until it rejects or completes its $k$th emit. All these computations
terminate: there are only $k$ bounded membership tests, and each implemented
operation terminates. Compute the maximum of their finite runtimes and
monotonize these maxima. This gives a total computable $h(k)$ bounding the
$k$th emit time on every surviving path. Hence $\CB$ holds.

If a recurrent counter path were computable, search its state sequence for
successive emit boundaries and decode the chosen bits there. Event
reflection and effective boundary decoding make this a total computable
infinite branch of $T_D$. \Cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc} rules this out. Thus $\CP$
does not hold.
\end{proof}

This is a constructive specification of the system and its common deadline
algorithm, not a constructive specification of an infinite branch. It is
precisely the distinction between effective finite constraints and effective
choice from their nonempty inverse limit.

\subsubsection{Recurrence with no computable bound on any successful run}

For $\sigma\in\N^n$, define another tree:
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:halttree}
 \sigma\in T_H\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall e<n:\bigl[
 \varphi_e(e)\text{ halts within }n\text{ steps at time }t
 \ \Longrightarrow\ \sigma(e)\geq t\bigr].
\end{equation}
Again membership is decidable by bounded simulation, and earlier checks
remain in force under extension.

\begin{lemma}\label[lemma]{lbh:qd:lem:haltbound}
$T_H$ is a recursive tree with an infinite branch. No infinite branch of
$T_H$ is pointwise bounded by a total computable function.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Prefix closure and decidability follow as in \Cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc}. Define
$f(e)$ to be the halting time of $\varphi_e(e)$ if it halts, and zero
otherwise. Every prefix of $f$ passes the test, so a branch exists.

Let $g$ be any infinite branch. For every halting computation
$\varphi_e(e)$ with time $t$, take a prefix length at least both $e+1$ and
$t$. The condition then forces $g(e)\geq t$. If a total computable function
$b(e)$ bounded $g(e)$ for every $e$, one could decide the diagonal halting
problem: simulate $\varphi_e(e)$ for $b(e)$ steps, answer yes if it halts and
no otherwise. A halting computation must have halted within that bound.
This contradicts undecidability of the halting problem.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Second strict inclusion]\label{lbh:qd:thm:recnotcb}
There is an input to the fixed quadratic system in $\REC\setminus\CB$.
Every one of its recurrent paths has return times not pointwise bounded by
any total computable function.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the unary-guess program from \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:analytic} with tree $T_H$.
A branch exists, so a recurrent run exists. On any recurrent run, let $g(e)$
be its $e$th completed natural-number guess. The corresponding guesses form
a branch of $T_H$.

Producing the guess $g(e)$ requires $g(e)$ iterations of the explicit
CONTINUE loop before the $(e+1)$st emit. Each implemented iteration takes
at least one counter step. Therefore
\[
 g(e)\leq \tau_s(e+1).
\]
If a total computable deadline $h$ bounded the marked times of this path,
then $e\mapsto h(e+1)$ would pointwise bound the branch $g$. This contradicts
\Cref{lbh:qd:lem:haltbound}. The argument applies to every recurrent path,
not just the particular branch used to prove existence.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Strict hierarchy inside one quadratic graph]
\label[corollary]{lbh:qd:cor:strict}
For suitable inputs of the same fixed $E_U$,
\[
 \boxed{\CP\ \subsetneq\ \CB\ \subsetneq\ \REC.}
\]
The first two sets are both $\Sigma^0_3$-complete; the third is
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\end{corollary}

The pointwise form of the bound is intentional. If an eventually dominating
computable function existed for a particular branch, finitely many constant
corrections could turn it into a pointwise computable bound. Thus the second
separation also excludes eventual computable domination for a successful
run's return times.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11 proves the two separations for effectively presented binary-branching
programs and their polynomial presentations, with the trees $\T_{\mathrm{diag}}$ and
$\T_{\mathrm{halt}}$. Its section ``Two explicit strict separations'' follows; its
proposition on $0'$-computable runs is printed after \cref{lbh:thm:core}.}
\subsection{Two explicit strict separations\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:separations}
Fix a standard effective enumeration $(\varphi_e)$ of partial computable
natural functions, with a decidable bounded-step simulation relation. A
computation that is said to halt ``within $n$ steps'' refers to this fixed
simulation convention.

\subsubsection{A computable deadline, but no computable recurrent run}
Define a binary tree $\T_{\mathrm{diag}}\subseteq\{0,1\}^{<\N}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:dnrtree}
 \sigma\in\T_{\mathrm{diag}}
 \iff
 \forall e<|\sigma|\;
 \left[
 \begin{array}{l}
 \varphi_e(e)\text{ halts within }|\sigma|\text{ steps}\\[-1pt]
 \text{with output }b\in\{0,1\}
 \end{array}
 \Longrightarrow \sigma(e)\neq b\right].
\end{equation}
Membership is decidable by bounded simulation. The set is prefix-closed:
every constraint seen at an earlier length is still seen at a later length.

\wtag{} Source~11's lemma (``$\T_{\mathrm{diag}}$ has an infinite path but no computable infinite
path'') is \cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc} for the tree \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:dnrtree}, which is
\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:dnctree}.
\begin{proof}[Third proof of \cref{lbh:qd:lem:dnc} (source 11)]
For each $e$, choose the bit opposite to the eventual binary output of
$\varphi_e(e)$ if there is one, and choose zero otherwise. This defines a
path through the tree. If a computable binary path $f$ existed, it would have
an index $e$ with $\varphi_e(e)=f(e)$. At a sufficiently long prefix, that
halting computation would be detected and \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:dnrtree} would require
$f(e)\neq f(e)$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}
Now build a binary-branching program that chooses one bit at each stage,
appends it to $\sigma$, tests \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:dnrtree}, halts on failure, and marks
success once.

\begin{theorem}[Strictness of $\CRec\subseteq\DRec$]
\label{lbh:ql:thm:firstseparation}
This program has a recurrent run with a computable deadline but has no
computable recurrent run. The same is true of its polynomial presentation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
An infinite tree path gives a recurrent run. The test at each fixed length is
a total bounded computation. There are finitely many binary words at that
length. Exhaustively simulate the finitely many stage computations and take
a maximum of their running times; add the preceding maxima and normalize.
This gives a total computable deadline for every run that successfully reaches
those stages, in particular for the recurrent one.

From a computable recurrent run, search for successive marked checkpoints
and read the chosen bits. This would compute an infinite path in
$\T_{\mathrm{diag}}$, contradicting \cref{lbh:ql:lem:dnr}. The incoming-edge
compiler preserves complete paths and marker times exactly, so it preserves
both conclusions.
\end{proof}
The deadline need not be efficient. Its computability is enough. This example
also shows why finite branching plus an effective clock does not automatically
produce a computable infinite choice sequence.

\subsubsection{Recurrence with no computable deadline at all}
Let $h(e)$ be the halting time of $\varphi_e(e)$ when it halts. Define a tree
$\T_{\mathrm{halt}}\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:halttree}
 \sigma\in\T_{\mathrm{halt}}
 \iff
 \forall e<|\sigma|\;
 \bigl[\varphi_e(e)\text{ halts within }|\sigma|\text{ steps}
       \Longrightarrow h(e)\leq\sigma(e)\bigr].
\end{equation}
Again membership is decidable and the tree is prefix-closed. There is an
infinite path $f$, obtained by taking $f(e)=h(e)$ for halting computations and
$f(e)=0$ otherwise.

Use the unary-guessing program of \cref{lbh:ql:lem:tree} for this tree. Implement the
choice of $f(e)$ with at least one machine transition per increment, and give
exactly one mark for each successfully completed tree level.
\begin{theorem}[Strictness of $\DRec\subseteq\Rec$]
\label{lbh:ql:thm:secondseparation}
The resulting binary-branching program is recurrent, but no recurrent run of
it admits any total computable deadline. Its polynomial presentation has the
same property.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The path described above gives recurrence. Let $\rho$ be any recurrent run
and $f(e)$ its $e$th chosen natural number. For every halting $e$, sufficiently
late tests in \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:halttree} force $h(e)\leq f(e)$. The unary choice
mechanism ensures
\[
 f(e)\leq\tau_{e+1}(\rho).
\]
If a total computable $g$ bounded all visit times, then for every halting $e$,
\[
 h(e)\leq f(e)\leq g(e+1).
\]
The diagonal halting problem could therefore be decided by computing
$g(e+1)$ and simulating $\varphi_e(e)$ for that many steps. This contradicts
its undecidability. The argument applies to every recurrent run, not just to
the particular path used to prove existence.
\end{proof}
This example has a recurrent witness computable in the halting oracle: that
oracle can determine each $h(e)$ or its divergence. Thus even existence of a
$0'$-computable recurrent run does not imply existence of a computable deadline.

Combining the results gives the strict witness hierarchy
\[
 \boxed{\CRec\ \subsetneq\ \DRec\ \subsetneq\ \Rec.}
\]
The first two index sets are both $\Sigma^0_3$-complete; the third is
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete. The proof supplies concrete separating machines in
addition to these index-set distinctions.
\section{The deterministic specialization}\label{lbh:sec:deterministic}
If the transition ignores the action bit, an input has a unique computable
run. This removes the source of the analytical jump.

\begin{theorem}[Deterministic boundary]\label{lbh:thm:deterministic}\label{lbh:qd:prop:det}
For a computable deterministic system,
\[
 \Clock(e)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\Buchi(e).
\]
The common predicate is $\ppi2$ in general and $\ppi2$-complete for a
suitable fixed deterministic two-stack system. Finite reachability,
fixed-deadline recurrence, and bounded-gap recurrence have sharp bounds
$\sig1$, $\ppi1$, and $\sig2$, respectively.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A recurrent computable run yields a program computing its $k$th accepting
time by simulation, so it yields a computable deadline. Conversely every
deadline entails recurrence. For the unique run, the condition
$\forall k\exists t\,N_e(t)\ge k$ has a decidable matrix. To obtain
$\ppi2$ hardness, on program index $p$ sequentially simulate
$\varphi_p(0),\varphi_p(1),\ldots$, emitting one pulse after each halt.
The run is recurrent exactly when $\varphi_p$ is total. More generally,
the same construction implements any $\forall k\exists s$ decidable
normal form.

The reachability and fixed-deadline lower bounds in
\cref{lbh:sec:hierarchy} are already deterministic. For bounded gaps, the
unique run yields the upper bound $\exists b\forall t$ directly.
For hardness, use the $\sig2$-complete finiteness problem for a c.e. set
$W_p$. Run a standard staged enumerator, marking all ordinary work steps
accepting. At the $j$th distinct enumeration event, insert a nonaccepting
block of at least $j$ microsteps and then resume ordinary work. Each stage
and each inserted block is finite. If $W_p$ is finite, only finitely many
nonaccepting blocks occur, so a gap bound exists. If $W_p$ is infinite,
nonaccepting blocks have unbounded lengths, so no gap bound exists.

For completeness, finiteness of c.e. sets has the required hardness by a
standard moving-candidate construction. Given
$\exists m\forall k R(e,m,k)$, at stage $s$ let $m_s$ be the least
$m\le s$ passing all tests $k\le s$, or let $m_s=s+1$ if there is none.
Enumerate a fresh number whenever $m_s$ changes. If there is a least true
witness $m$, all smaller false candidates are eventually excluded and
$m_s$ stabilizes at $m$. If no witness exists, every finite candidate is
eventually excluded and $m_s$ changes infinitely often. This reduces the
normal form to finiteness. These procedures are modes of one fixed
deterministic interpreter, with accepting flags as above.
\end{proof}
\wtag{} Source~03 proves the same boundary (its Proposition ``Deterministic recurrence''):
\emph{for effective deterministic systems, recurrence is $\Pi^0_2$, and this bound is attained
in an effectively coded family of counter programs, hence in the deterministic inputs of~$U$}
(\cref{lbh:qd:lem:universal}). Its proof refers to the last paragraph of the proof of
\cref{lbh:qd:thm:affine}, which treats a one-successor relation.
\begin{proof}[Second proof (source 03)]
The upper bound is the formula in the last paragraph of
\Cref{lbh:qd:thm:affine}. For a predicate $\forall b\exists c\,R(n,b,c)$
with decidable $R$, run a deterministic search for a witness $c$ for $b=0$,
emit when found, then search for $b=1$, and so on. There are infinitely many
emits exactly when the predicate holds. A failed search makes no further
emits. This is a reduction from any $\Pi^0_2$ predicate.
\end{proof}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
Property & Controlled universal system & Deterministic universal system\\
\midrule
Finite reachability & $\sig1$ & $\sig1$\\
Fixed computable deadline & $\ppi1$ & $\ppi1$\\
Some finite gap bound & $\sig2$ & $\sig2$\\
Some computable deadline & $\sig3$ & $\ppi2$\\
Unrestricted recurrence & $\an$ & $\ppi2$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every entry denotes completeness for the corresponding fixed universal
system. Infinite behavior by itself does not force analytic complexity;
existential quantification over arbitrary compatible schedules does.
\part{Low-degree transition laws and finite certificates}\label{lbh:part:certificates}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Part~III prints the three polynomial compilers: source~03's degree-two
control-vector law with its affine lower bound and fixed universal instance, source~11's
degree-two incoming-edge law with its finite-history and deadline certificates and fixed
analytic-complete instance, and source~10's two-stack trace quartic. They are compared first.}

\section{Three compilers compared}\label{lbh:sec:threecompilers}
\wtag{} The three sources compile three different machine models, and their numbers are not
interchangeable.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{0.17\textwidth}XXX@{}}
\toprule
 & Source 03, \cref{lbh:qd:thm:compiler} & Source 11, \cref{lbh:ql:thm:compiler} & Source 10, \cref{lbh:thm:compiler}\\
\midrule
system & counter network & binary-branching unit-counter machine & two-stack machine, one trace\\
coordinates & $q+r+m$: one-hot control, counters, last-edge tag & $d+m$: counters, incoming-edge tag & horizon-indexed trace, $H(3+R+G)+k$\\
degree & $2$ & $2$ ($P_2$); sum of squares of degree $4$ ($P_4$) & $4$\\
coefficient height & $\le8$, sharp for this encoding (\cref{lbh:qd:thm:height}) & $\le6$, sharp for this encoding (\cref{lbh:ql:sec:height}) & not stated\\
old tag & free (the source tag is unrestricted) & constrained by $U(b)^2$ & ---\\
auxiliaries & none & none & rule indicators and slacks, all unique\\
deadlines & --- & degree $2$ (\cref{lbh:ql:cor:deadlinepoly}) & inside the quartic (\cref{lbh:eq:deadline-residual})\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The two height bounds concern different encodings; neither contradicts the other's sharpness.
Source~03 keeps a one-hot control vector and separate source and target residuals, so two
parallel increments share four residual terms and reach~$8$; source~11 reads the control from
the target of the incoming edge and uses one flow residual per control, so the same pair reaches
$6$ in its expansion. Source~11 thereby answers in part two of source~03's questions for its own
encoding: height drops from $8$ to $6$ while $q$ control coordinates disappear (``Height reduction
versus dimension'', \cref{lbh:q:qd-height}), and control coordinates are dispensable when tags are
kept, the dual of ``When are edge tags dispensable?'' (\cref{lbh:q:qd-tags}).

For counter machines, source~11's degree-two deadline certificate
(\cref{lbh:ql:cor:deadlinepoly}) uses the same surplus device as source~10's deadline residual
(\cref{lbh:eq:deadline-residual}) but keeps the whole certificate at degree two, where
source~10's two-stack trace needs degree four.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Batch~79, 2 October 2026: for two-stack traces as well, source~12's
\cref{lbh:cs:thm:quadratic} (Part~VI) keeps the labelled-trace certificate with deadlines at degree
two, with $3(T+1)+9mT+k$ natural coordinates and unique helpers; \cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation} compares
it with \cref{lbh:thm:compiler}.}

Report~A of this category (\path{canonical-diophantine-certificates}) encodes a zero guard of a
counter program by the squared product $e_{i,a}x_{i,p}=0$ (its \texttt{cdc:eq:zero-guard}) or by
an affine big-$M$ guard for fixed numerical registers, and observes that with free initial
registers ``the uniform-parameter presentation is then quartic unless another representation is
supplied''. Sources~03 and~11 supply that other representation: the unsquared nonnegative product
$e_ax_{j(a)}$ (source~03) or $c_ex_{i(e)}$ (source~11) keeps the transition relation at degree
two uniformly in the registers, at the price of convexity and of a pure sum-of-squares form;
source~11's $P_4$ squares the guard, which is how Report~A reaches its quartics.
\section{An exact quadratic transition compiler\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:compiler}

\subsection{The counter-network language}

Let $Q=\{0,\ldots,q-1\}$ be a nonempty set of controls, let there be $r$
natural counters, and let $\mathcal E=\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$ be a finite list of
labelled edges. An edge $a$ has source $u(a)$ and target $v(a)$, and one of the
following operations:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Operation & Enabling condition & Counter update\\
\midrule
$\mathsf{inc}(j)$ & Always & $x_j'=x_j+1$\\
$\mathsf{dec}(j)$ & $x_j>0$ & $x_j'=x_j-1$\\
$\mathsf{zero}(j)$ & $x_j=0$ & All counters unchanged\\
$\mathsf{nop}$ & Always & All counters unchanged\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All counters not named in an update remain unchanged. Distinct edges remain
distinct even if their endpoints and operations coincide. A usual conditional
decrement instruction has a decrement edge and a zero edge from the same
control. A binary nondeterministic choice uses two no-operation edges.

Write $\Delta_a\in\{-1,0,1\}^r$ for the update vector. It has at most one
nonzero coordinate. Let $Z\subseteq\mathcal E$ consist of the zero-test edges,
and let $j(a)$ be the tested counter for $a\in Z$. In particular,
$\Delta_a=0$ for every $a\in Z$.

\subsection{State coordinates and the polynomial}

Use the state space
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:state}
 s=(p,x,h)\in\N^q\times\N^r\times\N^m=\N^D,
 \qquad D=q+r+m.
\end{equation}
Here $p$ is a one-hot control vector, $x$ is the counter vector, and $h$
records the most recently selected edge. The old tag $h$ is deliberately
ignored. Initial states use $h=0$. If $s'=(p',x',h')$, write $e=h'$ and set
\begin{align}
 L_0&=\sum_{a\in\mathcal E}e_a-1,\nonumber\\
 O_i&=p_i-\sum_{u(a)=i}e_a,\qquad
 I_i=p_i'-\sum_{v(a)=i}e_a,\label{lbh:qd:eq:residuals}\\
 C_j&=x_j'-x_j-\sum_{a\in\mathcal E}\Delta_{a,j}e_a.\nonumber
\end{align}
The compiler outputs
\begin{equation}\boxed{\displaystyle
 E_M(s,s')=L_0^2+\sum_{i<q}O_i^2+\sum_{i<q}I_i^2
                +\sum_{j<r}C_j^2+\sum_{a\in Z}e_a x_{j(a)}.}
 \label{lbh:qd:eq:energy}
\end{equation}
This is one polynomial in $2D$ coordinates. There are no implicit witness
variables. Some of its variables, namely the source tag coordinates, do not
occur with nonzero coefficients.

\begin{theorem}[Exact local semantics]\label{lbh:qd:thm:compiler}
For every source state $s\in\N^D$ and target state $s'\in\N^D$,
$E_M(s,s')=0$ if and only if $s$ has a one-hot control vector and $s'$ is
obtained by one enabled labelled edge of $M$, with $h'$ the one-hot tag of
that edge. The old source tag is unrestricted. Every state has at most
\[
 b=\max_{i<q}\#\{a:u(a)=i\}
\]
successors. The polynomial has degree at most two and is nonnegative on
$\R_{\geq0}^{2D}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
All terms in~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:energy} are nonnegative on the stated orthant. If the
sum is zero, every square vanishes and every displayed product vanishes.
The equation $L_0=0$ says that natural numbers $e_a$ sum to one. Therefore
there is exactly one $a_*$ with $e_{a_*}=1$, and all other selectors are zero.
The equations $O_i=0$ and $I_i=0$ now give the source and target control
vectors of precisely this edge. In particular, malformed source controls
cannot acquire a successor.

The equations $C_j=0$ give $x'=x+\Delta_{a_*}$. If the operation is a
decrement, $x'_{j(a_*)}\in\N$ implies $x_{j(a_*)}>0$. If it is a zero test,
the term $e_{a_*}x_{j(a_*)}$ forces $x_{j(a_*)}=0$. No additional guard is
needed for an increment or a no-operation edge. Finally, $h'=e$ records the
edge uniquely.

Conversely, an enabled edge with its prescribed target and tag makes every
residual and zero-test term vanish. Once the edge is chosen, its target
control, all counters, and tag are fixed. Distinct labelled edges have
different target tags, so the number of successors is exactly the number
of enabled edges at a well-formed source, and is at most $b$. Every term has
degree at most two. Nonnegativity follows directly from the displayed
factorization, without relying on the signs of expanded coefficients.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why there is no Boolean equation]
Over $\N$, the equation $\sum e_a=1$ already forces one-hotness. Equations
$e_a(e_a-1)=0$ would be redundant. Over $\R_{\geq0}$ this inference fails:
for two parallel increment edges, selectors $(1/2,1/2)$ satisfy the relaxed
control and update equations. The continuous zero set is not the intended
finite-branching graph.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why the zero-test term is not squared]
The unsquared product $e_a x_j$ is nonnegative because both factors are
natural numbers. Thus $E=0$ forces this product to vanish. Replacing it by
$(e_a x_j)^2$ would preserve natural semantics but unnecessarily raise the
degree to four. Over unrestricted integers, the nonnegativity argument would
no longer justify the unsquared term.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Coefficient height and circuit size}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform quantitative bounds]\label{lbh:qd:thm:height}
The compiler satisfies $\htp(E_M)\leq8$, independently of $q,r,m$. The bound
is attained by two parallel increment edges. If $m_u$ is the number of
increment or decrement edges and $z=|Z|$, the displayed factored evaluation
uses at most
\begin{align}
 1+2q+r+z&\quad\text{multiplications},\label{lbh:qd:eq:circuitM}\\
 3m+m_u+2q+2r+z&\quad\text{additions or subtractions},\label{lbh:qd:eq:circuitA}
\end{align}
when input access, constants, and copying are free. Thus its factored size
is linear in the edge table and state dimension.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a selector $e_a$, the coefficient of $e_a^2$ is
\[
 1+1+1+\langle\Delta_a,\Delta_a\rangle\leq4.
\]
For distinct selectors $e_a,e_b$, the coefficient is
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:qd:eq:crosscoefficient}
 2\bigl(1+\one_{u(a)=u(b)}+\one_{v(a)=v(b)}
                       +\langle\Delta_a,\Delta_b\rangle\bigr).
\end{equation}
The inner product lies between $-1$ and $1$, so the absolute value is at most
eight. Source-control--selector and target-control--selector coefficients
are $-2$. Counter--selector coefficients contributed by a square are $0$ or
$\pm2$. A zero-test term contributes coefficient $1$ to $e_a x_{j(a)}$;
this cannot add to an update contribution for the same selector because
$\Delta_a=0$ on a zero edge. Counter squares have coefficient $1$,
$x_jx_j'$ has coefficient $-2$, and the remaining linear and constant terms
have absolute value at most two. Old tag coordinates never occur. This
exhausts the monomial types.

If two distinct edges have identical source, target, and increment update,
every term inside the parentheses in~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:crosscoefficient} equals one,
so their cross coefficient equals eight. This proves sharpness for this
compiler, not for all possible encodings.

To count the factored computation, initialize $L_0$ to $-1$ and add each
selector once, costing $m$ additions. Initialize each $O_i$ to $p_i$ and
subtract its outgoing selectors, for $m$ more operations; do the same for
$I_i$, for another $m$. Counter residuals cost $r+m_u$ operations. There
are $1+2q+r$ squares and $z$ zero-test products. Summing these scalar terms
costs $2q+r+z$ additions. This yields~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:circuitM}--\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:circuitA}.
\end{proof}

The expanded polynomial may have quadratically many selector cross terms.
The linear-size assertion concerns the factored circuit, not a sparse
monomial list. Likewise, the operation count is for \emph{one local step};
it is not a competing operation count for a complete universal halting
certificate.
\section{Why quadratic degree is minimal here\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:affine}

The next obstruction is useful because it states exactly which constraints
make the degree-two result optimal. There must be one equality, the entire
state space must be $\N^D$, no existential variables or side conditions may
hide a second relation, and outdegree must have a global finite bound.

\begin{theorem}[Affine bounded-branching obstruction]\label{lbh:qd:thm:affine}
Let
\[
 L(x,y)=c+\sum_{i=1}^D a_i x_i+\sum_{j=1}^D b_j y_j
 \quad(c,a_i,b_j\in\Z)
\]
define a graph on $\N^D$ by $L(x,y)=0$. Suppose every vertex has at most
$B<\infty$ successors.

If $D\geq2$, either the graph has no edges, or all its targets lie in a fixed
finite set. Consequently recurrence from a computable initial-state map,
with any fixed decidable marker, is decidable. If $D=1$, recurrence is at
most $\Pi^0_2$. In no dimension can such an affine graph have
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete recurrence.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Assume first that some edge exists and $D\geq2$. If one $b_j$ were zero,
we could vary $y_j$ arbitrarily along that edge, producing infinitely many
successors. If two target coefficients had opposite signs, there would be
a nonzero $v\in\N^D$ with $b\cdot v=0$, and $y+tv$ would again give
infinitely many successors. Hence the $b_j$ are all nonzero and of the same
sign. Multiply $L$ by $-1$ if necessary, so all $b_j>0$.

We claim all $a_i\geq0$. Suppose instead that $a_i=-u<0$, and fix a
solution $(x^{(0)},y^{(0)})$. Choose two target coordinates with coefficients
$b_1,b_2$. For $t\geq0$ increase $x_i^{(0)}$ by $b_1b_2t$. Then, for each
$k\in\{0,\ldots,t\}$, the target obtained by adding
\[
 b_2u k\quad\text{to }y_1^{(0)},\qquad
 b_1u(t-k)\quad\text{to }y_2^{(0)}
\]
and leaving other coordinates unchanged is a solution for the same new
source. Indeed, the target contribution rises by $u b_1b_2t$, exactly
cancelling the source contribution. These are $t+1$ distinct successors,
contradicting the fixed bound $B$ for large $t$.

It follows that every edge satisfies
\[
 \sum_j b_jy_j=-c-\sum_i a_ix_i\leq -c.
\]
Thus each target is in the finite box
$0\leq y_j\leq\lfloor(-c)/b_j\rfloor$. Every path, after its first step,
lies in this fixed finite set. Enumerate the possible first successors and
then inspect the finite graph. An infinite recurrent path exists exactly
when a reachable directed cycle can visit a marked state. This is decidable.
The empty-edge cases are also trivial. Under the promised global branching
bound, the coefficient cases ruled out above may simply be treated as empty.

For $D=1$, if $b_1=0$ an edge would have infinitely many targets, so the
graph is empty. Otherwise each source has at most one successor,
$y=(-c-a_1x)/b_1$, when this is a natural integer. Its partial successor
function is computable. Recurrence is therefore expressible by
\[
 \forall k\ \exists t\geq k:
 \text{the unique run is defined through time }t\text{ and is marked at }t,
\]
a $\Pi^0_2$ statement. The strictness of the analytical hierarchy excludes
$\Sigma^1_1$-completeness in all cases.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Degree optimality]\label[corollary]{lbh:qd:cor:minimal}
Among single-polynomial natural transition relations on an unrestricted
fixed finite-dimensional state space, with a uniform finite outdegree bound
and no existential transition auxiliaries, degree two is the least degree
that can realize $\Sigma^1_1$-complete recurrent-path existence.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The lower bound is \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:affine}; constant polynomials are included.
The upper bound is the universal construction and recurrence theorem proved
in \Cref{lbh:qd:sec:universal,lbh:qd:sec:analytic}.
\end{proof}

This lower bound does not apply to a conjunction of affine equations together
with external nonlinear guards, to a restricted state domain that already
encodes computation, or to unbounded branching. Those are different models.
It also does not assert minimal state dimension or minimal coefficient height.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11 proves a degree-one obstruction under complementary hypotheses:
\cref{lbh:qd:thm:affine} allows a signed affine relation but needs a uniform finite branching
bound; source~11's \cref{lbh:ql:prop:degree-one} needs the affine polynomial to be nonnegative on
the orthant but allows unbounded branching. Its subsection follows (from its section on the
fixed analytic-complete law, \cref{lbh:ql:sec:analytic}).}
\subsection{A degree-one obstruction\srctag{11}}
\begin{proposition}[Degree two is minimal in the stated nonnegative model]
\label[proposition]{lbh:ql:prop:degree-one}
Let $L(X,Y)$ be a polynomial of degree at most one with integer coefficients,
nonnegative on $\R_{\geq0}^{2D}$. For a computable marked set, recurrence in
its natural zero graph is at most computably enumerable as a predicate of the
initial state. In particular it cannot be $\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write $L=c+\alpha\cdot X+\beta\cdot Y$. Nonnegativity at zero and along
each coordinate ray forces $c,\alpha_i,\beta_i\geq0$. If $c>0$, the graph is
empty. Otherwise it is a Cartesian product $U\times V$, where $U$ and $V$
are coordinate-zero faces of $\N^D$.

A recurrent run from $a$ exists precisely when $a\in U$ and there is a
marked $z\in U\cap V$. Sufficiency follows from the run $a,z,z,\ldots$.
Necessity follows because every positive-time state of an infinite run lies
in $U\cap V$, and some such state is marked. The existential search for $z$
is computably enumerable. For the linear one-hot-style marker used in this
report it is even an elementary integer linear feasibility problem.
\end{proof}
This is a lower bound for a \emph{single nonnegative affine polynomial}.
It is not a classification of arbitrary signed affine relations, arbitrary
systems, or different ambient-domain conventions.
\section{A fixed universal quadratic substrate\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:universal}

To transport reductions without introducing false recurrent runs, ordinary
forward simulation is not enough. We require event reflection and terminating
implementations of individual source steps.

\begin{lemma}[Positive-step, event-reflecting compilation]\label[lemma]{lbh:qd:lem:universal}
There is a fixed finite counter network $U$ with at most two syntactic outgoing
edges per control, a marked control $q_A$, and a computable input map
$n\mapsto c(n)$ such that the following programs can be interpreted from $n$:
deterministic effective algorithms, binary choices, and a distinguished
$\EMIT$ operation. On well-formed programs, every implemented source step
takes a positive finite number of counter steps; nondeterministic choices
are exactly the source choices; and visits to $q_A$ correspond exactly to
completed $\EMIT$ operations. Decoding source configurations at instruction
boundaries is effective. Halting is not replaced by an accepting self-loop.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We spell out the operational ingredients; counter universality itself is
classical~\cite{shepherdson-sturgis}. Represent a finite stack over a fixed
alphabet by a natural number in base $B$, assigning symbols digits
$1,\ldots,B-1$ and representing the empty stack by zero. If the top is the
least significant digit, push has the form $x\mapsto Bx+d$, and pop returns
the remainder and quotient on division by $B$. An empty pop returns a
specified blank symbol and leaves the stack empty.

Both operations can be implemented with increment, decrement, zero, and
no-operation edges. To multiply $x$ by $B$, clear a scratch counter, decrease
$x$ once per loop iteration while increasing the scratch counter $B$ times,
and then transfer the scratch value back. A finite string of $d$ increments
completes the push. To divide, repeatedly decrease $x$, cycling a remainder
in the finite control through $0,\ldots,B-1$ and increasing a quotient
counter whenever the remainder wraps. When $x$ reaches zero, transfer the
quotient back. The final control records the remainder. Scratch counters
can be cleared or restored by terminating transfer loops. Every loop counts
down a natural number, so every individual stack operation terminates.

Two stacks, together with a current scanned symbol in the finite control,
simulate a Turing tape. A head move pushes the old symbol onto one stack and
pops from the other. Every source tape step therefore has a finite positive
implementation. Conditional loops have two syntactic edges, a decrement
edge and a zero edge, of which exactly one is enabled. A binary choice uses
two no-operation edges; splitting a larger finite choice into a binary
sequence is effective.

Take a fixed sequential interpreter for coded tape programs, augmented with
binary choice and $\EMIT$. A program description, its work tape, and its
current simulated instruction are finite strings, so the interpreter stores
and updates them by the same tape operations. It scans the finite code table
to implement the next source instruction. The interpreter does not optimize
away source steps. Its sole nondeterministic operation is the explicit
binary choice. Route $\EMIT$ through one reserved marked control, with a
single exit returning to the interpreter. No other operation enters that
control. A halt instruction has no continuation.

Compile this fixed interpreter into the counter network just described.
The initial natural $n$ codes the program and its finite input. Parsing and
loading terminate on valid codes; invalid codes may be rejected. The stack
representation and interpreter bookkeeping give effective decoding at
instruction boundaries. Every transition used to execute a source step is
positive in duration, and no unfinished step passes through $q_A$. These
facts give both event preservation and reflection.
\end{proof}

Apply \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:compiler} to $U$, and write its polynomial $E_U$. We
will use the fixed marker $A(s)\iff p_{q_A}=1$. The dimensions $q_U,r_U,m_U$
and $D_U=q_U+r_U+m_U$ are finite constants, independent of the input.

\begin{corollary}[One fixed low-degree graph]\label[corollary]{lbh:qd:cor:fixed}
A fixed integer polynomial $E_U$ of degree two and height at most eight,
on $\N^{D_U}\times\N^{D_U}$, has globally at most two successors per state
and can interpret all the programs used in this manuscript. The complete
successor list is computable from each state.
\end{corollary}

\noindent\textbf{Explicitness boundary.}
Formula~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:energy} is an explicit compiler for every supplied finite
edge table. The universal interpreter in \Cref{lbh:qd:lem:universal} is specified
constructively at the simulation level, not delivered as a fully enumerated
edge table. Accordingly, this paper claims no numerical value of $D_U$, no
smallest universal machine, and no numerical expanded universal polynomial.
The existence and degree/height bounds do not depend on such an optimization.
\section{Finite paths remain Diophantine\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:finite}

\begin{proposition}[Quadratic fixed-horizon certificate]\label[proposition]{lbh:qd:prop:finite}
For a fixed counter network and $T\in\N$, set
\[
 \mathcal Q_{M,T}(s_0,\ldots,s_T)
       =\sum_{t=0}^{T-1} E_M(s_t,s_{t+1}).
\]
On natural tuples, $\mathcal Q_{M,T}=0$ if and only if
$s_0,\ldots,s_T$ is a legal path. This certificate has degree at most two,
$(T+1)D$ state coordinates, and factored size linear in $T(q+r+m)$.
Fixed endpoints can be imposed by adding squares of coordinate differences.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every summand is nonnegative, so the sum vanishes exactly when every local
transition is legal. Adding endpoint squares preserves this equivalence.
The variable and size assertions follow by counting the displayed copies.
\end{proof}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~03's source tag $h$ is unrestricted (\cref{lbh:qd:thm:compiler}), so
$\mathcal Q_{M,T}$ has infinitely many natural zeros unless the initial tag is pinned. For a
single-fold certificate, pin the initial tag $h_0=0$ (source~03's initial states use $h=0$) by
adding the squares of its coordinates, as the last sentence of the proposition allows. Source~11's
history certificate (\cref{lbh:ql:cor:finite}) substitutes its initial state $X_0=a$ and so has
its zeros in bijection with paths.}
This does not give a single fixed finite tuple of variables for arbitrary
$T$. That compression is a different operation. It can be obtained through
finite-trace arithmetic or MRDP, but cannot be inferred merely by writing a
sum whose number of variables depends on $T$.
\section{An incoming-edge polynomial normal form\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:compiler}

\subsection{Encoding the current control without a control integer}
Adjoin a fresh label $*$ and a dummy edge
\[
 e_0:*\longrightarrow q_0
\]
with no counter change. No edge targets $*$. Write $E$ for the resulting edge
set, $m=|E|$, and $\bar Q=Q\cup\{*\}$. Let
\[
 \delta_e\in\{0,\pm\mathbf e_1,\ldots,\pm\mathbf e_d\}
\]
be the effect of edge $e$. A zero edge has $\delta_e=0$. Write $Z\subseteq E$
for the zero edges and $i(e)$ for their tested counter.

A state is
\[
 X=(x,b)\in\N^d\times\N^m,
 \qquad\sum_{e\in E}b_e=1.
\]
Because the coordinates are natural, this last equation already says that
$b$ is one-hot. No Boolean equations are needed. If $b_e=1$, the current
control is $\tgt(e)$. The initial state uses $b_{e_0}=1$ and the chosen input
counters. For a successor $Y=(y,c)$, the selected coordinate $c_f=1$ records
\emph{the edge just taken}. Incoming-edge memory distinguishes parallel
choices without creating an existential auxiliary witness.

Define the linear residuals
\begin{align}
 U(b)&=\sum_{e\in E}b_e-1,\label{lbh:ql:eq:U}\\
 V_q(b,c)&=\sum_{\tgt(e)=q}b_e-\sum_{\src(e)=q}c_e
              &&(q\in\bar Q),\label{lbh:ql:eq:V}\\
 W_i(x,y,c)&=y_i-x_i-\sum_{e\in E}\delta_{e,i}c_e
              &&(1\leq i\leq d).\label{lbh:ql:eq:W}
\end{align}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:common}
 C(X,Y)=U(b)^2+U(c)^2+
       \sum_{q\in\bar Q}V_q(b,c)^2+
       \sum_{i=1}^dW_i(x,y,c)^2.
\end{equation}
The two promised polynomials are
\begin{align}
 P_2(X,Y)&=C(X,Y)+\sum_{e\in Z}c_e x_{i(e)},\label{lbh:ql:eq:P2}\\
 P_4(X,Y)&=C(X,Y)+\sum_{e\in Z}\bigl(c_e x_{i(e)}\bigr)^2.
                                                        \label{lbh:ql:eq:P4}
\end{align}
The subscripts are degree bounds, not assertions that every instance attains
the bound. For example, a machine without zero edges gives a quadratic $P_4$.

\subsection{Exactness and the absence of hidden witnesses}
\begin{theorem}[Witness-free polynomial compiler]\label{lbh:ql:thm:compiler}
For every unit-counter machine as above, \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:P2} and \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:P4} have
integer coefficients and satisfy:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item $\deg P_2\leq2$, and $P_2\geq0$ on
$\R_{\geq0}^{2(d+m)}$.
\item $\deg P_4\leq4$, and $P_4$ is a sum of
$2+|\bar Q|+d+|Z|$ squares, hence is nonnegative on all real inputs.
\item For $X,Y\in\N^{d+m}$, $P_2(X,Y)=0$ if and only if
$P_4(X,Y)=0$, if and only if $Y$ is the next incoming-edge state obtained by
one enabled machine edge from $X$.
\item Every natural state has at most two successors. From an encoded initial
state there is a bijection between edge-labelled machine paths and polynomial
paths, at each finite length and for infinite runs.
\item Every expanded coefficient of either polynomial has absolute value at
most six.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Parts (a) and (b) follow from the displayed formulas. In (a), each product
$c_e x_{i(e)}$ is nonnegative on the nonnegative orthant; the other terms are
squares.

Suppose $P_2(X,Y)=0$ at natural coordinates. Nonnegativity forces every
summand to vanish. In particular, $U(b)=U(c)=0$, so $b$ and $c$ select unique
edges, say $e$ and $f$. The vector
$(\sum_{\tgt(e')=q}b_{e'})_{q\in\bar Q}$ is the unit vector at $\tgt(e)$.
Similarly the outgoing-source vector defined by $c$ is the unit vector at
$\src(f)$. Thus all $V_q=0$ mean precisely
\[
 \tgt(e)=\src(f).
\]
The dummy edge cannot be selected as $f$: no incoming edge targets its source
$*$. The equations $W_i=0$ say $y=x+\delta_f$. If $f$ decrements counter $i$,
then $y_i\in\N$ forces $x_i\geq1$. If $f$ is a zero edge, its product term
becomes $x_{i(f)}$, forcing that counter to be zero. These are exactly the
edge's enabling conditions.

Conversely, an enabled edge gives one-hot $b,c$, matching source and target,
the correct update, and all required zero tests. Every summand vanishes.
Squaring a product does not alter its zero set, so the same equivalence holds
for $P_4$.

For a fixed valid $X$, each outgoing edge specifies all coordinates of $Y$:
the updated counters and the incoming-edge unit vector. There are at most two
such edges. If $X$ is invalid, $U(b)^2$ prevents any successor. The bijection
