of paths follows inductively, and the coordinatewise correspondence extends
to streams. The coefficient claim is established in the next subsection.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A coefficient audit independent of machine size}\label{lbh:ql:sec:height}
The height bound does not come from estimating the number of summands.
Instead, each type of monomial can occur in only a bounded number of places.
This distinction is essential because the machine may have many labels.

For distinct $e,f$, the possible coefficients are as follows:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Monomial & Expanded coefficient or bound\\
\midrule
$1$ & $2$\\
$b_e$, $c_e$ & $-2$\\
$b_e^2$ & $2$\\
$c_e^2$ & $2+\|\delta_e\|_2^2\leq3$\\
$b_e b_f$ & $2+2\1_{\tgt(e)=\tgt(f)}\leq4$\\
$c_e c_f$ & $2+2\1_{\src(e)=\src(f)}+2\delta_e\cdot\delta_f$\\
$b_e c_f$ & $-2\1_{\tgt(e)=\src(f)}$\\
$x_i^2$, $y_i^2$, $x_i y_i$ & $1$, $1$, $-2$\\
$x_i c_e$, $y_i c_e$ from updates & $2\delta_{e,i}$, $-2\delta_{e,i}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Since each effect is zero or a signed unit vector,
$|\delta_e\cdot\delta_f|\leq1$. The $c_ec_f$ coefficient therefore has
absolute value at most six. A zero-edge guard contributes $c_e x_{i(e)}$
to $P_2$, or $c_e^2x_{i(e)}^2$ to $P_4$, with coefficient one. Because a zero
edge has zero effect, the quadratic guard does not overlap a nonzero update
coefficient for that edge. All variables in the table belong to their indicated,
distinct coordinate blocks. This accounts for every monomial.

Two parallel-source increment edges acting on the same counter attain the
coefficient six. Thus six is sharp for this particular unweighted expansion;
it is not a universal lower bound for all possible encodings.

The residual representation has linear size in $m+d+|\bar Q|$. Each $b_e$
appears in one target residual and each $c_e$ in one source residual; a
nonzero effect appears in one update residual. Expanded polynomials can be
quadratic in the number of edges, so the residual form is preferable for
storage and proof checking.

\subsection{A geometric observation}
Homogenize $P_2$ with an additional variable $h$, replacing the constants in
$U(b)$ and $U(c)$ by $h$. Its homogeneous quadratic form is a sum of squares
of linear forms plus nonnegative coordinate products. Equivalently, its
symmetric matrix is a positive-semidefinite matrix plus an entrywise
nonnegative matrix. No difficult copositivity certification is being hidden:
the decomposition is supplied by the compiler.

\begin{proposition}[The one-step graph is semilinear]\label[proposition]{lbh:ql:prop:semilinear}
The relation $P_2(X,Y)=0$ over $\N$ is effectively a finite union of linear
sets, and hence is Presburger-definable.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix the finitely many possibilities for the selected incoming edge $e$ and
next edge $f$. Discard mismatched source/target pairs. The remaining case
fixes $b$ and $c$, requires $y=x+\delta_f$, and possibly sets a single
counter to zero or imposes its positive lower bound. Every unrestricted
counter can be chosen independently above a fixed lower bound; its increase
adds the same unit vector to the old and new counter blocks. This is a
linear set. Taking the finite union proves the assertion.
\end{proof}
Thus the non-arithmetical complexity to come is not present in one local
Diophantine relation. It arises from quantifying over coherent infinite paths
through repeated copies of that relation.
\section{Finite histories and what their certificates mean\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:finite}
For a fixed initial state $a$, write
\[
 A(b)=\sum_{\tgt(e)\in F}b_e.
\]
On valid natural states, $A(b)$ is exactly the marked-state indicator.
For $T\geq1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:finite-energy}
 H_T(X_1,\ldots,X_T)=
  \sum_{t=0}^{T-1}P_2(X_t,X_{t+1})+
  \bigl(A(b_T)-1\bigr)^2,
 \qquad X_0=a.
\end{equation}
\begin{corollary}[Exact finite-history certificates]\label[corollary]{lbh:ql:cor:finite}
The natural zeros of $H_T$ are in bijection with length-$T$ paths from $a$
ending at a marked state. The polynomial has degree at most two and exactly
$T(d+m)$ available state coordinates. It uses no transition auxiliaries.
There are at most $2^T$ zeros, and each counter on a zero is at most its
initial value plus $T$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every summand is nonnegative on natural coordinates. Vanishing is therefore
equivalent to every local transition being valid and the terminal marker
being one. Apply \cref{lbh:ql:thm:compiler}. The branching and counter bounds follow
from the machine semantics.
\end{proof}
For $T=0$, endpoint acceptance is tested directly on $a$. Terminal deadlocks
are allowed in a finite path; we have not padded halted computations with
self-loops.

A deterministic machine has at most one zero for each fixed input and fixed
$T$. This is \emph{not} a single-fold MRDP representation of unbounded
acceptance. The dimension in \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:finite-energy} grows with $T$, and
existentially choosing $T$ does not turn the family into a single fixed-arity
polynomial while preserving its witness multiplicity. The coefficient height
six is a one-step bound; summing time slices or substituting initial constants
can increase coefficient heights.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The next two subsections are the second half of source~11's section
``Compatible deadlines restore effective compactness''; its first half, the compactness theorem,
is printed as the third route to \cref{lbh:thm:compact}. The second subsection is a second route
to the observation after \cref{lbh:eq:schema}.}
\subsection{The explicit quadratic certificate family}
Let $T=g(K)$ and put $X_0=a$. Introduce $K$ natural slacks $s_1,\ldots,s_K$.
Define
\begin{align}\label{lbh:ql:eq:deadlinepoly}
 R_{g,K}(X_1,\ldots,X_T,s_1,\ldots,s_K)
  ={}&\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}P_2(X_t,X_{t+1})\\
  &+\sum_{j=1}^K
  \left(\sum_{t=0}^{g(j)}A(b_t)-j-s_j\right)^2.\notag
\end{align}
\begin{corollary}[Compatible quadratic deadline certificates]
\label[corollary]{lbh:ql:cor:deadlinepoly}
$W_g(K)$ holds exactly when $R_{g,K}$ has a natural zero. This polynomial has
degree at most two and $g(K)(d+m)+K$ available witness coordinates. Once the
finite path is fixed, every slack is uniquely determined.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every transition summand is nonnegative, so a zero first gives a valid path.
The remaining equations say that the number of marked positions up to $g(j)$
is at least $j$, with its excess recorded by $s_j$. This is exactly the
simultaneous deadline requirement. The converse and slack uniqueness follow
from the same equations.
\end{proof}
For \cref{lbh:ql:ex:budget}, take $g(k)=4k$. The first deadline is feasible: choose a
budget of one and visit $A$ at time four. The first two deadlines are not
simultaneously feasible. A budget of two first visits $A$ at time six, missing
the deadline at four, whereas a budget of one has no second visit. This
illustrates the extra compatibility missing in \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:wrong}.

\subsection{Arithmetical formulas and redundant witnesses}
For fixed computable $g$, $W_g(K)$ is a recursive predicate. MRDP can express
it by an existential polynomial certificate, giving a formula of the shape
$\forall K\,\exists w\,B_g(K,w)=0$. That display alone suggests a
$\Pi^0_2$ upper bound, but it is not sharp. The complement of $W_g$ is also
recursive. Applying MRDP to that complement gives a polynomial $D_g$ with
\[
 \forall K\;W_g(K)
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall K\,\forall w\;D_g(K,w)\neq0.
\]
This is consistent with the $\Pi^0_1$ classification. Representation
witnesses can obscure a simpler logical complexity; their presence does not
prove that an additional quantifier alternation is necessary.
\section{A fixed quadratic law with analytic-complete recurrence\srctag{11}}
\label{lbh:ql:sec:analytic}

\subsection{The classical source problem}
A tree on $\N$ is a prefix-closed set
$\T\subseteq\N^{<\N}$. Its path space is
\[
 [\T]=\{f\in\N^\N:\forall k\;f\pref k\in\T\}.
\]
The standard effective tree normal form gives a uniformly decidable family
of primitive-recursive trees $(\T_e)$ whose ill-foundedness index set
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:IF}
 \mathsf{IF}=\{e:[\T_e]\neq\varnothing\}
\end{equation}
is $\Sigma^1_1$-complete. This is the classical normal-form input to the
high-undecidability argument; it is not a new theorem of this report
\cite{harel1986}; see also \cite[Theorem 2.6(g)]{cenzer-marek-remmel}.

Here $\Sigma^1_1$ means existential quantification over one infinite natural
sequence followed by an arithmetical condition. This is different from
$\Sigma^0_1$, which means computable enumerability. The superscripts are not
interchangeable.

\subsection{Replacing infinitely many choices by binary delays}
Given $e$, a finite program performs the following stages:
\begin{lstlisting}
s := empty sequence
repeat:
    n := 0
    repeatedly choose either (n := n + 1) or STOP-CHOOSING
    append n to s
    decide whether s belongs to T_e
    if not, halt
    visit the marked checkpoint exactly once
\end{lstlisting}
The choice of $n$ is implemented by a binary branch. All computations between
a completed choice and the next checkpoint are deterministic and terminate,
because membership in $\T_e$ is decidable. There are no marked states in the
unbounded choosing loop.

\begin{lemma}[Binary recurrence realizes tree paths]\label[lemma]{lbh:ql:lem:tree}
The program has a recurrent run if and only if $[\T_e]\neq\varnothing$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An infinite branch $f$ determines finite choices $f(0),f(1),\ldots$. Every
test succeeds, so the induced run visits the checkpoint once at every stage.
Conversely, infinitely many checkpoint visits imply infinitely many completed
choices and successful tests. Their values form an infinite path through
$\T_e$. A run which chooses forever at some stage has no further checkpoint
visits and therefore is not recurrent.
\end{proof}
This last observation is the mechanism that turns an infinitely branching
tree problem into a finitely branching \emph{recurrent} path problem. Without
the recurrence requirement, the infinite choosing loop would be a spurious
positive instance.

\subsection{Uniformity and a fixed polynomial}
A single universal interpreter can read $e$ from its finite encoded input,
perform the preceding program, and expose one designated checkpoint. A
Turing-machine implementation can be converted to unit counters. For
completeness, \cref{lbh:ql:sec:planar} describes the two-stack route and the
three-counter arithmetic routines underlying it. Every internal routine
terminates at a simulated step boundary. Acceptance marks are attached only
to the designated checkpoints. This gives one fixed finite counter machine,
not one polynomial with infinitely many instructions.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-law analytic completeness]\label{lbh:ql:thm:fixed}
There exist a fixed integer $D$, a fixed polynomial
$P_2\in\Z[X_1,\ldots,X_D,Y_1,\ldots,Y_D]$, a fixed linear marker $A$, and
a total computable input map $a:\N\to\N^D$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\deg P_2\leq2$, $\htp(P_2)\leq6$, and $P_2\geq0$ on the
nonnegative real orthant;
\item every natural state has at most two successors under $P_2=0$;
\item the set of $n$ for which some run from $a(n)$ satisfies
$P_2(X_t,X_{t+1})=0$ for every $t$ and $A(X_t)=1$ infinitely often is
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\end{enumerate}
There is also a fixed globally nonnegative sum-of-squares $P_4$ with degree
at most four, height at most six, and exactly the same natural transition graph.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Compile the fixed universal checkpoint machine using
\cref{lbh:ql:thm:compiler}. The input map writes the code of $e$ into its prescribed
initial counter encoding and chooses the dummy incoming edge. By
\cref{lbh:ql:lem:tree}, this reduces \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:IF} to recurrent reachability.
Membership in $\Sigma^1_1$ follows by existentially quantifying the stream
$(X_t)_{t\in\N}$ and requiring the computable local equations together with
$\forall k\,\exists t\geq k\,[A(X_t)=1]$. The companion $P_4$ comes from the
same compilation.
\end{proof}
The fixed universal instruction table and its fully expanded polynomial are
not supplied in this package. The theorem proves their existence by a
specified finite compilation route; the implementation supplied here compiles
any explicitly supplied finite counter table. This distinction matters when
assessing how much of the construction is already executable.
\section{Exact quartic certificates for two-stack computations}\label{lbh:sec:compiler}

\subsection{A canonical natural coding}
A stack word is written top first, $w=d_1\cdots d_l$ with $d_i\in\{1,3\}$.
Use the natural sentinel code
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:stackcode}
 \nu(\epsilon)=1,\qquad \nu(dw)=4\nu(w)+d.
\end{equation}
For example,
\[
 \nu(1)=5,\quad \nu(3)=7,\quad\nu(13)=29,\quad\nu(31)=23.
\]
The base-four representation has a leading sentinel $1$; least significant
digits are successive top symbols. This avoids a collision between the
empty word and leading blank symbols. Push and pop are linear equations:
\[
 \text{push }d:\ n'=4n+d,
 \qquad
 \text{pop }d:\ n=4n'+d,\quad n'\ge1.
\]
A top-$d$ guard can be written, with a natural slack $u$, as
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:top-slack}
 n=4u+4+d,\qquad\text{so the popped stack is }u+1.
\end{equation}
An empty guard is $n=1$.

\subsection{Rule format and variables}
A finite machine has control labels in $\N$, two stack codes $x,y$, and a
finite labelled rule set $\rules$ of size $R$. A rule $r$ has source
$s_r$, target $t_r$, and one guard/update pair for each stack. Guards are
unrestricted, empty, top-$1$, or top-$3$. Updates are stay, push-$1$,
push-$3$, or pop; a pop requires an explicit top guard. The guards do not
need to be mutually exclusive. Distinct rules remain distinct choices.
Let $G$ be the total number of top guards and $E$ the total number of empty
guards, counted across all rule/stack pairs.

Fix a valid initial configuration $(q_0,x_0,y_0)$ and horizon $H$.
Let $\vec d=(d_1,\ldots,d_k)$ be strictly increasing deadlines with
$1\le d_1<\cdots<d_k\le H$. The initial configuration is substituted as
constants. Introduce the following natural variables:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.65\linewidth}r@{}}
\toprule
Coordinates & Number\\
\midrule
$q_t,x_t,y_t$, for $1\le t\le H$ & $3H$\\
Rule indicators $e_{t,r}$, for $0\le t<H$, $r\in\rules$ & $HR$\\
A top-guard slack $u_{t,r,a}$ for each top guard on stack $a$ & $HG$\\
Deadline surplus $w_j$, for $1\le j\le k$ & $k$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The total is
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:Ncount}
 N=H(3+R+G)+k.
\end{equation}
We now give all the polynomial equations; there is no hidden trace-coding
or existential validity predicate.

\subsection{The quadratic residuals}
For each step $t$, impose Booleanity and one-hot selection:
\begin{align}
 e_{t,r}(e_{t,r}-1)&=0 &&(r\in\rules),\label{lbh:eq:boolean}\\
 \sum_{r\in\rules}e_{t,r}-1&=0.\label{lbh:eq:onehot}
\end{align}
Impose source control and target control:
\begin{align}
 e_{t,r}(q_t-s_r)&=0 &&(r\in\rules),\label{lbh:eq:source}\\
 q_{t+1}-\sum_{r\in\rules}t_r e_{t,r}&=0.\label{lbh:eq:target}
\end{align}
For an empty guard on a stack value $n_t$, impose
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:empty}
 e_{t,r}(n_t-1)=0.
\end{equation}
For a top-$d$ guard with its own slack $u=u_{t,r,a}$, impose
\begin{align}
 e_{t,r}(n_t-4u-4-d)&=0,\label{lbh:eq:top}\\
 (1-e_{t,r})u&=0.\label{lbh:eq:inactive}
\end{align}
The second equation is not optional: it canonically sets the slack of an
inactive rule to zero. Without it, each inactive top guard would contribute
arbitrary spurious witnesses.

For every rule and every stack, impose one gated update residual, chosen
from
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:updates}
 \begin{array}{c|l}
 \text{update}&\text{residual}\\ \hline
 \text{stay}&e_{t,r}(n_{t+1}-n_t)\\
 \text{push }d&e_{t,r}(n_{t+1}-4n_t-d)\\
 \text{pop}&e_{t,r}(n_{t+1}-u_{t,r,a}-1).
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
Let $F$ be the accepting control labels. At the end of step $t$ the
acceptance indicator is
\[
 a_{t+1}=\sum_{r:t_r\in F}e_{t,r}.
\]
It is automatically either zero or one on a solution. For each deadline
$d_j$, impose
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:deadline-residual}
 \sum_{t=0}^{d_j-1}\ \sum_{r:t_r\in F}e_{t,r}-j-w_j=0.
\end{equation}
Because $w_j\in\N$, this is exactly the assertion of at least $j$ accepting
visits by that deadline; the surplus is uniquely determined.

All residuals have degree at most two. The step-wise residual counts are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
Constraint family & Residuals per step\\
\midrule
Boolean indicators and one-hot sum & $R+1$\\
Source guards and target equation & $R+1$\\
Empty guards & $E$\\
Top guards and inactive-slack equations & $2G$\\
Two stack updates for each rule & $2R$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Thus there are exactly
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:Mcount}
 M=H(4R+E+2G+2)+k
\end{equation}
listed residuals, including any residual that simplifies to the zero
polynomial for a special instance. Retaining those listed residuals is the
counting convention implemented by the prototype.

\subsection{The polynomial and the bijection theorem}
Let $L_1,\ldots,L_M$ denote the residuals above, and put
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:quartic}
 Q_{\mathcal M,H,\vec d}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}L_i^2.
\end{equation}
Here $\mathcal M$ denotes the machine; when no confusion is possible
we simply write $Q$. It has integer
coefficients and degree at most four, and is nonnegative on every real
assignment.

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-trace compiler]\label{lbh:thm:compiler}
For every finite two-stack machine, canonical initial stacks, horizon $H$,
and deadline vector $\vec d$, the natural zeros of $Q$ are in bijection
with rule-labelled legal runs of length $H$ satisfying all the deadlines.
The certificate has exactly the variable and listed-residual counts in
\cref{lbh:eq:Ncount,lbh:eq:Mcount}. In particular, a fixed legal rule-labelled run
has exactly one natural witness assignment when it satisfies the deadlines,
and no assignment otherwise.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Because all $L_i$ take integer values, $Q=0$ is equivalent to the vanishing
of every residual. The Boolean equations force every $e_{t,r}$ to be zero
or one, and the one-hot equation forces exactly one active rule per step.
The source residual for that rule forces $q_t=s_r$, and the target equation
forces $q_{t+1}=t_r$.

Induct on the step number, starting from the stipulated valid stack codes.
For an empty guard of the active rule, \cref{lbh:eq:empty} gives $n_t=1$.
For a top guard, \cref{lbh:eq:top} gives
$n_t=4(u+1)+d$ with $u+1\ge1$. As $n_t$ is a canonical stack code, its
least significant digit is precisely the tested top symbol, and its
canonical tail has code $u+1$. The appropriate update equation then
produces exactly the stack resulting from stay, push, or pop. Thus the
next stack code is canonical, completing the induction. No independent
regular-language constraints on intermediate stack codes are needed.

The deadline residuals assert the required inequalities because their
surpluses are natural. This constructs a legal deadline-respecting run
from every natural zero.

Conversely, given a legal labelled run, insert its actual configurations,
set the chosen indicator to one and all others to zero, set each active
top slack to the tail code minus one, set every inactive top slack to
zero, and set $w_j$ to the acceptance count by $d_j$ minus $j$. All variables
are natural and all residuals vanish.

Finally, the labelled run fixes all configurations and indicators. Active
top slacks are fixed by the quotient equation, inactive ones by
\cref{lbh:eq:inactive}, and deadline slacks by \cref{lbh:eq:deadline-residual}.
This proves uniqueness and hence the bijection. The counts follow from
the explicit inventory, and squaring quadratic residuals proves the degree
bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The boundary of the uniqueness claim]\label[remark]{lbh:rem:singlefold}
The dimension grows with $H$ and the number of deadlines. This is a
variable-size finite certificate, not a single fixed-arity single-fold
representation of arbitrary c.e. sets. Replacing all finite traces by a
fixed-arity MRDP polynomial can introduce additional witnesses; uniqueness
is not asserted to survive that replacement. In particular,
\cref{lbh:thm:compiler} does not resolve the finite-fold or single-fold
Diophantine representation questions.
\end{remark}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Batch~79, 2 October 2026: source~12's \cref{lbh:cs:thm:quadratic} (Part~VI) is a
degree-two form of \cref{lbh:thm:compiler}: the same bijection between natural zeros and
rule-labelled legal runs meeting all deadlines, with every auxiliary unique (inactive helpers
included), for binary stacks with six primitive operations, at total degree two instead of four. It
uses $3(T+1)+9mT+k$ coordinates for $m$ rules (more than $H(3+R+G)+k$), is nonnegative only where its
selectors and helpers are nonnegative, and its bijection is proved over $\N$ only. The comparison is
in \cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation}. The boundary of \cref{lbh:rem:singlefold} applies to it unchanged.}

\subsection{Deadline compactness as a quartic schema}
For a fixed computable deadline $d$, set $H=d(k)$ in the compiler, retaining
all $k$ deadlines. For the two-stack system we obtain the exact equivalence
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:schema}
 \Fixed_d(e)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall k\ge1\ \exists\vec z\in\N^{N(k)}:\ Q_{e,k}(\vec z)=0.
\end{equation}
The varying polynomial and dimension are generated effectively from $e,k$.
Equation \eqref{lbh:eq:schema} looks syntactically like a $\ppi2$ assertion if
one treats polynomial solvability as a black box. Its actual complexity is
$\ppi1$: the zeros correspond to a finite decidable set of schedules, and
that entire finite search can be carried out directly. Witness magnitude
is not a reason to ignore known operational bounds.

This is another useful distinction. A Diophantine certificate interface can
hide the fact that its particular instances are decidable. Complexity
classification should use the strongest available semantics, not only the
surface quantifier shape of one encoding.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11 makes the same observation for its counter certificates, applying
MRDP to the complement of $W_g$ (\cref{lbh:ql:sec:finite}, subsection ``Arithmetical formulas and
redundant witnesses'').}
\part{Transport, fairness and exact geometric realizations}\label{lbh:part:realizations}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Part~IV prints source~10's clock-transfer theorem with source~03's and
source~11's transport theorems, source~03's fairness reduction, source~10's compact
three-dimensional realization and source~11's planar realization.}
\section{Which simulations preserve a computable clock?}\label{lbh:sec:simulation}

A universality theorem can preserve finite halting while distorting temporal
properties. To transfer the preceding results to an unconventional substrate,
we therefore state a stronger, explicitly timed hypothesis.

\begin{definition}
A \emph{computable signal-faithful block simulation} maps each legal finite
source history to a target history, compatibly with prefixes. Each source
step expands to a nonempty, finite, effectively computable block. Every
infinite target execution from an encoded initial state decodes into such
blocks and projects to a source execution; there are no spurious infinite
internal executions. Accepting visits occur precisely at the ends of blocks
whose corresponding source step ends in an accepting state, with one target
visit per source visit. The projection of finite blocks is effective.
\end{definition}

The condition quantifies over all target executions, not merely those
chosen by a favorable simulator. It excludes, for example, an internal
nondeterministic loop that can generate infinitely many fake accepting
signals without simulating any further source step.

\begin{theorem}[Effective clock transfer]\label{lbh:thm:transfer}
For effective finitely branching source systems, a computable signal-faithful
block simulation preserves existential recurrence and the existence of a
computable deadline in both directions. It preserves bounded-gap recurrence
in both directions when block lengths have a uniform finite bound.
Without that bound, forward preservation of bounded gaps can fail.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The one-to-one correspondence of accepting signals proves the recurrence
assertion. For a fixed encoded input $e$ and source length $n$, enumerate
the finitely many legal histories of length at most $n$. Compute all of
their target expansions and let $g_e(n)$ be the maximum expansion length,
including zero for the empty history. This is a total computable,
nondecreasing function; the effective finite-branching hypothesis is used
exactly here.

If a source schedule meets a computable deadline $d$, its $k$th target pulse
occurs no later than $g_e(d(k))$. The strictly increasing computable function
$k+g_e(d(k))$ is therefore a target deadline. Conversely, each source step
uses at least one target step. On any target run with deadline $d$, the
$k$th source pulse occurs after at most $d(k)$ source steps, so the same
$d$ bounds the projected source run.

If each block has length at most $L$, a source gap bound $b$ becomes a
target gap bound $Lb$; the initial delay obeys the same estimate. A target
gap bound is also a source gap bound because blocks are nonempty.
For failure without a uniform upper bound, take a source that pulses every
step and expand its $n$th step into $n$ nonaccepting steps followed by one
pulse. This is a computable signal-faithful simulation, but the target gaps
are unbounded although its progress clock is computable.
\end{proof}

This theorem provides a practical contract for future translations to
rewriting systems, cellular automata, or reaction-style models. It is not
enough to cite their Turing completeness: one must prove finite effective
branching, total expansion of each block, absence of rogue infinite
executions, and the appropriate correspondence of acceptance signals.
A step-exact simulation is especially valuable, since it preserves a
specified deadline without modification.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~03's transport theorem is a second route to \cref{lbh:thm:transfer}
and extends it to computable runs ($\CP$, source~10's $\Comp$); source~11's terminating-macro
lemma (\cref{lbh:ql:lem:macros}, in \cref{lbh:ql:sec:planar}) is a third. Source~03's section
follows.}
\section{Transport to other effective computational substrates\srctag{03}}
\label{lbh:qd:sec:transport}

A useful compiler should preserve more than halting. The next statement
identifies a sufficient semantic interface that can be established for a
particular rewriting language, circuit formalism, or other effective model.
It avoids claiming that Turing completeness alone preserves an arbitrarily
chosen notion of recurrence.

\begin{definition}[Faithful finite-block simulation]
A simulation between marked effective systems is a faithful finite-block
simulation when each source edge is represented by a nonempty finite target
block; the block is computable from the source edge; instruction boundaries
are recognizable and effectively decodable; target paths from valid initial
encodings have no extra nondeterministic behaviors or infinite unfinished
blocks; and each source marked arrival produces exactly one target marked
arrival, with no other target marks. For every finite source history its
corresponding target history is therefore computable.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Preservation of the three recurrence notions]
\label{lbh:qd:thm:transport}
A faithful finite-block simulation of an effectively finitely branching
source system preserves and reflects $\REC$, $\CB$, and $\CP$. In
particular, each such source system with computable finite successor lists
and decidable marking admits an interpretation in the fixed quadratic
substrate with these three predicates preserved.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For unrestricted recurrence, concatenate the nonempty target blocks of an
infinite source path. The result is an infinite target path with precisely
the corresponding marked events. Conversely, a target recurrent path
crosses infinitely many blocks and decodes to an infinite source path,
because a single unfinished block cannot contain infinitely many marks or
support an infinite valid execution. Mark reflection gives recurrence.

A computable source path yields a computable target path by computing and
concatenating its finite blocks. To compute the target state at time $t$,
generate blocks until their total positive lengths exceed $t$; this
terminates. A computable target recurrent path yields a computable source
path by searching for successive recognizable boundaries and decoding them.
Thus $\CP$ is preserved and reflected.

For the less immediate forward direction of $\CB$, fix an initial source
state $c$ and a finite source horizon $T$. Its finite successor tree through
depth $T$ is computable and finite. Compute the target implementations of
all edges in these finitely many histories, then take the largest total
implemented duration of a history of length at most $T$. Including any
initial loading time gives a total computable bound $F(c,T)$. If a source
path has $k$th visit time at most $h(k)$, then the corresponding target path
has $k$th marked time at most $F(c,h(k))$, after monotonization if needed.
This is a computable deadline. Conversely, each source step consumes at least
one target step, so a target deadline also bounds the number of source
steps preceding the corresponding marked event. Thus $\CB$ is reflected.

For the final assertion, an interpreter computes the complete finite list of
source successors, then makes a bounded finite binary selection from that
list and computes the selected next state. Invalid selections can be
avoided by a finite binary decision tree. A zero-length list causes halting.
The algorithm for the list terminates by hypothesis, and the selection
procedure has a finite bound once the list is available; hence there is no
spurious infinite guess within a source step. After a chosen edge is
completed, the interpreter tests the decidable marker and emits exactly
when required. Standard finite coding and the counter implementation of
\Cref{lbh:qd:sec:universal} realize this interface inside $U$.
\end{proof}

The last assertion is under the promise that the supplied successor-list
and marker algorithms are total on valid source states. The interpreter
need not decide whether arbitrary program codes satisfy that promise.
For a particular substrate this totality and faithful boundary decoding
are the obligations to prove. A mere embedding of terminating computations
would not justify any of the three infinite-path conclusions.

This theorem also explains why a change to a slower conventional machine
model does not repair the noncomputable-deadline example. Effective finite
branching permits a computable worst-case time distortion over every fixed
source horizon. The absence of \emph{any} computable bound survives such
faithful changes of implementation.
\section{One weak-fairness obligation already reaches the analytic boundary\srctag{03}}
\label{lbh:qd:sec:fairness}

Fairness concerns the choices made along a path, not just the existence of
local transitions. We use the following precise convention. An action
$\mu$ is weakly fair on an infinite run if, whenever it is continuously
enabled from some point onward, it occurs infinitely often. Equivalently,
each suffix on which it remains continuously enabled contains a subsequent
occurrence. In the construction below $\mu$ is enabled at every live state,
so weak fairness simply requires infinitely many occurrences of $\mu$.
No fairness requirement is imposed on the other actions.

\begin{theorem}[Explicit one-obligation fairness reduction]
\label{lbh:qd:thm:fairness}
Given a marked counter network with at most two outgoing edges per control,
one can effectively construct a counter network with at most three outgoing
edges per control and one designated action $\mu$, enabled at every live
state, such that
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{the original has a recurrent infinite run}\\
 \Longleftrightarrow\\[-2pt]
 \text{the new network has a weakly }\mu\text{-fair infinite run}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The new network again has an exact degree-two, height-at-most-eight
polynomial transition relation. For a fixed universal instance, existence
of such a fair run is $\Sigma^1_1$-complete.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Replace every original control $q$ by two controls $(q,0)$ and $(q,1)$,
called unlocked and locked, and add a dead control $\bot$. Both copies
retain the original outgoing edges and counter updates, but every such edge
goes to the unlocked copy of its original target. Add one $\mu$ no-operation
edge at every live control as follows:
\[
 \begin{array}{rcll}
 (q,0)&\xrightarrow{\mu}&(q,1),&q\text{ marked},\\
 (q,0)&\xrightarrow{\mu}&\bot,&q\text{ not marked},\\
 (q,1)&\xrightarrow{\mu}&\bot,&\text{all }q.
 \end{array}
\]
There are no outgoing edges from $\bot$. Thus $\mu$ is enabled at every
live configuration, and each live control has at most two old edges plus
one new edge.

An infinite fair run cannot take an edge to $\bot$, so its infinitely many
$\mu$ occurrences must all be successful unlocked-to-locked transitions at
marked original controls. After such a transition, another $\mu$ would
reach $\bot$. At least one original edge must therefore occur before the
next successful $\mu$. Delete the $\mu$ steps and forget the lock bit. The
remaining sequence contains infinitely many original edges, is a valid
original run, and visits marked original controls infinitely often.

Conversely, given an original recurrent run, start unlocked and insert one
successful $\mu$ step at each of its marked visits before taking its next
original edge. That original edge unlocks the target. The resulting run is
infinite and takes $\mu$ infinitely often, hence is weakly fair. The lock is
what prevents a single accepting state from producing an artificial fair
self-loop without source progress.

The quadratic compiler applies unchanged to the augmented edge table.
Membership in $\Sigma^1_1$ follows by existentially quantifying an infinite
path and checking transitions and fairness arithmetically. Apply the
construction to $U$ and use \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:analytic} for hardness.
\end{proof}

This is a specific elementary realization of the classical link between
recurrence, trees, and fairness~\cite{harel1986}. It does not claim hardness
for simultaneous fairness of every original edge, nor does it retain the
binary outdegree bound: the stated bound is three.
\section{A compact rational piecewise-affine substrate}\label{lbh:sec:geometry}

\subsection{Finite stacks as rational points}
For a top-first finite word $w=d_1\cdots d_l$, set
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:rational-stack}
 \rho(w)=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{d_i}{4^i},\qquad\rho(\epsilon)=0.
\end{equation}
Every such point is rational and belongs to $[0,1)$. The stack operations
become rational affine maps:
\begin{align}
 \rho(dw)&=\frac{\rho(w)+d}{4},\label{lbh:eq:affine-push}\\
 \rho(w)&=4\rho(dw)-d.\label{lbh:eq:affine-pop}
\end{align}
For a valid nonempty stack, the top-$d$ guard is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:affine-top}
 \rho(w)\in[d/4,(d+1)/4),\qquad d\in\{1,3\}.
\end{equation}
The empty stack is characterized by $\rho(w)=0$. Strict upper endpoints
are intentional: no finite valid tail has coordinate $1$.

Choose distinct rational control levels $z_q\in[0,1]$, for example
$q/(s+1)$ for $s$ finite control labels numbered $0,\ldots,s-1$.
A configuration is represented by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:geometric-config}
 (\rho(w_L),\rho(w_R),z_q)\in[0,1]^3.
\end{equation}
Each rule has rational linear guards and an affine update on these three
coordinates. A finite union of these guarded graphs is a finitely branching
piecewise-affine transition relation. For a binary-controlled machine,
there are two guarded piecewise-affine functions $F_0,F_1$, one per action,
whose pieces are disjoint on valid configurations. The finite guard partition can be refined and completed by a rejecting
sink on its unused regions. This does not assert a finite-guard test for
the entire countable set of finite-stack encodings; noncanonical points
inside the chosen affine pieces may follow the same affine formulas.

\begin{theorem}[Step-exact compact geometric realization]\label{lbh:thm:geometry}
There is a fixed binary-controlled rational guarded piecewise-affine system
on $[0,1]^3$, a fixed accepting set given by rational control levels, and a
computable family of rational initial points, for which all the completeness
statements in \cref{lbh:thm:hierarchy} hold. The c.e.-degree spectrum realizations
of \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum} hold as well. For every finite horizon, the natural
quartic certificates of \cref{lbh:thm:compiler} certify its encoded trajectories
with exactly the same deadline and witness counts.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lbh:eq:rational-stack,lbh:eq:geometric-config} to the fixed two-stack
system constructed in \cref{lbh:sec:universal}. Equations
\eqref{lbh:eq:affine-push}--\eqref{lbh:eq:affine-top} show, by induction on the run,
that each rule and its affine piece have the same enabledness, resulting
configuration, and accepting label. The initial point is rational and
computable from the finite input, and every reachable encoded point is a
finite-stack point. Thus the simulation preserves every microstep,
acceptance count, and deadline. The hierarchy and the spectrum results
transfer without overhead. The quartic witnesses use $\nu(w)$, not
$\rho(w)$, to represent exactly the same finite words.
\end{proof}

The compactness of the ambient cube does not turn it into a finite-state
system. Stack denominators and numerators in their alternative natural
coding have no uniform bound. Nor is the guarded relation asserted to be
closed or the functions continuous. Rational interval guards and exact
real arithmetic are part of the mathematical semantics. None of these
statements asserts noise tolerance, experimentally realizable infinite
precision, or a physical solution of the halting problem.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Batch~79: source~12's \cref{lbh:cs:thm:affine} (Part~VI) is this theorem's coding,
digit for digit after $d=1+2b$, used to transfer the new spectra of Part~VI by the same step-exact
argument.}

\subsection{One deterministic map with the scheduler in the initial point}
The external control stream can instead be encoded in one more real
coordinate. For $f\in\Cantor$, define
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:eta}
 \eta(f)=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\frac{1+2f(t)}{4^{t+1}},
 \qquad C=\eta(\Cantor)\subseteq[1/3,1].
\end{equation}
This is a Cantor set with disjoint first-digit regions
\[
 f(0)=0:\quad\eta(f)\in[1/3,1/2],
 \qquad
 f(0)=1:\quad\eta(f)\in[5/6,1].
\]
The digit can therefore be read by rational interval guards. With
$S$ the left shift on schedules,
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:shift}
 \eta(Sf)=4\eta(f)-1-2f(0).
\end{equation}
Define a deterministic guarded rational piecewise-affine map on $[0,1]^4$
by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:autonomous}
 \mathcal F(X,z)=\bigl(F_b(X),\ 4z-1-2b\bigr)
 \quad\text{on the first-digit region for }b,
\end{equation}
with an arbitrary rejecting completion elsewhere. For a standard encoded
initial point $(X_e,\eta(f))$, its orbit is exactly the controlled run on
schedule $f$, with one bit consumed at every microstep, including steps
where the action is ignored.

The map $\eta$ preserves Turing degree under the usual effective
representation of reals: $f$ computes arbitrarily accurate rational
approximations to $\eta(f)$ by truncation, while the separated digit regions
allow recovery of each bit from increasingly accurate approximations to
the real. Also, $\eta(f)$ is rational exactly when $f$ is eventually
periodic. Indeed, rational base-four expansions are eventually periodic,
and conversely any eventual period yields a rational geometric series.
The endpoint $1$ is represented by the all-$3$ digit stream and causes no
ambiguity within this digit set.

\begin{theorem}[Initial-parameter hierarchy]\label{lbh:thm:initial}
For the fixed deterministic map $\mathcal F$ above, with standard finite
input $e$ determining $X_e$, the following classifications are sharp:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.70\linewidth}c@{}}
\toprule
Question about a recurrent orbit from $(X_e,z)$ & Complexity\\
\midrule
The scheduler coordinate $z\in C\cap\Q$ is specified as finite input & $\ppi2$\\
Some rational scheduler coordinate $z\in C\cap\Q$ works & $\sig3$\\
Some computable scheduler coordinate $z\in C$ works & $\sig3$\\
Some scheduler coordinate admits a computable progress deadline & $\sig3$\\
Some scheduler coordinate of HIF degree works & $\sig3$\\
Some unrestricted scheduler coordinate $z\in C$ works & $\an$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The existential computable-parameter condition is strictly stronger than
the existential computable-deadline condition, despite their equal index
complexity.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A specified rational scheduler coordinate has an effectively computable
orbit, giving the $\ppi2$ upper bound. A deterministic mode ignoring the
scheduler realizes the $\ppi2$ lower bound from
\cref{lbh:thm:deterministic}, even with the fixed value $z=1/3$.

Enumerate rational points of $C$ by finite descriptions of eventually
periodic digit streams. For a chosen description, recurrence is $\ppi2$;
existentially choosing the description gives $\sig3$. The $\sig3$ reduction
of \cref{lbh:sec:hierarchy} uses only one completed finite unary choice of $m$.
All later simulation steps are deterministic and may receive the constant
zero action. The finitely many nonzero action bits before completion of
the guess, followed by zeros forever, form an eventually periodic schedule.
Thus every positive instance has a rational scheduler coordinate, whereas
a negative instance has no accepting coordinate at all. This proves
$\sig3$ hardness for rational coordinates.

Computable coordinates correspond exactly to computable schedules, so their
classification is that of $\Comp$. Computable deadlines correspond to
$\Clock$, and the HIF condition is equivalent by \cref{lbh:thm:core} and the
degree equivalence under $\eta$. Unrestricted coordinates correspond to
arbitrary schedules, giving $\an$ completeness. The DNR construction
in \cref{lbh:prop:noncomputable-scheduler} separates computable schedules from
computable deadlines, and the step-exact coding preserves that separation.
\end{proof}

The quantified domain of the initial coordinate is indispensable.
``A deterministic dynamical system'' alone does not fix the problem's
complexity. A specified rational initial point, an existentially chosen
rational point, and an existentially chosen arbitrary real point encode
three different questions. The analytical result is obtained by allowing
an entire infinite schedule in the real initial condition, not by deriving
noncomputable information from a finite rational input under a deterministic
computable evolution.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11's planar realization follows. It packs the finite control into the
integer part of one coordinate, so its controlled relation lives in a planar strip where
source~10's lives in $[0,1]^3$; it proves the $\an$ row for a fixed system and the $\sig3$ rows
and separations across systems, where source~10's \cref{lbh:thm:geometry} carries every row,
the c.e.\ spectra and a deterministic map with the scheduler in a fourth coordinate. Its stack
code $\eta$ is source~10's $\rho$ after the digit relabelling $d=1+2a$, not source~10's scheduler
code $\eta$ (\cref{lbh:conv:dictionary}).}
\section{An unconventional realization: planar rational stack dynamics\srctag{11}}
\label{lbh:ql:sec:planar}

\subsection{Finite stacks as rational points}
The counter presentation is convenient for Diophantine equations, but the
same phenomena occur in a bounded planar phase space. Let a finite binary
stack be $w=a_1\cdots a_\ell$, with $a_1$ at the top. Encode it by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:stackcode}
 \eta(w)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\frac{1+2a_j}{4^j},
 \qquad\eta(\eps)=0.
\end{equation}
Every code is rational and lies in $[0,1)$. The encoding is injective: after
clearing the larger power-of-four denominator, distinct finite words with
digits one and three cannot differ only by trailing zero digits.

The empty stack is exactly zero. A nonempty stack with top zero has code in
$[1/4,1/2)$, while top one has code in $[3/4,1)$. On these cells, push and pop
are the affine operations
\begin{align}
 \mathsf{push}_a(u)&=\frac{1+2a+u}{4},\label{lbh:ql:eq:push}\\
 \mathsf{pop}_a(u)&=4u-(1+2a).\label{lbh:ql:eq:pop}
\end{align}
For valid finite codes these formulas are exact inverses. There is no numerical
rounding and no test for the equality of unspecified computable real numbers.

\subsection{Packing finite control into planar strips}
Number the control labels $q=0,\ldots,s-1$. Encode a two-stack configuration
$(q,L,R)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:ql:eq:planecode}
 (z,v)=(q+\eta(L),\eta(R))\in[0,s)\times[0,1).
\end{equation}
Writing $u=z-q$, the left-stack instructions become
\begin{align*}
 \mathsf{push}_a(L):&\quad
       (z,v)\longmapsto\left(q'+\frac{1+2a+z-q}{4},v\right),\\
 \mathsf{pop}_a(L):&\quad
       (z,v)\longmapsto\bigl(q'+4(z-q)-(1+2a),v\bigr).
\end{align*}
Right-stack instructions use \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:push} or \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:pop} on $v$ and
replace $z$ by $q'+z-q$. Empty tests use $u=0$ or $v=0$. Top-symbol tests use
the two half-open rational intervals above. Pure binary choices give two
possible target strips without changing the stack fractions.

These are finitely many rational affine maps on rational rectangular cells,
including degenerate cells for empty-stack tests. Pop tests have mutually
disjoint domains, so they do not increase the number of enabled choices.
The whole region can be rescaled into the unit square. Marking a control label
means marking its corresponding strip.

\begin{proposition}[Exact planar simulation]\label[proposition]{lbh:ql:prop:planarsim}
A finite binary-choice two-stack machine has a rational piecewise-affine
planar realization whose runs from encoded finite-stack inputs are in
bijection with its machine runs. Every iterate of an encoded input is again
an encoded finite-stack configuration. Marked visits are preserved exactly.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The top-symbol cells separate all valid nonempty codes, and zero separates the
empty code. Equations \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:push}--\eqref{lbh:ql:eq:pop} implement the stack
updates exactly. The integer strip records the unique next control label.
Induction proves invariance of valid finite codes and one-step correspondence;
iteration gives the path bijection.
\end{proof}
Invalid real or rational points can have additional behaviors. They are not
quantified over in the input problem. Universality is obtained from a
computable sequence of exact finite rational input codes, not from arbitrary
real inputs containing uncomputable information.

\subsection{Why this is Turing-complete, and how it reaches counters}
Two stacks represent a Turing tape: one contains the symbols left of the
head, nearest first, and the other contains the current symbol and those to
its right. A finite control holds a popped symbol while a move or rewrite is
performed. Empty stacks supply the blank symbol. Explicit blank symbols can
be stored when needed, so no infinite tape needs to be preloaded. Encoding a
fixed finite tape alphabet into binary blocks reduces the alphabet to two
symbols. Each Turing transition becomes a finite terminating stack routine.

For the counter implementation, one may instead encode each finite stack by
an integer in base three with digits one and two, keeping the top at the least
significant end:
\[
 N(a_1\cdots a_\ell)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(1+a_j)3^{j-1}.
\]
Push replaces $N$ by $3N+(1+a)$, and pop divides by three, with remainder
one or two. The two stack integers and one scratch counter suffice for the
following routines. To multiply by three, decrement the source to zero,
adding three units to the scratch counter on each pass, then transfer the
scratch back and add the fixed digit. To divide by three, decrement the source
while cycling a three-state remainder register and incrementing the scratch
on each complete group of three; transfer the quotient back and retain the
remainder in finite control. The other stack counter is untouched. Scratch
starts and ends at zero. All loops terminate on natural inputs.

This supplies the elementary finite compilation route used in
\cref{lbh:ql:sec:analytic}. The alphabet and control size may grow, and no small
universal state-count record is claimed. The underlying geometric perspective
is related to generalized shifts \cite{moore1991}; the formulas above are given
explicitly so that their boundary conventions can be checked independently.

\subsection{Deadlines survive terminating simulations}
\begin{lemma}[Computable-deadline preservation under finite macros]
\label[lemma]{lbh:ql:lem:macros}
Let the source and target have computable complete finite successor lists.
Suppose an exact effective simulation maps each source transition, after its
choice has been made, to a positive, finite, deterministic target routine.
Assume that macro boundaries are effectively recognizable, source
configurations are effectively recoverable there, and there is no additional
target behavior from encoded inputs. Mark only the boundary corresponding to
each marked source configuration, once per visit. If the routines terminate
for all reachable source configurations, then existence of recurrent runs,
computable recurrent runs, and recurrent runs with computable deadlines is
preserved in both directions.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Run correspondence and checkpoint preservation give recurrence. A computable
source run can be expanded by executing the routines. Conversely, from a
computable recurrent target run, successive completed checkpoints and their
source configurations can be found effectively; intermediate source steps
can also be recovered from the simulation boundaries. This gives a computable
source run. Infinite internal divergence cannot create an accepting target
run, since it produces no further marked checkpoints.

For a source deadline $g$, the source tree up to depth $g(k)$ is finite and
can be fully generated. Simulate all routines along all its paths and take
the maximum total target length. Every simulation in this finite collection
terminates, so the maximum is a total computable function of $k$. It bounds
the corresponding target checkpoint time. In the reverse direction, every
source transition uses at least one target transition, so source visit times
are bounded by target visit times, up to a fixed initialization convention
which can be absorbed into the bound.
\end{proof}
The lemma is existential rather than an efficiency theorem. It does not
assert a polynomial simulation-time bound.

\begin{corollary}[Planar recurrence and the witness hierarchy]
\label[corollary]{lbh:ql:cor:planar}
A fixed finite rational piecewise-affine relation in a bounded planar region,
with at most two successors on valid encoded states, has
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete recurrent reachability on an effectively encoded family
of finite rational inputs. Across such effectively presented systems, the
computable-run and computable-deadline problems are $\Sigma^0_3$-complete and
are strictly distinct. The separating examples of \cref{lbh:ql:sec:separations}
are realizable in this substrate.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use a fixed universal two-stack interpreter for the tree program and
\cref{lbh:ql:prop:planarsim} for the fixed-system assertion. For upper bounds, exact
rational states and their finite successor lists are effectively computable,
so the arguments of \cref{lbh:ql:thm:sigma3} apply. Hardness and the strict
separations transfer through the terminating stack and counter routines using
\cref{lbh:ql:lem:macros}.
\end{proof}
These maps are not asserted to be continuous across their cell boundaries,
noise-robust, volume-preserving, or injective. The result concerns exact
rational dynamics. Removing those qualifications would be a different
research problem.
\part{Fixed-arity signatures, no-go theorems, implementation, formalization and questions}\label{lbh:part:limits}
\srcnote{\wtag{} Part~V prints source~10's fixed-arity quantifier signatures and no-go theorem
with source~03's and source~11's finite-certificate obstructions, the three implementation and
verification sections, the three formalization plans, the merged research questions and the
three conclusions.}
\section{Fixed-arity Diophantine normal forms and impossibility results}\label{lbh:sec:mrdp}

\subsection{Parameter tuples and degree reduction}
The repository interface \cref{lbh:eq:mrdp} is stated with one free natural
input. It yields the usual finite-tuple form by a polynomial injection.
For example,
\[
 \langle u,v\rangle=(u+v)^2+u
\]
is injective on $\N^2$: for a fixed sum $s$, its values lie in
$[s^2,s^2+s]$, and these intervals are disjoint for different $s$.
A c.e. relation on pairs can be enumerated under this coding; substitute
the displayed polynomial into its MRDP representation. Iterate for larger
tuples. The projection of any natural polynomial zero set is c.e. in the
opposite direction, simply by enumerating natural witness tuples and
checking exact integer evaluation.

Whenever a fixed polynomial has degree higher than four, it can be replaced
by an existential quartic certificate. Split it as $P=P^+-P^-$ with
nonnegative integer coefficients. Compute the two parts by straight-line
circuits using natural additions and multiplications, introduce one natural
coordinate for each intermediate value, and impose residuals
$z-x-y$, $z-xy$, and the equality of the final outputs. Squaring and adding
the residuals produces a nonnegative quartic $Q$ with
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:eq:degree-reduction}
 P(\vec x)=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \exists\vec v\in\N^r:\ Q(\vec x,\vec v)=0.
\end{equation}
This is a degree normalization, not a small-variable claim. All circuit
intermediates are natural because positive and negative parts were separated
before evaluation. The extra coordinates belong to the same existential
block, or to a universal block after negating the zero-existence condition.

\subsection{The precise arithmetic signatures}
\begin{theorem}[Fixed-polynomial liveness signatures]\label{lbh:thm:polynomial-signatures}
For every fixed computable binary-controlled system and every fixed
computable deadline $d$, there exist fixed finite integer polynomials
$P_d,P_{\rm gap},P_{\rm clk}$ such that
\begin{align}
 \Fixed_d(e)&\iff\forall\vec y\in\N^{r_d}:\quad P_d(e,\vec y)\ne0,
       \label{lbh:eq:polyD}\\
 \Gap(e)&\iff\exists b\in\N\ \forall\vec y\in\N^{r_g}:\quad
               P_{\rm gap}(e,b,\vec y)\ne0,
       \label{lbh:eq:polyBG}\\
 \Clock(e)&\iff\exists m\in\N\ \forall k\in\N\ \exists\vec z\in\N^{r_c}:\quad
               P_{\rm clk}(e,m,k,\vec z)=0.
       \label{lbh:eq:polyCD}
\end{align}
The polynomials can be chosen nonnegative of degree at most four, with
additional fixed witness coordinates. No numerical fixed-arity bound is
claimed. For a deterministic system, recurrence also has a fixed polynomial
form $\forall k\exists\vec z\,P_{\rm det}(e,k,\vec z)=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For the first formula, the failure relation
\[
 \operatorname{Bad}_d(e)\iff\exists k:\neg\Fin(e,d\pref k)
\]
is c.e. Apply MRDP and negate its existential zero condition. This gives
\cref{lbh:eq:polyD} by \cref{lbh:thm:compact}.

For the second, let $\operatorname{BadGap}(e,b)$ mean that some finite
horizon has no $(b+1)$-admissible prefix. This is again c.e., uniformly in
$e,b$. Its MRDP polynomial has no natural zero precisely when a run meets
the gap bound $b+1$. Existentially quantify $b$.

For the third, the relation
\[
 V^*(e,m,k)\iff\exists s\ V(e,m,k,s)
\]
from \cref{lbh:eq:CDsigma3} is c.e. uniformly in all three parameters. Its
MRDP representation gives \cref{lbh:eq:polyCD}. Notice that the stage bound
$s$ and all computation witnesses can be included in the last existential
block; there is no need to assert that the program index $m$ is total by
an extra outside formula.

In a deterministic system, ``the first $k$ accepting visits occur'' is c.e.
as a relation on $e,k$, which proves the last assertion. Finally apply
\cref{lbh:eq:degree-reduction}. In a negated zero-existence condition,
$\neg\exists\vec y\,P=0$ becomes
$\forall\vec y\forall\vec v\,Q\ne0$, so the quantifier-block patterns
are preserved.
\end{proof}

These are theorem-level uses of MRDP. The accompanying program implements
only the explicit finite-horizon compiler, not the general extraction of
$P_d$, $P_{\rm gap}$, or $P_{\rm clk}$ from arbitrary program syntax.
The repository guide makes the same distinction for its existence interface
\cite{proveit-guide}.

\subsection{Why a stronger universal polynomial compiler cannot exist}
\begin{theorem}[Quantifier-sensitive no-go theorem]\label{lbh:thm:nogo}
For the fixed universal controlled system of \cref{lbh:thm:hierarchy}, none of
$\Fixed_d$, $\Gap$, $\Clock$, or $\Buchi$ has an ordinary existential
Diophantine representation as a set of finite inputs. Nor can there be a
total computable procedure mapping each input $e$ to a finite integer
polynomial whose natural solvability is equivalent to any one of these
properties. The unrestricted recurrence set $\Buchi$ is not definable by
any single first-order arithmetical formula, with any fixed finite number
of number-quantifier alternations.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Ordinary Diophantine sets are c.e.: enumerate the finite natural witness
tuples and evaluate the polynomial. The same argument applies to a
computably input-dependent polynomial with input-dependent finite arity:
compute that instance's finite syntax, then enumerate its witnesses.
But a $\ppi1$-complete set, a $\sig2$-complete set, and a
$\sig3$-complete set cannot be c.e., by strictness of the arithmetical
hierarchy. A $\an$-complete set cannot be c.e. either.

If a single first-order arithmetic formula defined the analytic-complete
set $\Buchi$, it would place that set at some finite level of the
arithmetical hierarchy. Every analytic set many-one reduces to $\Buchi$,
contradicting the existence of nonarithmetical analytic sets. This proves
the final assertion. The finite-horizon schema \cref{lbh:eq:schema} and the
alternating formulas \cref{lbh:eq:polyD,lbh:eq:polyBG,lbh:eq:polyCD} do not contradict
these conclusions: they keep the quantifiers that ordinary Diophantine
representation would remove.
\end{proof}

The theorem is not a prohibition on all useful arithmetic certificates.
A supplied successful finite prefix has a quartic certificate; failure of
a fixed deadline has an existential polynomial certificate; bounded-gap
existence has a two-block description; computable-clock existence has a
three-block description. The obstruction concerns the stronger assertion
that one existential finite integer witness can characterize every temporal
property of a Turing-complete substrate.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~03's obstruction is a second route to \cref{lbh:thm:nogo} for its fixed
quadratic graph (and extends it to computable runs); source~11's (\cref{lbh:ql:thm:nofinite}) is
a third, for recurrence. Source~03's section follows.}
\section{What finite Diophantine certificates can and cannot certify\srctag{03}}
\label{lbh:qd:sec:certificates}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-certificate obstruction]\label{lbh:qd:thm:nocert}
For the fixed universal quadratic system, no ordinary Diophantine
representation defines $\REC$, $\CB$, or $\CP$. In the case of $\REC$,
no first-order arithmetic formula with any fixed finite arrangement of
number quantifiers defines the set. More generally, there is no sound and
complete computably checkable finite positive certificate system for any
of these three predicates.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
An ordinary Diophantine set is computably enumerable. A sound and complete
computably checkable finite certificate system also makes the accepted
inputs computably enumerable: enumerate all input--certificate pairs and
run the checker. \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:cb,lbh:qd:thm:cp} give
$\Sigma^0_3$-completeness, and \Cref{lbh:qd:thm:analytic} gives
$\Sigma^1_1$-completeness. Hierarchy strictness shows none of these sets is
computably enumerable. The same strictness says that a
$\Sigma^1_1$-complete set is not arithmetic, proving the stronger assertion
for unrestricted recurrence.
\end{proof}

The obstruction is one of \emph{uniform completeness}. An individual run
can have an excellent finite proof, a particular program can have a simple
invariant, and a restricted class can admit complete certificates. None of
these facts conflicts with the theorem. For $\CB$ and $\CP$, arithmetic
formulas with three alternating number-quantifier blocks do exist; it is
only finite existential certification that fails in general.

\subsection{A useful positive fragment: finite recurrent regions}

\begin{proposition}[Finite-region lasso certificate]\label[proposition]{lbh:qd:prop:lasso}
Suppose a finite set $F$ of $N$ states contains an initial state and is closed
under successors. A recurrent infinite path exists if and only if there is
a path of length at most $N-1$ from the initial state to a marked state,
followed by a nonempty closed walk of length at most $N$ back to that marked
state. The finite path and closed walk form a checkable lasso certificate.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If a recurrent path exists, some marked state $a\in F$ occurs infinitely
often, since only finitely many states exist. There is a path from the
initial state to $a$; deleting loops gives one of length at most $N-1$.
There is also a positive return walk from $a$ to itself. A shortest such
return walk has no repeated internal vertices and has length at most $N$.
Conversely, follow the finite prefix and repeat the closed walk forever.
The marked state is visited infinitely often.
\end{proof}

For a known counter box, the state count includes controls and tags as well
as counter values; the starting zero tag should also be included if it is
not already in the selected set. The closure assumption is a separate
obligation, not something implied by observing a few bounded trajectories.
Each proposed finite lasso is checked using the local quadratic polynomial,
and endpoint equality can be added by affine squares. A variable-length
lasso predicate is computably enumerable and hence Diophantine via MRDP.

Lassos are not complete for unbounded counter systems. A single marked
control with the transition $x\mapsto x+1$ has a computable recurrent run
with deadline $h(k)=k$, but no state ever repeats. Thus even the existence
of a computable recurrent path need not have a periodic witness.

\subsection{No hidden conclusion about finite-fold MRDP}

The target tag makes the witness of an individual \emph{labelled local edge}
canonical, and a fixed finite path is checked exactly. This does not imply
that a fixed-variable Diophantine representation of arbitrary halting has
only finitely many witnesses, or only one witness. Passing from arbitrarily
long histories to a fixed finite tuple is a substantive additional
arithmetization, with its own witness behavior. No finite-fold or
single-fold MRDP conjecture is resolved here.
\section{Consequences for Diophantine representation\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:barriers}

\begin{theorem}[No complete finite existential compiler for recurrence]
\label{lbh:ql:thm:nofinite}
There is no total computable procedure which takes an arbitrary instance of
the recurrent reachability problem in \cref{lbh:ql:thm:fixed} and produces a finite
integer polynomial system whose natural solvability is equivalent to a
positive recurrence answer. This remains impossible when degree, coefficient
size, and the finite number of variables may depend arbitrarily on the input.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The natural solvability of any effectively produced finite integer polynomial
system is computably enumerable: enumerate tuples of the required finite
arity and evaluate the equations exactly. A total computable reduction of
recurrence to that property would make recurrence computably enumerable.
But the recurrence set is $\Sigma^1_1$-complete and not arithmetical, hence
not computably enumerable.
\end{proof}
In particular no one fixed MRDP-style existential equation represents that
recurrence set. The problem is not merely a shortage of available variables.
Adding a fixed finite number of alternating natural quantifier blocks also
cannot represent it, because that would give an arithmetical definition.
This is a sharp contrast with the c.e. finite-reachability relation in the
same quadratic graph.

The same reasoning shows that there is no sound, complete, effectively
enumerable finite-certificate system for all true recurrent instances. A
sound proof assistant can verify particular positive recurrence proofs, and
complete proof systems may exist for restricted classes. What fails is the
claim that all positive instances of the unrestricted problem have certificates
in one effectively enumerable complete scheme.

\subsection{A useful contract for future substrate compilers}
A compilation claim should specify the observation being preserved. In this
setting, the following are different outputs:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}lX@{}}
\toprule
Observation & Appropriate mathematical object\\
\midrule
One step & One witness-free low-degree polynomial relation\\
A fixed finite horizon & A finite quadratic history certificate\\
Some finite accepting horizon & An ordinary c.e./MRDP representation\\
An infinite safe run & A compatible family of finite-prefix tests\\
Recurrence with a supplied computable clock & Deadline-compatible certificates\\
Some computable-clock recurrent run & A $\Sigma^0_3$ condition\\
Arbitrary recurrent behavior & A genuine infinite-path quantifier\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
A compiler that proves only finite acceptance cannot be reused for recurrence
without an additional semantic theorem. Similarly, a finite collection of
successful horizon tests is evidence about those horizons, not a proof of
infinite recurrence.
\section{The executable certificate package}\label{lbh:sec:implementation}

\subsection{Implementation surface}
The archive includes a dependency-free Python module
\code{code/clock\_compiler.py}. Its sparse polynomial representation uses
integer coefficients and explicitly stored monomials; it provides exact
addition, multiplication, evaluation, and degree computation. Rational
stack tests use Python's exact \code{fractions.Fraction}, never a
floating-point approximation.

A machine is a finite tuple of \code{Rule} values with source and target
labels and two \code{StackOp} guard/update pairs. The module checks the
initial stack codes and rejects malformed guards, incompatible pop
operations, and invalid deadline vectors. Its public construction is
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
certificate = compile_trace(machine, horizon, deadlines)
witness = certificate.witness(rule_sequence)
assert witness is not None
assert certificate.evaluate(witness) == 0
\end{lstlisting}
The certificate stores the residual polynomials and their variable names.
An optional method expands their sum of squares into an ordinary quartic. Keeping the sum-of-squares representation normally avoids
unnecessary expression growth. Witness generation follows the canonical
assignment in the proof; it returns \code{None} for an illegal trace or
for a missed deadline.

This interface supplies and verifies witnesses for finite traces. It does
not contain a solver for arbitrary quartic equations and does not decide
any infinite recurrence property.

\subsection{A completely explicit example}
The sample machine has initial state $(q,x,y)=(0,1,1)$, accepting state
$2$, and five rules:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}cllll@{}}
\toprule
Rule & Source & Target & Left stack & Right stack\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0$ & $1$ & push $1$ & stay\\
$1$ & $0$ & $1$ & push $3$ & stay\\
$2$ & $1$ & $2$ & top-$1$, pop & stay\\
$3$ & $1$ & $2$ & top-$3$, pop & stay\\
$4$ & $2$ & $0$ & empty, stay & stay\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For $H=5$ and deadlines $(2,5)$, it has $R=5$, $G=2$, $E=1$, and $k=2$.
The compiler therefore gives
\[
 N=5(3+5+2)+2=52,\qquad
 M=5(20+1+4+2)+2=137.
\]
The rule sequence $(0,2,4,1,3)$ produces
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrc@{}}
\toprule
Time & $q$ & $x$ & $y$ & Accepting\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0$ & $1$ & $1$ & no\\
$1$ & $1$ & $5$ & $1$ & no\\
$2$ & $2$ & $1$ & $1$ & yes\\
$3$ & $0$ & $1$ & $1$ & no\\
$4$ & $1$ & $7$ & $1$ & no\\
$5$ & $2$ & $1$ & $1$ & yes\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Both deadline surpluses are zero, and every active top slack is zero
because the popped tail is empty with code one. The exported
\code{artifacts/example\_certificate.json} contains all 52 variable names,
all 137 residuals in sparse form and readable text, the rule sequence,
and the complete natural witness. Direct evaluation gives $Q=0$, and
expansion gives degree four.

\subsection{Executed tests and their limits}
The test script \code{code/test\_clock\_compiler.py} was executed using
Python 3.13.5. The resulting report is stored in
\code{artifacts/test\_report.json}. All tests passed. The principal checked
families are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.73\linewidth}r@{}}
\toprule
Finite check family & Count\\
\midrule
Stack words of length at most eight: exact natural-code round trips & 511\\
Exact affine push identities & 1,022\\
Exact affine pop identities & 510\\
Rule sequences for the sample machine, horizons zero through five & 3,906\\
Candidate assignments exhaustively evaluated in a local auxiliary test & 3,888\\
Guard/update cases exercising both stacks & 3,150\\
Single-coordinate increments of valid witnesses, all rejected & 496\\
Individual burst-prefix feasibility checks & 12\\
Coherent burst-prefix obstructions & 11\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The report also records residual/variable count assertions, direct and
expanded polynomial evaluation, degree checks, natural-domain rejection,
and invalid-input cases. Its total of 17,197 counts combines assertions
and enumerated candidate assignments; it should not be read as 17,197
independent infinite-system tests.

The exhaustive local test uses two legal, observationally identical stay
rules, one with a top guard and one without. It finds exactly two zeros,
one per rule label, with inactive slack zero. This tests a subtle part of
the claimed bijection. All full correctness and infinite-behavior statements
rest on the mathematical proofs, not on finite enumeration. The package
contains no executable universal interpreter table and no machine-checked
proof of the completeness reductions.
\section{Executed verification and proof audit\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:qd:sec:verification}

The accompanying Python implementation uses only the standard library.
It represents expanded polynomials exactly as integer coefficients indexed
by degree-at-most-two monomials. Its factored energy evaluator, sparse
polynomial evaluator, and direct operational successor function are separate
implementations. The verification program compares them and exercises
malformed controls, malformed selectors, illegal decrements, zero tests,
parallel labelled edges, and mutated valid target states.

The following results were obtained by running \texttt{python code/verify.py}
with Python 3.13.5 and random seed 20260930:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lr@{}}
\toprule
Exact finite check category & Number\\
\midrule
Local polynomial/transition equivalence checks & 302,498\\
Sparse-versus-factored polynomial evaluations & 27,652\\
Valid transitions explicitly checked & 5,152\\
Coefficient tables checked & 169\\
Finite-visit example paths checked & 41\\
Affine-fiber growth examples checked & 576\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All checks passed. The first count includes exhaustive tests of the sixteen
two-edge instruction-kind combinations on the documented small coordinate
ranges, plus random networks and targeted mutations. The examples include
the sharp coefficient-eight pair and the polynomial~\eqref{lbh:qd:eq:small}.

These tests do not establish a universal quantified theorem by themselves.
They provide independent finite checks against errors in the explicit
construction. In particular, no finite test proves $\Sigma^1_1$-completeness,
nonexistence of a computable bound, or absence of every computable branch.
Those assertions rely on the mathematical proofs above. No Lean or Rocq
execution was performed for this manuscript.

\subsection{The highest-risk proof points}

The local energy argument uses \emph{natural} coordinates, not arbitrary
integers. The global branching bound counts successor states, not witnesses
of an existential transition formula. The affine lower bound depends on a
\emph{uniform} finite bound, rather than mere finite branching. The deadline
compactness proof checks all elapsed deadlines, not just the final visit
count. The strict-separation examples test bounded computations at finite
stages, so their trees are decidable despite using undecidable halting facts
in the proofs of existence or nonexistence. Finally, the fairness reduction
requires its lock bit to rule out acceptance by stuttering.

Each of these distinctions appears explicitly in the statements, in the
code where applicable, and in the separate \texttt{PROOF\_AUDIT.md}. The
package also states the non-instantiated universal-interpreter boundary.
\section{Executable artifacts and verification results\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:ql:sec:verification}

\subsection{What is implemented}
The package contains a standard-library-only Python implementation of finite
unit-counter tables, a direct successor semantics, and an independent sparse
integer-polynomial compiler for \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:P2} and \eqref{lbh:ql:eq:P4}. It also
contains exact rational stack operations, finite-prefix exploration,
simultaneous deadline checking, and fully expanded JSON polynomials for the
budget counterexample.

The sparse representation stores an integer coefficient and a sorted tuple
of variable indices for every monomial. All integer and rational evaluations
are exact. There is no numerical solver, floating-point zero test, or hidden
computer-algebra oracle in these checks.

\subsection{The performed checks}
The reproducible run in \code{data/verification\_results.json} reports:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Check & Number\\
\midrule
All pairs in a complete natural-coordinate box for a guarded fixture & 59,049\\
Independent residual/expanded-polynomial comparisons on rational inputs & 1,000\\
Global quartic nonnegativity checks on signed integer inputs & 1,000\\
Generated finite machines checked for degree and coefficient bounds & 300\\
Exact finite-stack push/pop tests & 2,046\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
Every state coordinate of the exhaustive fixture ranges over $\{0,1,2\}$,
including malformed selectors. Thus the test does not assume one-hot encodings
when checking that non-encodings cannot create zero-energy transitions. Seven
state pairs in that box are genuine edges. The generated machines use a fixed
seed, and their largest observed expanded coefficient height is six. A
separate explicit two-increment fixture attains six.

For the budget example, the exploration checks horizons through 100. At
horizon $4k$ it finds a path with at least $k$ marks for $1\leq k\leq25$.
The simultaneous deadline tests for $g(k)=4k$ are true for $K=1$ and false for
$2\leq K\leq8$, exactly as the proof predicts.

These are finite regression tests of the compiler and examples. They do not
establish the infinite-run theorems, the tree normal form, or literature
priority. Those assertions rest on the mathematical arguments and the
explicitly cited classical inputs.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
\subsection{Reproduction}
From the extracted package directory, run
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The Python scripts require Python 3.10 or later and no third-party packages.
The LaTeX source uses standard packages and an embedded bibliography.
The first command regenerates the JSON results, the two example polynomials,
and the finite-prefix CSV. The runtime field in the result JSON is naturally
machine-dependent.
\section{Formalization and integration with ProveIt}\label{lbh:sec:formalization}

\subsection{A small trusted interface rather than an all-at-once port}
The natural integration point is a new liveness layer between finite trace
semantics and the repository's MRDP theorem. The following separation keeps
classical existence arguments away from executable certificate generation.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[node distance=9mm,
 box/.style={draw=ink!55,rounded corners=1mm,fill=wash,
 text width=10.7cm,align=center,inner sep=6pt,font=\small},
 arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},draw=ink}]
\node[box] (sem) {Two-stack operational semantics and canonical stack coding};
\node[box,below=of sem] (poly) {Finite trace $\longleftrightarrow$ unique natural zero of explicit quartic};
\node[box,below=of poly] (clock) {Coherent finite deadlines $\longleftrightarrow$ paths of a computable binary tree};
\node[box,below=of clock] (basis) {Nonempty effective closed subclass $\longleftrightarrow$ computable clock\par
HIF and low basis theorems: separate classical dependencies};
\node[box,below=of basis] (mrdp) {C.e. failure/prefix relations $\xrightarrow{\text{MRDP}}$
fixed-polynomial quantifier signatures};
\draw[arr] (sem)--(poly);
\draw[arr] (poly)--(clock);
\draw[arr] (clock)--(basis);
\draw[arr] (basis)--(mrdp);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

The arrows show a sensible implementation order, not that the MRDP theorem
logically needs the basis theorems. In fact, the arithmetic signatures can
be proved after the clock compactness theorem without any HIF construction.

\subsection{Suggested module boundaries}
A practical Lean development could use the following conceptual modules;
these are proposed names, not assertions that the files already exist.

\paragraph{\code{Liveness/Operational.lean}.}
Define an effective binary transition system, finite execution by recursion,
accepting counts, and the $k$th visit relation. Prove that a finite schedule
fixes every configuration and count. Keep totalized rejecting behavior
explicit rather than using an implicit convention for halted paths.

\paragraph{\code{Liveness/Deadlines.lean}.}
Define finite coherent deadline feasibility and its recursive binary tree.
Prove prefix closure and the exact path characterization. Establish
\cref{lbh:thm:compact} through a clearly identified compactness or K\"onig
lemma interface, rather than introducing an axiom named after the desired
liveness theorem.

\paragraph{\code{Liveness/TwoStackQuartic.lean}.}
Define finite rule syntax, canonical stack coding, the variable-index sum
type, and a polynomial generator. The critical theorem is an equivalence
between a labelled trace and the generated constraints, followed by
