\subsection{What Part VI does not claim}\label{lbh:cs:sec:nonclaims}
\wtag{} Collected from source~12's text, with nothing added: no fixed universal quadratic
Diophantine equation; no single-fold or finite-fold MRDP; no improvement of a fixed universal
polynomial's operation count; no numerically instantiated universal two-stack table (the seven-rule
fixture is not universal); no implementation of an oracle for a $\Delta^0_2$ set, of the
transfinite jump, or of an infinite-path solver; no complete classification of least clock degrees;
no uniform self-modulus from leastness; no continuity, noise tolerance or Hilbert's tenth problem
over $\Q$ for the affine substrate; no literature-wide priority (unique paths and moduli, jump
hierarchies, Friedberg--Muchnik and the modulus theorem are classical and credited); and no Lean or
Rocq verification. The finite Python checks test the compiler implementation and do not prove the
infinitary theorems. ProveIt's formal Hilbert's-tenth-problem project has formalized none of
Part~VI's statements.

\section{The question, the repository, and the contribution boundary\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:intro}

\subsection{Why clocks are a genuine extra resource}
Finite successful computations and infinite successful computations have
different kinds of witnesses. A finite computation is a finite string.
An infinite run that produces infinitely many designated events is a
function, and so is a schedule of deadlines for those events. Representing
finite prefixes by polynomial equations does not, by itself, say how much
information a single compatible infinite computation requires.

The present question is quantitative in a computability-theoretic sense:
which oracle degrees can supply enough time for one recurrent run to keep
making progress? This is subtler than asking which oracles can produce a
recurrent run. A bound on a path through an infinite tree can be easier
to compute than the path itself. Our proofs preserve that distinction.

\subsection{What was already available}
The inspected \repo{} manuscript \emph{Beyond Halting: Recurrence, Progress
Deadlines and Diophantine Liveness} already treats the relationship between
computable clocks, closed classes of accepting schedules, and
hyperimmune-free schedulers. It establishes clock cones above c.e.\ degrees,
and asks explicitly for spectra beyond those cones, including which degrees
can be least \cite{cs-repo-liveness}. We take its question
\code{lbh:q:spectra} as our research target. We do not claim its recurrence
hierarchy, its compactness argument, or its counter-machine quadratic
laws as new results.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The inspected manuscript is this report, Parts~I--V; its question is
\cref{lbh:q:spectra} and its c.e.\ clock cones are \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}. Source~12's citations
\cite{cs-repo-liveness} are kept as provenance; the pointers of \cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation} name the
labels they refer to.}

The source was inspected at revision
\begin{center}\small
\code{e58b724c25bd34533b7a5834cfcbe873dfa01288}.
\end{center}
Its path is
\begin{quote}\small
\path{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/hilbert-tenth-problem/}\allowbreak
\path{liveness-beyond-halting/article.tex}.
\end{quote}
The current Hilbert-tenth-problem README was also inspected.\footnote{\wtag{} On the default
branch on 2~October 2026, not frozen at the pin (source~12's source audit says so); the README at this
write still states the programme's fixed universal bounds of 75 and 87 operations, which source~12
neither uses nor improves.} It explicitly
distinguishes external-horizon certificates from its fixed universal
polynomial bounds \cite{cs-repo-readme}. No operation-count improvement to those
universal constructions is claimed here. Our proofs do not depend on their
numerical ledgers.

\subsection{What this paper actually proves}
The contribution has three layers. First, a tree-exploration compiler
identifies clock spectra with path-bounding spectra. Second, explicit tree
constructions extend the c.e.\ cone result to all limit-computable degrees
and to the jump hierarchies below. Third, a canonical quadratic compiler
realizes every finite progress obligation of the resulting two-stack
systems. The rational affine substrate is a step-for-step re-encoding of
that same computation, not a separate appeal to universality.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{0.23\textwidth}Xp{0.23\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Claim & Exact scope & Status here\\
\midrule
Tree bounding realization & All recursive Baire-space trees & Constructive proof\\
Limit-computable cones & Every $A\leq_T\emptyset'$ & Explicit singleton-tree proof\\
Higher cones & The effective jump hierarchies defined in \cref{lbh:cs:sec:jumps} & Explicit singleton-tree proof\\
Nonprincipal spectra & Unions of incomparable realized cones & Constructive proof\\
Necessary restriction & A least degree contains a self-modulus & Proof plus classical modulus theorem\\
Finite arithmetic & Degree two; natural domain; external $T$ & Proof and tested compiler\\
Universal numerical table & One fixed interpreter exists & Not instantiated in code\\
Full least-degree classification & Beyond the sufficient and necessary classes above & Left unresolved\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The classical relationship between unique-path trees and uniform moduli
is particularly important. It predates this paper; Gerdes reviews its
history and develops nonuniform self-moduli \cite{cs-gerdes}. We give the
short arguments needed for the machine application rather than rebrand
that relationship as a new result. Similarly, the jump-hierarchy examples
are a transparent realization of familiar computability-theoretic objects,
not a new hierarchy.

\section{Semantics and the tree-bounding realization\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:realization}

\subsection{Machines, ticks, and oracle spectra}
We use $\N=\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$. A machine has effectively encoded finite
configurations, a computably obtainable finite list of outgoing labelled
transitions from each configuration, and a decidable tick designation.
At most one tick is produced by a transition. Time is the number of
primitive transitions. A halted run is not extended by artificial ticks.
For a run $\rho$, let $N_\rho(t)$ be the number of ticks among its first
$t$ transitions.

\begin{definition}[Adequate deadline and clock spectrum]
A total function $d:\N_{>0}\to\N$ is an \emph{adequate deadline} for an
initialized machine $M$ if some infinite run $\rho$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:clock}
 N_\rho(d(k))\geq k\qquad(k\geq1).
\end{equation}
Write $\csClock_M$ for the set of all such $d$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:spec}
 \csSpec(M)=\{\csdegT(X):\text{some }d\in\csClock_M\text{ satisfies }d\leq_T X\}.
\end{equation}
For a degree $\mathbf a$, write
$\csCone{\mathbf a}=\{\mathbf x:\mathbf a\leq\mathbf x\}$.
\end{definition}

A deadline need not be monotone. Replacing it by
$\widehat d(k)=k+\max_{i\leq k}d(i)$ gives a strictly increasing adequate
deadline computable from it. Thus allowing arbitrary total deadlines does
not enlarge the degree spectrum. Pointwise enlarging an adequate deadline
always preserves adequacy.

A tree $T\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ is prefix closed. Throughout, \emph{recursive}
means that membership in $T$ is decidable by a total algorithm. Its path
set is $[T]=\{f:\forall n\ f\pref n\in T\}$. A bound $b\in\N^\N$ is
pointwise: $f\leq b$ means $f(n)\leq b(n)$ for every $n$.

\begin{definition}[Bounding spectrum]
For a recursive tree $T$, put
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:bspec}
 \csBS(T)=\{\csdegT(X):\exists b\leq_T X\ \exists f\in[T]\ (f\leq b)\}.
\end{equation}
The bound, but not necessarily the path, is computed by $X$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{The explorer}
Given a program deciding $T$, construct the following machine $E_T$.
It stores a finite tuple $\sigma$, initially empty. At the next stage it
guesses a number $n$ in unary: a binary choice either increments a counter
and continues, or stops. Each increment costs at least one primitive
transition. It appends $n$ to $\sigma$, runs the decision procedure for
$\sigma\in T$, and halts on rejection. On acceptance it emits exactly one
tick and starts the next stage. There are no ticks inside a guessing loop
or a membership computation.

This is an effective finitely branching machine. It can be implemented
by two binary stacks; a concrete primitive rule format appears in
\cref{lbh:cs:sec:quadratic}. The compiler uses the membership program as data.
No algorithm is asserted to recognize whether a supplied program is total
or whether its accepted set is a tree. Those are semantic hypotheses of
the theorem.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The explorer is the recurrent machine of the paragraph after
\cref{lbh:lem:analytic-tree}, which guesses the next entry in unary, checks the tree and pulses on
success, and the tree-verification interpreter of the proof of \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}; it is
printed as source~12 states it, with no novelty claimed.]}

\begin{theorem}[Tree-bounding realization]\label{lbh:cs:thm:tree-realization}
Uniformly from a total decision procedure for a recursive tree $T$, the
explorer $E_T$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:realization}
 \csSpec(E_T)=\csBS(T).
\end{equation}
More explicitly, an adequate deadline uniformly gives a bound on some
path, and a bound on some path uniformly gives an adequate deadline.
The latter transformation does not require computing a path.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every recurrent run completes infinitely many guesses and membership
checks. Its successive guesses form a path $f\in[T]$. Conversely, every
$f\in[T]$ determines a recurrent run: make exactly its prescribed guesses
and execute the deterministic checks. An infinite unfinished guess or
check produces only finitely many ticks and is irrelevant.

Suppose $d$ is adequate and let $f$ be the path selected by its witnessing
run. Before tick $n+1$, the machine spent at least $f(n)$ transitions on
the $n$th unary guess. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:clock-bound}
 f(n)\leq d(n+1).
\end{equation}
The function $b(n)=d(n+1)$ is a bound computable from $d$.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The same step is $g(i)\le\tau_f(i+1)\le d(i+1)$ in the proof of
\cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}.]}

Conversely, suppose $b$ bounds at least one path. To compute a deadline
$d_b(k)$, enumerate the finitely many tuples
\[
 \sigma\in\prod_{n<k}\{0,\ldots,b(n)\}.
\]
For each tuple simulate the explorer with those guesses until it either
rejects or produces its $k$th tick. Each simulation terminates: all guesses
are finite and every membership test is total. Take the maximum of these
finite runtimes, increasing it to at least $k$ if necessary. This is a
computation from the finite oracle segment $b\pref k$.

Any path bounded by $b$ has its $k$-prefix among the tuples just simulated.
The corresponding run therefore produces its $k$th tick by $d_b(k)$.
The \emph{same} path works for every $k$. This proves adequacy and the
claimed uniform transformations.
\end{proof}

The finite maximum in this proof is essential. Substituting ``choose a
bounded path'' would make the proof circular: obtaining a bound is in
general strictly weaker than selecting an infinite branch.

\subsection{One fixed two-stack system}

\begin{proposition}[Uniform interpreter]\label[proposition]{lbh:cs:prop:fixed}
There is one fixed finitely branching binary two-stack machine $U$ and
an effective map taking a tree-program index $e$ to a finite input $x_e$
such that the conclusion of \cref{lbh:cs:thm:tree-realization} holds for
$U(x_e)$ whenever $e$ decides a recursive tree.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix a universal tape-machine interpreter for finite programs, with an
explicit binary-choice instruction and a distinguished tick instruction.
Implement the explorer program as a finite program for this interpreter.
Its input contains $e$ and an initially empty tuple.

A tape configuration is represented by the tape contents on either side
of the head, with the nearest cells at the tops of two stacks; the scanned
symbol and finite control are held in the control state. Encode every
symbol of the fixed tape alphabet by a fixed-length binary block.
A tape step is implemented by a finite sequence of bit pushes, bit pops,
and empty tests. Empty tape regions are interpreted as blanks. The
interpreter's finite alphabet and instruction set are fixed, so the
resulting two-stack transition table is fixed.

Use dedicated control states to emit one tick, and only one, for each
explorer tick. All decoding, guessing, and interpreter steps are unticked.
A completed unary increment consumes at least one target transition.
The lower bound \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:clock-bound} survives. For the reverse direction,
perform the finite simulations in the proof of
\cref{lbh:cs:thm:tree-realization} using this actual interpreter's primitive
transition count. They remain total. Hence the same spectrum equality
holds, including all interpretation overhead.
\end{proof}

This is an effective existence construction, not a small-universal-machine
claim. The package implements certificates for supplied finite tables; it
does not include an instantiated transition table for $U$.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [\Cref{lbh:cs:prop:fixed} is a second route to \cref{lbh:sec:universal}, by the
same argument: the tape on two stacks, a fixed block code, designated pulse steps, no
small-universal-machine claim.]}

\section{The structural class of clock problems\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:closed}

\subsection{Adequate clocks form an upward effectively closed class}
A subset of Baire space is \emph{effectively closed} if it is the path set
of a recursive tree. Equivalently its complement is an effectively
enumerable union of basic open cylinders. A class is \emph{upward} here
in the \emph{pointwise function order}, not merely in Turing degrees.

\begin{theorem}[Closed-class normal form]\label{lbh:cs:thm:closed}
For every initialized effective finitely branching machine $M$, the set
$\csClock_M$ is upward and effectively closed. Conversely, for every upward
effectively closed class $C\subseteq\N^\N$, there is an explorer whose
clock problem and the problem of producing a member of $C$ are uniformly
computably equivalent. Thus clock problems are, up to these uniform
transformations, exactly upward effectively closed mass problems.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a finite tuple $\delta=(d(1),\ldots,d(k))$, set
\[
 H_k=\max\bigl(\{k\}\cup\{d(i):1\leq i\leq k\}\bigr).
\]
Let $\operatorname{Good}_M(\delta)$ mean that some legal length-$H_k$ run
satisfies all $k$ deadline conditions in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:clock}. This predicate
is decidable: effectively enumerate the finite tree of all runs through
that horizon and check the finitely many counts. If it holds for
$\delta$, it holds for every initial subtuple by truncating the witnessing
run. Hence the good tuples form a recursive tree.

An adequate $d$ has every prefix good. Conversely, suppose every prefix
is good. Consider the tree whose length-$t$ nodes are legal runs satisfying every
requirement with index $i\leq t$ and deadline $d(i)\leq t$. It is finitely
branching and prefix closed. It has a node at every length $t$: take a
witness for $\operatorname{Good}_M(d\pref t)$ and truncate it to time $t$.
K\"onig's lemma gives an infinite path. Its prefix at any time
$t\geq\max(i,d(i))$ checks the $i$th requirement, so the resulting run
satisfies every deadline.
This proves effective closedness. Upwardness follows directly from the
monotonicity of $N_\rho(t)$.
\par\wtag{} [The compactness half just proved is \cref{lbh:thm:compact}, which holds for every
deadline, computable or not; $\operatorname{Good}_M$ is source~10's $\Fin$ of
\cref{lbh:def:deadline} for runs of a finitely branching machine. New here is the reading of the
adequate deadlines as an upward effectively closed class of Baire space and the converse normal form
below.]\par

For the converse write $C=[T]$ for a recursive tree. An arbitrary effective
closed-set presentation can be put in this form by delaying enumerated
exclusions to the length of the node being checked. Apply
\cref{lbh:cs:thm:tree-realization}. An adequate $d$ computes $b(n)=d(n+1)$, which
bounds a path $f\in C$. Upwardness gives $b\in C$. Conversely, a member
$h\in C$ is a bound on itself, so the finite-maximum algorithm produces
an adequate $d_h$. Both maps are single oracle algorithms on their stated
domains. The case $C=\varnothing$ is vacuous on both sides.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Meaning of ``equivalent'']
The two transformations need not be inverses and need not preserve a
specific point of either class. They solve one mass problem uniformly
from a solution to the other, the usual notion of Medvedev equivalence.
Their induced degree spectra are equal. This does not assert that every
upward collection of Turing degrees has an effective closed presentation.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Why earlier deadlines cannot be discarded}
The predicate $\operatorname{Good}_M$ checks \emph{all} earlier deadlines,
not only the last one. This is a semantic requirement, not an accounting
choice. A machine can first guess an arbitrary $n$, then emit exactly $n$
ticks and halt. It has a finite run with $k$ ticks for each $k$, but no
recurrent run. Those finite witnesses can use different initial guesses.
A common deadline on the first tick bounds the initial unary guess and
prevents this escape. This compactness distinction is already central
in the prior report \cite{cs-repo-liveness}; our normal form uses it rather
than treating isolated finite successes as compatible witnesses.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [In this report: \cref{lbh:sec:traps} and the counterexample machines of Part~I,
three independent proofs that finite horizons checked separately do not suffice.]}

\section{Unique paths and all limit-computable clock cones\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:delta}

\subsection{Recovering a unique path from a bound}

\begin{lemma}[Unique bounded-path recovery]\label[lemma]{lbh:cs:lem:unique}
If a recursive tree $T\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ has exactly one path $f$, then
every total $b\geq f$ computes $f$, uniformly from $b$ and an index for $T$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Restrict to
\[
 T_b=\{\sigma\in T:\forall i<|\sigma|\ \sigma(i)\leq b(i)\}.
\]
This is a $b$-computable, $b$-bounded finitely branching tree with unique
path $f$. To determine $f\pref n$, search levels $h\geq n$ until all nodes
at level $h$ have the same prefix of length $n$. Each level is a finite
effectively searchable product. It is nonempty because $f\pref h\in T_b$.

The search terminates. There are finitely many length-$n$ nodes. Each node
other than $f\pref n$ has no infinite extension, so its finitely branching
subtree has finite height by K\"onig's lemma. Beyond the maximum of these
finitely many heights, only the true prefix remains. Output it.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Singleton-tree cones]\label[corollary]{lbh:cs:cor:singleton}
If $[T]=\{f\}$ for a recursive tree, then
\[
 \csSpec(E_T)=\csCone{\csdegT(f)}.
\]
The same equality holds for the corresponding input of the fixed machine
$U$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
A deadline computes a bound on $f$, hence computes $f$ by
\cref{lbh:cs:lem:unique}. Conversely $f$ computes a bound on itself and thus a
deadline by \cref{lbh:cs:thm:tree-realization}.
\end{proof}

The recovery lemma is the familiar unique-path/uniform-modulus argument
\cite{cs-gerdes}. The application is that a \emph{clock}, even without a
scheduler, is forced into exactly the desired degree cone.

\subsection{The first-matching-stage construction}
Let $a:\N\times\N\to\B$ be total computable, and assume
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:approx}
 \lim_{s\to\infty}a(s,j)=A(j)\qquad(j\in\N).
\end{equation}
Write $a_s\pref n=(a(s,0),\ldots,a(s,n-1))$. The limit lemma identifies
sets with such approximations as precisely the sets computable from
$\emptyset'$ \cite{cs-shoenfield}. Only the displayed approximation hypothesis
is used in the proof below.

\begin{theorem}[First matches give a recursive singleton]\label{lbh:cs:thm:firstmatch}
For $n\geq1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:firstmatch}
 f_A(n-1)=\min\{s\geq n:a_s\pref n=A\pref n\}.
\end{equation}
Uniformly from $a$, one can construct a recursive tree $T_a$ with
$[T_a]=\{f_A\}$. Moreover $f_A\equiv_T A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A tuple $\sigma=(s_1,\ldots,s_k)$ belongs to $T_a$ exactly when the
following finite conditions hold. For each $1\leq n\leq k$, require
$s_n\geq n$. Put $w_n=a_{s_n}\pref n$. Require that these words are
nested, so $w_n=w_{n+1}\pref n$ whenever $n<k$. Finally require
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:minimalmatch}
 a_t\pref n\ne w_n\qquad(n\leq t<s_n).
\end{equation}
Every test is finite and computable. The conditions are inherited by
initial tuples, so $T_a$ is a recursive tree.

Convergence guarantees the existence of every minimum in
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:firstmatch}. Its chosen word is $A\pref n$; these words are
nested and each stage is minimal for its word. Hence every prefix of
$f_A$ belongs to $T_a$.

Now let $(s_n)_{n\geq1}$ be any infinite path. Its nested words determine
one binary sequence $B$. Fix a bit $j$ and choose a stage $s_0$ after which
$a(s,j)=A(j)$. For any $n>j$ with $n\geq s_0$, we have $s_n\geq n\geq s_0$,
so
\[
 B(j)=a(s_n,j)=A(j).
\]
Thus $B=A$. Condition \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:minimalmatch} now forces each $s_n$ to be
the least stage at least $n$ matching $A\pref n$, namely $f_A(n-1)$.
This proves uniqueness.

Given $A$ and the fixed approximation program, a finite search computes
the minimum in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:firstmatch}, so $f_A\leq_T A$. Conversely
\[
 A(j)=a\bigl(f_A(j),j\bigr),
\]
because coordinate $j$ certifies a word of length $j+1$. Hence
$A\leq_T f_A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[All limit-computable cones]\label[corollary]{lbh:cs:cor:delta}
For every $\Delta^0_2$ set $A$, there is an effectively specified finite
input $x_a$ for the fixed two-stack system $U$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:delta-cone}
 \csSpec(U(x_a))=\csCone{\csdegT(A)}.
\end{equation}
The construction is uniform in an index for a convergent computable
approximation to $A$, not in a decision procedure for convergence.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Combine \cref{lbh:cs:thm:firstmatch,lbh:cs:cor:singleton,lbh:cs:prop:fixed}.
\end{proof}
\srcnote{\wtag{} [\Cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum} is the c.e.\ case of \cref{lbh:cs:cor:delta}, for
source~10's own fixed system and by a different tree; see \cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation} for the
comparison and the scheduler clause.]}

This answers the specific obstacle raised in the inspected repository
question: moving beyond c.e.\ cones need not require an $A$-computable
\emph{settling-time} modulus for an arbitrary approximation to $A$.
First matching stages and nested consistency suffice.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The obstacle is the commentary after \cref{lbh:q:spectra}: an arbitrary limit
approximation need not have a convergence modulus computable from its limit. That remark stays true;
first matching stages avoid the need for one.]}

\begin{example}[A first match can occur before a later error]\label{lbh:cs:ex:early}
Take $A(j)=j\bmod2$. Define a computable approximation that flips bit $0$
at stages $3,4,5$, flips bit $1$ at stages $0,1,2$, and agrees with $A$
otherwise. Its first ten coordinates from \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:firstmatch} are
\[
 (1,6,6,6,6,6,7,8,9,10).
\]
The first coordinate is $1$, although bit $0$ becomes incorrect at stage
$3$. The code tests this example exactly. It is a finite illustration of
the distinction, not a substitute for the general proof.
\end{example}

The requirement $s_n\geq n$ cannot simply be omitted: without it, a path
could repeatedly reuse a bounded collection of early, incorrect stages.
Minimality is also substantive: without it the path need not be unique,
and its degree need not equal that of the limit.

\section{Higher cones by deferred oracle verification\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:jumps}

The previous construction applies to a limit-computable set without an
oracle. We now give a separate recursive-tree construction for iterated
jumps. This makes the extension beyond c.e.\ degrees particularly concrete:
it includes $\cszero''$ and $\cszero^{(\omega)}$. The underlying existence of
singleton degrees and moduli high in the hyperarithmetic hierarchy is
classical; compare \cite[Section~4]{cs-gerdes}. The purpose here is to expose
a checker suitable for the tree-exploration interface.

\subsection{A hierarchy along an effective well-order}
Fix a decidable set $D\subseteq\N$ and a decidable relation $<_L$ on $D$
which is a well-order. This well-foundedness is a hypothesis, not a
property the compiler decides. Fix an effective pairing bijection
$\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:\N^2\to\N$ and an enumeration of oracle machines.
For $a\in D$, define, by well-founded recursion,
\begin{align}
 H_{<a}(\langle b,n\rangle)
   &=\begin{cases}J_b(n),&b\in D\text{ and }b<_L a,\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
       \label{lbh:cs:eq:lowerjoin}\\
 J_a&=(H_{<a})',\qquad
 J_a(n)=\ind\{\Phi_n^{H_{<a}}(n)\text{ halts}\}.
       \label{lbh:cs:eq:jump}
\end{align}
Let $H_L(\langle a,n\rangle)=J_a(n)$ for $a\in D$, and let it be zero
otherwise. This specifies the representation of the hierarchy; no hidden
choice of a limit-stage notation is needed in the checker.

\begin{theorem}[A recursive singleton for the hierarchy]\label{lbh:cs:thm:jump-tree}
Uniformly from indices for the decidable presentation $(D,<_L)$, one can
construct a recursive tree $S_L\subseteq\N^{<\N}$ which, under the
well-order hypothesis, has a unique path $f_L$ satisfying
\[
 f_L\equiv_T H_L.
\]
Consequently $\csSpec(E_{S_L})=\csCone{\csdegT(H_L)}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use a fixed convention counting machine transitions, so every halting
computation has a unique first halting time $t\geq0$. The intended value
of coordinate $\langle a,n\rangle$ is zero when $a\notin D$ or when the
computation in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:jump} does not halt. Otherwise it is $t+1$.
Call this total function $f_L$.

Here is the recursive finite test. Given a tuple $\sigma$ of length
$\ell$, first require $\sigma(\langle a,n\rangle)=0$ for every available
coordinate with $a\notin D$. For a coordinate with $a\in D$, regard an
available lower oracle bit $J_b(m)$ as $1$ precisely when
$\sigma(\langle b,m\rangle)>0$. A query outside $D$ or not below $a$ is
answered $0$ by definition. A query to a lower coordinate not yet present
in $\sigma$ is \emph{unknown}.

If the coordinate claims a positive value $t+1$, simulate the indicated
oracle machine for at most $t$ steps, also inspecting the initial state
for a time-zero halt. If an unknown query is encountered, defer the check.
If the finite simulation can be completed with known answers, reject
unless its first halt is exactly at time $t$. In particular an earlier
halt or failure to halt by time $t$ rejects the claim.

If the coordinate claims zero, simulate for $\ell$ steps, stopping early
if a query is unknown. Reject only if a halt has been witnessed using
known answers. Accept the tuple if none of these finite tests rejects.
All simulations have explicit finite budgets, so this predicate is
recursive even when the relation $<_L$ supplied to it is not well-founded.

Rejection persists under extension. Every rejecting computation uses only
answers already present, and these answers cannot change. The zero-claim
time budget only increases. Thus accepted tuples form a prefix-closed
tree $S_L$.

The true function $f_L$ passes every test. Consider any infinite path $g$.
For a fixed coordinate, every finite oracle computation uses finitely many
queries. All its queried coordinates eventually occur in a long enough
prefix of $g$. Therefore a positive claim in $g$ must give the exact first
halting time relative to the lower-row bits determined by $g$. A zero
claim must describe genuine nonhalting relative to those bits: otherwise
a long enough prefix and time budget would detect a halt.

Use well-founded induction on $a$. If all rows below $a$ agree with the
intended hierarchy, the oracle for row $a$ is exactly $H_{<a}$. The preceding
paragraph then uniquely forces the zero or exact-time value of every
coordinate in row $a$. This also handles the least row, whose oracle is
identically zero. Hence $g=f_L$, proving uniqueness.

From $f_L$ we obtain every bit of $H_L$ by testing positivity. From $H_L$
we first decide whether a coordinate halts. For a positive answer we
simulate it, answering lower-row queries from $H_L$, until its first halt,
and output that time plus one. Thus $f_L\equiv_T H_L$. Apply
\cref{lbh:cs:cor:singleton} for the spectrum assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Examples and limits of the construction}
If $D$ has one element, $H_L$ has degree $\cszero'$. For an ordinary finite
order of length $r\geq1$, its last row has degree $\cszero^{(r)}$ and computes
all preceding rows, so
\[
 \csdegT(H_L)=\cszero^{(r)}.
\]
For $D=\N$ in its usual order, row $n$ has degree $\cszero^{(n+1)}$ and the
whole join has degree
\[
 \csdegT(H_L)=\csdegT\Bigl(\bigoplus_{n\in\N}\emptyset^{(n+1)}\Bigr)
 =\cszero^{(\omega)}.
\]
These degree computations follow immediately by induction from
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:lowerjoin}--\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:jump} with the fixed standard presentations.
In particular the two-row example already supplies a least clock degree
strictly above every c.e.\ degree.

For a general supplied computable well-order, \cref{lbh:cs:thm:jump-tree}
asserts exactly the degree of the hierarchy as defined above. It does not
require effectively recognizing an arbitrary presentation as a well-order,
computing its ordinal type, or building an ordinal-notation oracle into
the machine. The finite checker knows only membership in $D$, the order
comparison, and finite program simulations. The noncomputable hierarchy
is forced by consistency across an infinite path, not placed into the
finite machine description.

\begin{remark}[Why ``unknown'' is not zero]
Replacing a missing oracle coordinate by zero would be unsound. A
positive claim might be rejected because a temporarily zero answer caused
a spurious halt; or a negative claim might survive for the wrong reason.
Deferred checks avoid both errors. The finite verifier records only what
its current prefix actually determines. This is also why the tree is
prefix closed without needing a computable order-respecting enumeration
of all lower rows.
\end{remark}

\section{Nonprincipal spectra and the restriction on least degrees\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:nonprincipal}

\subsection{Effective disjoint sums}

\begin{theorem}[Union construction]\label{lbh:cs:thm:unions}
Let $(T_n)_{n\in\N}$ be a uniformly recursive family of trees. There is
an effective finitely branching machine $E$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:union}
 \csSpec(E)=\bigcup_{n\in\N}\csBS(T_n).
\end{equation}
The analogous statement holds for a finite family.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The machine first guesses an index $n$ in unary, without ticks, then
commits permanently to explorer $E_{T_n}$. A run that never commits has
no ticks. Every recurrent run therefore has one fixed committed index.

If $X$ computes an adequate deadline for this combined machine, take a
run witnessing it and its committed index $n$. Its later guesses are
bounded by the absolute deadlines just as in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:clock-bound}.
The same oracle thus computes a bound on a path through $T_n$, proving
membership in the right side of \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:union}. This implication is a
statement about degree membership: it does not claim a uniform algorithm
that extracts the correct component from the deadline.

Conversely, if $\csdegT(X)\in\csBS(T_n)$ for a particular $n$, hardcode that
finite index, choose it at the start, and use the finite-maximum deadline
construction for the selected explorer, adding the fixed initialization
overhead. This produces an $X$-computable adequate deadline for $E$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A spectrum with no least degree]\label[corollary]{lbh:cs:cor:nonprincipal}
Let $\mathbf a,\mathbf b$ be incomparable degrees of limit-computable
sets. There is a finite input of $U$ whose clock spectrum is
\[
 \csCone{\mathbf a}\ \cup\ \csCone{\mathbf b}.
\]
It has exactly two minimal degrees, $\mathbf a$ and $\mathbf b$, and no
least degree. Such incomparable degrees exist, already among c.e.\ degrees
by the Friedberg--Muchnik theorem \cite{cs-friedberg}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{lbh:cs:cor:delta,lbh:cs:thm:unions}. A least degree would belong to one of the
two cones and lie below the generator of the other, contradicting
incomparability. Every degree strictly above one generator is not minimal.
The fixed interpreter implements the disjoint-sum program as finite data.
\end{proof}

The same proof realizes the union of the cones above any given finite
antichain of singleton-tree degrees. The countable version requires
\emph{uniform} tree presentations; an arbitrary noneffective list of
realizable degrees is not an input to the theorem.

\subsection{A least degree must contain a self-modulus}

\begin{definition}[Modulus and self-modulus]
A function $f\in\N^\N$ is a modulus for $A$ if every total $g\geq f$
computes $A$. It is a \emph{self-modulus} if every total $g\geq f$ computes
$f$. Uniform self-moduli additionally require one fixed Turing functional
to recover $f$ from every such $g$. These definitions use pointwise
majorization; eventual-majorization variants have the same nonuniform
degree consequence after finitely many corrections.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Necessary condition for a least clock degree]\label{lbh:cs:thm:least}
If $\csSpec(M)$ is nonempty and has a least degree $\mathbf a$, then
$\mathbf a$ contains a self-modulus. In particular $\mathbf a$ is a
hyperarithmetic degree.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose an oracle $X$ of degree $\mathbf a$ and an adequate deadline
$d\leq_T X$. The function $d$ itself is an oracle computing an adequate
deadline. Hence $\csdegT(d)\in\csSpec(M)$. Leastness and $d\leq_T X$ imply
$\csdegT(d)=\mathbf a$.

Every $g\geq d$ is again adequate, so its degree belongs to the spectrum.
Leastness now gives $\csdegT(d)\leq\csdegT(g)$. Thus every pointwise majorant
of $d$ computes $d$: the function $d$, after the harmless index shift from
$\N_{>0}$ to $\N$, is a self-modulus.

The classical modulus theorem says that a function admits a modulus if
and only if it is hyperarithmetic. Apply it to $d$; a self-modulus is, in
particular, a modulus for itself. A modern proof and attribution are given
by Monin and Patey \cite[Corollary~2.2]{cs-monin-patey}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[A domination obstruction]\label[corollary]{lbh:cs:cor:hif}
No nonzero hyperimmune-free degree can be the least degree of a clock
spectrum. More generally, if every total function computed by $X$ is
pointwise dominated by a function computed by $Y$, then
\[
 \csdegT(X)\in\csSpec(M)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\csdegT(Y)\in\csSpec(M).
\]
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Dominate an $X$-computable adequate deadline by a $Y$-computable function.
Upwardness makes the latter adequate. For a hyperimmune-free degree,
every computed total function has a computable eventual bound; finitely
many exceptions can be corrected by fixed constants to give a computable
pointwise bound. Thus the computable degree would also belong to the
spectrum, precluding a nonzero least hyperimmune-free degree.
\end{proof}
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Compare \cref{lbh:thm:core}: a computable adequate deadline exists exactly when an
accepting schedule of hyperimmune-free degree exists; \cref{lbh:cs:cor:hif} is the complementary
statement that a nonzero hyperimmune-free degree is never the least clock degree.]}

Notice that \cref{lbh:cs:thm:least} produces only a \emph{nonuniform}
self-modulus. Leastness gives a Turing reduction for each majorant; it
does not provide one reduction that works for all majorants. The unique-path
construction, by contrast, has a uniform recovery algorithm. Gerdes's
work makes clear that uniform and nonuniform self-moduli must be
separated \cite{cs-gerdes}. We therefore do not silently upgrade the necessary
condition to the sufficient one.

\subsection{The precise remaining gap}
Let $\mathcal S$ be the degrees of unique paths through recursive
Baire-space trees, $\mathcal L$ the degrees occurring as a least clock
degree, and $\mathcal M$ the degrees containing a self-modulus. The proved
inclusions are
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:gap}
 \mathcal S\ \subseteq\ \mathcal L\ \subseteq\ \mathcal M
 \ \subseteq\ \{\text{hyperarithmetic degrees}\}.
\end{equation}
All limit-computable degrees and all the explicit jump-hierarchy degrees
in \cref{lbh:cs:sec:jumps} belong to the leftmost class by the constructions
above. We do not prove equality of the middle classes. The structural
normal form of \cref{lbh:cs:thm:closed} restates the missing part as a precise
effective closed-set problem, rather than as an unspecified search for
faster computations.

\section{A canonical quadratic two-stack compiler\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:quadratic}

\subsection{Primitive stack rules}
A binary stack is a finite bit word. Store the bits from bottom to top
after a leading sentinel $1$ in the binary expansion of $u+1$. Thus the
empty stack has code $u=0$. For $b\in\B$, a push has law
\[
 u'=2u+b+1,
\]
and a pop of bit $b$ has law
\[
 u=2u'+b+1.
\]
The latter automatically enforces nonemptiness and the correct top bit
when $u,u'\in\N$.

The six permitted operations are stay, empty test, push $0$, push $1$,
pop $0$, and pop $1$. Each has an affine residual $F_o(u,u')$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
Operation $o$ & Residual $F_o(u,u')$\\
\midrule
Stay & $u'-u$\\
Empty test and stay & $u+u'$\\
Push bit $b$ & $u'-2u-b-1$\\
Pop bit $b$ & $u-2u'-b-1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the empty test the equivalence uses nonnegativity: $u+u'=0$ forces
both codes to be zero.

A rule $r$ consists of a source state $\alpha_r$, a target state
$\beta_r$, and one operation on each stack. A configuration is
$c=(q,u,v)\in\N^3$. The rule is legal from $c$ to $c'$ exactly when four
affine residuals vanish:
\begin{align}
 D_{r,1}&=q-\alpha_r,&D_{r,2}&=q'-\beta_r,\label{lbh:cs:eq:res-state}\\
 D_{r,3}&=F_{o_r}(u,u'),&D_{r,4}&=F_{p_r}(v,v').\label{lbh:cs:eq:res-stack}
\end{align}
Accepting states form a fixed finite set $F$; a transition ticks when its
target state lies in $F$. Programs are arranged so that each intended
event visits a dedicated accepting state once. No initial tick is counted.

\subsection{The guarded-affine gadget}

\begin{lemma}[Unique natural lift of an affine guard]\label[lemma]{lbh:cs:lem:gadget}
For $D\in\Z$ and $s,a,b\in\N$, put
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:gadget}
 G(D,s,a,b)=(D-a+b)^2+ab+s(a+b).
\end{equation}
Then $G=0$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:gadget-zero}
 a=\max(D,0),\qquad b=\max(-D,0),\qquad sD=0.
\end{equation}
In particular the helpers $a,b$ are unique even when $s=0$.
When $D$ is an affine polynomial, $G$ has total degree at most two.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All three summands in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:gadget} are nonnegative on the stated
domain. Their vanishing gives $D=a-b$, $ab=0$, and $s(a+b)=0$.
The first two equations determine the positive and negative parts of $D$
uniquely. The last equation then says $s|D|=0$, equivalently $sD=0$.
The converse follows by substitution. Affineness of $D$ gives the degree
bound by inspection.
\end{proof}

This construction exchanges helper coordinates for a lower degree. The
more direct guard $sD^2$ is nonnegative on naturals and imposes the same
active residual, but has degree three. Our gadget retains a unique lift
rather than allowing arbitrary unused sign decompositions.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The gadget packs into one natural-domain term the canonical positive and negative
parts gated by a selector of Report~A's canonical branch activation
(\texttt{cdc:wf:lem:activation} in \path{canonical-diophantine-certificates}); it is not new as a
technique.]}

\subsection{The complete polynomial}
Fix an initialized machine with $m$ labelled rules, an external horizon
$T\geq0$, and deadlines $\delta=(d_1,\ldots,d_k)$ with $0\leq d_i\leq T$.
Use variables $c_t=(q_t,u_t,v_t)$ for $0\leq t\leq T$. For each time
$t<T$, use natural selectors $s_{t,r}$ for $0\leq r<m$. The equation
\[
 \sum_{r<m}s_{t,r}=1
\]
automatically makes them one-hot. No separate Boolean equations are needed.

For every $(t,r,j)$ with $j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$, introduce two natural helpers
$a_{t,r,j},b_{t,r,j}$. Let $D_{t,r,j}$ denote the residuals in
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:res-state}--\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:res-stack} at $(c_t,c_{t+1})$. Finally
introduce one natural slack $z_i$ per deadline and put
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:count}
 C_i=\sum_{t<d_i}\ \sum_{r:\beta_r\in F}s_{t,r}.
\end{equation}
For the fixed initial configuration $c^{\rm in}$, define
\begin{align}
 P_{M,T,\delta}={}&
 \sum_{j=1}^{3}(c_{0,j}-c^{\rm in}_j)^2
 +\sum_{t<T}\left(\sum_{r<m}s_{t,r}-1\right)^2\notag\\
 &+\sum_{t<T}\sum_{r<m}\sum_{j=1}^{4}
 G(D_{t,r,j},s_{t,r},a_{t,r,j},b_{t,r,j})\notag\\
 &+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(C_i-i-z_i)^2.
 \label{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly}
\end{align}
This is an explicit polynomial over $\Z$, not an appeal to MRDP.
All variables are quantified over $\N$.

\begin{theorem}[Quadratic labelled-trace bijection]\label{lbh:cs:thm:quadratic}
The natural zero set of \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly} is in bijection with the
legal length-$T$ labelled runs of $M$ satisfying
$N_\rho(d_i)\geq i$ for every $1\leq i\leq k$. The polynomial has total
degree at most two and exactly
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:count-vars}
 3(T+1)+9mT+k
\end{equation}
supplied natural coordinates in this construction. Every auxiliary
coordinate is uniquely determined by the labelled run.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Each summand in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly} is nonnegative over the natural
domain, so a zero forces every summand to vanish. The first group fixes
the initial configuration. The selector sum at each time selects exactly
one labelled rule. By \cref{lbh:cs:lem:gadget}, the four residuals of that rule
vanish; hence it is a legal transition. For inactive rules the gadget
still uniquely fixes their residuals' positive and negative parts.
The count $C_i$ is therefore the actual number of ticks by time $d_i$.
The last square gives $z_i=C_i-i\geq0$, exactly the deadline condition.

Conversely, a legal labelled run fixes its configurations and one-hot
selectors. For every residual, active or inactive, take its canonical
positive and negative parts as helpers. The active residuals are zero,
so every gadget vanishes. Set $z_i=N_\rho(d_i)-i$. These are natural for a
run satisfying the deadlines. All summands vanish, and every coordinate
has been fixed uniquely. Projection to the labelled run and this lift
are inverse maps.
\par\wtag{} [\Cref{lbh:cs:thm:quadratic} is a degree-two form of source~10's quartic
\cref{lbh:thm:compiler}, for a different but equivalent rule format and stack code, with more
coordinates; \cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation} compares the two precisely.]\par

There are $3(T+1)$ configuration coordinates, $mT$ selectors, $8mT$
helpers, and $k$ deadline slacks. Every residual is affine and every
count is linear, so all summands have degree at most two.
\end{proof}

Substituting the initial configuration rather than retaining its three
coordinates reduces the count by three. We retain them so that the
implementation has a uniform configuration array and a directly checkable
initial-state boundary.

\subsection{Finiteness, domains, and a cubic tradeoff}
For fixed data there are at most $m^T$ labelled traces, and a label sequence
has at most one execution. Thus the zero set is finite. More concretely,
any legal stack code obeys
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:stack-bound}
 u_t+2\leq 2^t(u_0+2),\qquad v_t+2\leq2^t(v_0+2).
\end{equation}
Indeed a push satisfies $u'+2\leq2(u+2)$, and the other operations do not
increase the code. State values are bounded by the largest state numeral
or the initial state; selectors are Boolean; deadline slacks are at most
$T$; and the sign helpers are bounded by the absolute affine residuals of
these bounded coordinates. These are effective finite-height bounds for
each fixed horizon, not bounds uniform in an unbounded computation.

The domain restriction is indispensable. Over signed integers $ab$ and
$s(a+b)$ need not be nonnegative, and a row of selectors summing to one
need not be one-hot. The theorem does not assert the same zero set over
$\Z$ or $\Q$. Replacing natural coordinates by four-square sums would
change both degree and fiber multiplicities.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The programme's review (\cref{lbh:cs:sec:review}) gives two examples. With a signed
helper, $(D,s,a,b)=(1,1,1,-1)$ gives $G=(1-1-1)^2+1\cdot(-1)+1\cdot0=0$ although the selected
residual is $D=1\neq0$. Over $\Q_{\ge0}$, when two identical stay rules are enabled at a step, the
selectors $\tfrac12,\tfrac12$ on those two rules (with zero helpers) give a zero that is no labelled
run: the natural-domain trace bijection does not extend to nonnegative rationals.}

There is also a smaller-coordinate cubic variant. Replace every gadget
in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly} by $s_{t,r}D_{t,r,j}^2$ and omit all $a,b$
variables. The same argument on naturals gives a labelled-trace bijection
with $3(T+1)+mT+k$ coordinates, now of degree at most three. This variant
shows exactly what is being traded: the quadratic version pays $8mT$
additional canonical coordinates to lower the degree. Neither construction
is advertised as an optimal variable count.

\section{Quasi-Diophantine infinite progress\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:quasi}

\subsection{One clock, all finite obligations}
For a total deadline $d$, let $\delta_k=(d(1),\ldots,d(k))$ and use
$H_k=\max(k,d(1),\ldots,d(k))$ as in \cref{lbh:cs:sec:closed}. The preceding
theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:quasi}
 d\in\csClock_M\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \forall k\geq1\ \exists\mathbf z_k\in\N^{v_k}:
 P_{M,H_k,\delta_k}(\mathbf z_k)=0,
\end{equation}
where $v_k=3(H_k+1)+9mH_k+k$.

\begin{proposition}[Coherent quadratic progress]\label[proposition]{lbh:cs:prop:coherent}
Equivalence \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:quasi} holds. Its right side is also equivalent to
the existence of a compatible family of these canonical certificates,
where compatibility means agreement after truncating a labelled run and
retaining the helpers and deadline slacks that remain in the shorter
problem.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
An adequate run restricts to a legal prefix at every $H_k$, and its
canonical lift supplies all the required zeros. The lifts are compatible:
helper values depend only on local residuals, and the slack for an earlier
deadline depends only on the retained earlier part of the run.

Conversely, zeros at every level project to finite runs witnessing the
predicate $\operatorname{Good}_M(\delta_k)$. The compactness proof in
\cref{lbh:cs:thm:closed} gives a single infinite run meeting all deadlines.
Its canonical lifts provide a compatible family. Thus one need not assume
that an arbitrary initial choice of levelwise zeros was already compatible;
compactness selects a compatible family. Restriction maps themselves need
not be surjective onto every shorter zero.
\end{proof}
\srcnote{\wtag{} [\Cref{lbh:cs:prop:coherent} is the quartic schema \eqref{lbh:eq:schema} of
source~10 at degree two, for source~12's compiler; source~11's compatible certificates
(\cref{lbh:ql:cor:deadlinepoly}) are the counter-machine form. The remark after
\eqref{lbh:eq:schema} applies here too: for a fixed computable $d$ the right side is decidable level
by level, and the predicate is $\ppi1$.]}

This is the sense in which the construction is \emph{quasi-Diophantine}:
it is an explicit family of finite polynomial conditions with a shared
infinite functional parameter. Existence of a recurrent run is
\[
 \exists d\in\N^{\N_{>0}}\ \forall k\geq1\ \exists\mathbf z_k:
 P_{M,H_k,\delta_k}(\mathbf z_k)=0.
\]
Specifying that $d$ be computable from a particular oracle $X$ produces
exactly the spectra studied above. A natural number coding a computable
function is not a replacement for an arbitrary $d$. For example the
$\cszero''$-cone construction is recurrent but has no computable adequate
clock.

\subsection{What ordinary MRDP does and does not add}
Finite tuple coding turns
$\operatorname{Good}_M(\delta)$ into an ordinary decidable relation on
natural-number codes. MRDP therefore gives a fixed-arity polynomial
representation of that coded finite relation; this is the established
computability-to-Diophantine bridge used throughout the repository
\cite{cs-repo-liveness,cs-repo-readme,cs-matiyasevich}. Our explicit quadratics serve
a different purpose: they expose the actual finite configurations,
rule labels, and unique helper values, at the cost of horizon-dependent
arity.

A fixed-arity MRDP representation obtained by that general route is not
asserted here to preserve the unique fibers of
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly}. Nor does it remove the infinite clock parameter
or the quantifier over all finite obligations. The following distinctions
must therefore be kept separate:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}p{0.28\textwidth}Xp{0.22\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Object & What it certifies & Limitation\\
\midrule
$P_{M,T,\delta}$ & A labelled finite run with all listed deadlines & Arity grows with $T$\\
An MRDP polynomial for $\operatorname{Good}$ & Existence of a coded finite successful prefix & No fiber bound claimed\\
The family in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:quasi} & One infinite run fitting a fixed clock & Infinitely many obligations\\
Existential functional clock & Unrestricted recurrent computation & Not a finite natural witness\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

In particular, this paper does not prove single-fold or finite-fold MRDP,
does not produce a fixed universal quadratic Diophantine equation, and
does not improve a fixed universal polynomial's operation count. Its
finite zero-set bijections are exact but belong to a different representation
regime.

\section{Unconventional substrates with the same spectra\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:substrates}

\subsection{Congruence-guarded affine lattice computation}
The shifted stack equations already define a two-register affine substrate
with finite control. Its instructions multiply by two and add one or two,
or perform the partial affine inverses
\[
 u\mapsto\frac{u-1}{2}\quad(u\geq1,\ u\text{ odd}),\qquad
 u\mapsto\frac{u-2}{2}\quad(u\geq2,\ u\text{ even}).
\]
It also has the zero guard. Thus all the spectra above occur in an explicit
finite-control, parity-guarded rational affine relation on $\N^2$.
This is a two-register generalized-Collatz-style substrate in the stated
sense; no identification with a particular named one-register Collatz
problem is intended. The certificate compiler is native to its transitions.

\subsection{A rational affine relation inside a unit cube}
Encode a finite stack with top-first bits $b_1,\ldots,b_\ell$ by
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:realcode}
 x(b_1\cdots b_\ell)=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}\frac{1+2b_j}{4^j},
 \qquad x(\varepsilon)=0.
\end{equation}
Every code is rational in $[0,1)$, and finite words have distinct codes.
A push of $b$ has affine law
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:affine-push}
 x'=(x+1+2b)/4.
\end{equation}
A pop of $b$ has inverse law
\begin{equation}\label{lbh:cs:eq:affine-pop}
 x'=4x-(1+2b),
\end{equation}
with guard
\[
 x\in[1/4,1/2)\quad(b=0),\qquad
 x\in[3/4,1)\quad(b=1).
\]
An empty test is the guard $x=0$. Staying is the identity.

If the finite control states are numbered $0,\ldots,Q$, represent state
$q$ by $z=(q+1)/(Q+2)$. Each labelled two-stack rule becomes a rational
affine update of $(z,x,y)$ on its guarded domain: the state coordinate is
set to the target-state rational, and the other two coordinates use
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:affine-push}, \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:affine-pop}, or the identity. Overlapping
rule domains mean nondeterministic choice, just as in the source system.

\begin{theorem}[Exact affine transfer]\label{lbh:cs:thm:affine}
For every initialized finite two-stack table $M$, the construction above
gives a finite rational piecewise-affine relation in $[0,1]^3$ with a
rational initial point whose labelled trajectories are in step-for-step
bijection with those of $M$. Tick times, adequate deadlines, and clock
spectra are identical. Consequently the cones and nonprincipal spectra
proved in this paper occur in one fixed such relation with varying
finite rational inputs, after fixing the interpreter $U$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A nonempty finite stack code has first base-four digit $1$ or $3$ and a
remaining tail in $[0,1)$. This places it in exactly the corresponding
guard interval. Applying \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:affine-pop} removes that digit; applying
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:affine-push} inserts it. Empty and stay instructions are equally
exact. Thus every source rule agrees with its affine rule on coded
configurations, and every legal affine rule at a coded configuration
has the corresponding legal source operation.

Starting at the rational code of a finite configuration, induction shows
that every later point is again the code of a finite configuration. The
state update and labelled rule are preserved at every step. The encoding
is injective: in two distinct finite base-four expansions with digits
$1,3$, the first differing digit outweighs the difference of the finite
tails; a strict inequality follows because the tails are finite. Hence
the correspondence is bijective. Accepting states and transition counts
are unchanged, proving the clock assertions. Fixing the finite table of
$U$ fixes this affine relation; only its initial coded input varies.
\end{proof}
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The coding \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:realcode}--\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:affine-pop} is source~10's
\eqref{lbh:eq:rational-stack}--\eqref{lbh:eq:affine-top} after the relabelling $d=1+2b$, and
\cref{lbh:cs:thm:affine} is \cref{lbh:thm:geometry}, which already transfers the c.e.\ spectra; the
new spectra transfer by the same step-exact argument. Only the control coordinate differs:
$(q+1)/(Q+2)$ here, $q/(s+1)$ in source~10.]}

No continuity or noise tolerance is claimed. The ambient cube is compact,
but the set of finite codes is countable and not closed; the guards are
not those of a globally continuous map. The construction assumes exact
rational arithmetic of unbounded precision. In particular an arbitrary
nearby real point is not a substitute for a finite input word. The
polynomial certificates quantify natural stack codes, not arbitrary
rational coordinates, and do not resolve Hilbert's tenth problem over
$\Q$.

\subsection{Why timing preservation matters}
Turing completeness by itself is not enough to transport a clock
spectrum. A simulator could introduce an infinite silent computation
while trying to implement a single source step, or could manufacture
accepting events during housekeeping. Our constructions exclude both:
the tree explorer emits only after total completed checks, the fixed
interpreter has explicitly designated event states, and the affine
translation preserves every primitive step. General timing-faithful
simulation is discussed in the earlier report \cite{cs-repo-liveness}; the
present implementations state the necessary conditions rather than
appeal to an unspecified universality reduction.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The earlier report's discussion is \cref{lbh:sec:simulation}.]}

\section{Implementation, experiments, and reproducibility\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:implementation}

\subsection{What the package implements}
The accompanying \code{code/clock\_certificates.py} is a standard-library-only
Python implementation. It provides a sparse exact integer polynomial
representation; a validated finite rule format; the quadratic compiler
\eqref{lbh:cs:eq:complete-poly}; a natural-domain zero checker; canonical lifting
of a labelled trace; JSON export of the complete polynomial; and the
finite-node predicate of \cref{lbh:cs:thm:firstmatch}. An independent list-of-bits
interpreter is used to generate traces. It does not use the affine residual
formulas to decide stack transitions.

The compiler rejects negative horizons and out-of-range deadlines, preserves
all inactive-rule helpers, checks that the supplied assignment has exactly
the declared variables, and rejects negative coordinates before evaluating
the polynomial. A JSON export is a full monomial list, not merely a hash,
operation count, or informal circuit description. Coefficients and values
use arbitrary-precision integers.

The code does \emph{not} implement an oracle for $A$, the transfinite jump,
a general infinite-path solver, or a small fixed universal table. The
function \code{first\_matches} takes the finite queried values of the
limit as an explicitly supplied callable. Its example limit is computable.
The transfinite theorem is proved mathematically, not claimed to be
validated by a finite toy halting experiment.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Delivered names. \path{code/clock_certificates.py}, \path{code/test_all.py},
\path{code/verify_export.py}, \path{examples/quadratic_certificate.json},
\path{test_results.json}, \path{build.sh} and \path{source_audit.md} are shipped as
\path{code/12-clock-spectra-clock_certificates.py}, \path{code/12-clock-spectra-test_all.py},
\path{code/12-clock-spectra-verify_export.py}, \path{data/12-clock-spectra-quadratic_certificate.json},
\path{data/12-clock-spectra-test_results.json}, \path{code/12-clock-spectra-build.sh} and
\path{12-clock-spectra-source_audit.md}. The shipped program is the unpatched original; the
programme's review found two input-boundary defects in it and supplies a patch
(\cref{lbh:cs:sec:review}).]}

\subsection{A complete finite certificate example}
The seven-rule test fixture has states $0,1,2$, accepting state $2$, and
initial configuration $(0,0,0)$:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}clll@{}}
\toprule
Label & State change & Left stack & Right stack\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0\to0$ & Push $0$ & Stay\\
$1$ & $0\to0$ & Push $1$ & Stay\\
$2$ & $0\to1$ & Stay & Stay\\
$3$ & $1\to1$ & Pop $0$ & Push $0$\\
$4$ & $1\to1$ & Pop $1$ & Push $1$\\
$5$ & $1\to2$ & Empty test & Stay\\
$6$ & $2\to0$ & Stay & Stay\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
This is a \emph{nonuniversal test fixture}. It guesses a finite left-stack
word, transfers its bits to the right, ticks when the left stack is empty,
and returns to state $0$.

For horizon $T=5$, deadline $d_1=4$, and labels $(1,2,4,5,6)$, the
configuration sequence is
\[
 (0,0,0),\ (0,2,0),\ (1,2,0),\ (1,0,2),\ (2,0,2),\ (0,0,2).
\]
There is one tick, at time $4$, and the deadline slack is zero. The
exported polynomial has degree $2$, $334$ declared variables, and $1494$
nonzero monomials. Its canonical zero assignment is included in
\path{examples/quadratic_certificate.json}. The variable count is exactly
$3\cdot6+9\cdot7\cdot5+1=334$.

\subsection{Exact finite checks performed}
The supplied test program was run under Python 3.13.5. All checks passed.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Check & Cases\\
\midrule
Guard gadget, $-8\leq D\leq8$, $0\leq s\leq2$, $0\leq a,b\leq10$ & 6,171\\
Stack residuals versus independent list semantics & 49,920\\
Labelled one-step configuration candidates versus list semantics & 81,648\\
Finite trace/deadline combinations through horizon six & 817\\
Accepted canonical certificates among those combinations & 314\\
Deadline failures rejected among those combinations & 503\\
Admissible one-coordinate unit perturbations rejected at the sample zero & 430\\
First-match-tree candidate nodes through length four & 4,681\\
Explicit malformed-input cases rejected & 7\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The two trace outcome rows partition the 817 combinations; they are not
additional independent test inputs. The local transition test ranges over
all source and target states in $\{0,1,2\}$, both stack codes in
$\{0,\ldots,5\}$, and every fixture rule. The stack-only test uses old
codes $0$ through $63$, new codes $0$ through $129$, and all six operations.
The first-match test includes the early-match/nonsettling example and
checks prefix closure on every accepted node in its finite search.

These experiments can detect sign errors, wrong parity, missing empty
conditions, degree/count discrepancies, and unconstrained helpers. They
do not exhaust the natural domain of the polynomial and do not establish
any infinitary theorem. Those conclusions rely on the proofs, not the
number of tested cases.

\subsection{Reproduction commands and formalization targets}
From the extracted package directory run
\begin{lstlisting}
python3 code/test_all.py
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The first command regenerates the complete example polynomial and
\code{test\_results.json}. The TeX source uses an internal bibliography;
no BibTeX run or network access is required.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [The listing keeps the delivered layout: \path{article.tex} is source~12's
manuscript, which is not shipped, and the programs import one another by delivered names and
rewrite \path{examples/} and \path{test_results.json}. Rerun them on a copy with the delivered
layout, as the README describes. At this write (Python 3.14.4) both commands passed; the regenerated
certificate equals the shipped one after LF normalization, and the test record differs only in the
\code{python} field.]}

A proof-assistant implementation should keep three interfaces separate.
The finite algebraic layer proves the guard lemma and the labelled-trace
bijection over naturals. The semantic layer proves the explorer's
path-bounding correspondence with explicit primitive runtime. The
computability layer proves unique bounded-path recovery and the
first-match-tree theorem. Only the last step of \cref{lbh:cs:thm:least} needs
the external hyperarithmetic modulus theorem. Keeping this dependency
isolated makes the remaining main constructive results independently
formalizable without importing the entire hyperarithmetic hierarchy.

\section{Further research questions\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:sec:questions}

These are precise questions left open by this article. They are not all
claimed to be new or previously published open problems; overlap with the
repository's existing agenda is identified where relevant.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The eight questions of source~12 follow, unchanged. Labels were added to them; a
bracketed note after a question names an overlapping question of \cref{lbh:sec:questions}.}

\begin{question}[The least-degree classification]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:classification}
Which of the inclusions in \eqref{lbh:cs:eq:gap} are equalities? Equivalently,
which degrees can be least among the degrees computing a member of a
nonempty upward effectively closed Baire-space class?
\end{question}
This sharpens the repository's spectrum question to a representation
problem for effective closed classes. A proof cannot simply identify
nonuniform and uniform self-moduli.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [It refines \cref{lbh:q:spectra}, which source~12 answers in part
(\cref{lbh:cs:sec:relation}).]}

\begin{question}[Realizing a nonuniform self-modulus]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:selfmodulus}
Suppose $f$ is a self-modulus, with no uniform recovery procedure assumed.
Can one effectively present an upward closed class whose solution degrees
form exactly $\csCone{\csdegT(f)}$, using suitable finite definability data for
$f$? What additional hypothesis on that data is necessary?
\end{question}
The input must be finite. Treating the entire noncomputable function as
a machine parameter would trivialize the problem and is not an answer.
Gerdes's definability questions provide a relevant classical boundary
\cite{cs-gerdes}.

\begin{question}[Minimal versus least resources]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:minimal}
Which antichains occur as the complete set of minimal degrees of a clock
spectrum? Can a spectrum with minimal degrees but no least degree have
minimal elements not containing a self-modulus?
\end{question}
The proof of \cref{lbh:cs:thm:least} uses leastness, not merely minimality.
The union construction supplies controlled finite examples but does not
classify general antichains or spectra with no minimal elements.

\begin{question}[Numerically instantiated universality]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:numerical}
Instantiate the fixed binary two-stack interpreter, including input
loading, error behavior, and tick control, and export its complete finite
quadratic certificates. What are the actual rule count, coefficient
heights, and arithmetic evaluation costs?
\end{question}
This is a concrete missing implementation step. A numerical claim must
include the transition table and the ordinary-input encoding, not merely
a universality citation.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Overlapping questions: \cref{lbh:q:qd-universal} of source~03, with source~11's
overlapping ``A completely expanded fixed universal law'' printed after it, and source~10's remark
after \cref{lbh:sec:universal} that no explicit small universal table is claimed.]}

\begin{question}[Lower-cost canonical quadratic guards]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:guards}
Can sign helpers be shared across rules with related affine residuals
while preserving every complete natural witness tuple, or at least a
specified bijection of zero sets? Can the $8mT$ helper budget be reduced
without raising degree or introducing unconstrained inactive witnesses?
\end{question}
Any proposed saving should state whether it preserves supplied tuples,
projects fibers, or gives only equivalence of the outer accepted relation.
These are different guarantees.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Overlapping question: ``Smaller exact local certificates'' in
\cref{lbh:sec:questions}, which asks the same for source~10's quartic counts while keeping degree
at most four; \cref{lbh:cs:thm:quadratic} lowers the degree to two and pays $8mT$ helpers.]}

\begin{question}[Verified first-match compilation]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:verified}
Formalize \cref{lbh:cs:thm:firstmatch} uniformly in a computable approximation,
including the distinction between first matches and settling times, and
compose it with a verified tree interpreter and polynomial compiler.
\end{question}
This would give a small, auditable formal answer to a portion of the
repository's spectrum question without requiring a new priority argument.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Overlapping question: ``A verified effective-closed-subclass compiler'' in
\cref{lbh:sec:questions}, which asks for the verified compiler from a certified tree to a deadline
program.]}

\begin{question}[Resource-bounded clocks and native substrates]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:resource}
Which of the realized spectra survive when the finite-maximum conversion
$b\mapsto d_b$ is constrained to primitive-recursive, elementary, or other
specified overheads? Can equally explicit clock-preserving certificates
be built for graph interaction, queue rewriting, or prioritized reaction
substrates?
\end{question}
The arbitrary total tree decider permits arbitrarily expensive checks.
The finite maximum is computable but not automatically in a fixed resource
class. This refines the related resource-clock and substrate questions
already present in \cite{cs-repo-liveness}.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Overlapping questions: ``Primitive-recursive and resource-bounded clocks'' in
\cref{lbh:sec:questions} with source~11's overlapping ``Witness spectra beyond computable deadlines''
printed after it, and ``Other unconventional substrates with timing certificates'' there.]}

\begin{question}[Robust affine semantics]\label[question]{lbh:cs:q:robust}
Which deadline spectra remain realizable after replacing exact rational
codes by a specified perturbation model, or after demanding a globally
continuous dynamics? Can a noise-tolerant coding preserve least clock
degrees without putting infinite information into the initial condition?
\end{question}
The exact affine construction alone does not answer this. Its high-order
digits are unbounded memory, and uniform positive noise can erase them.
\srcnote{\wtag{} [Overlapping question: ``Robust encodings and noise'' in \cref{lbh:sec:questions},
with source~11's overlapping ``Robust planar computation'' printed after it.]}

\section{Conclusion\srctag{12}}
\wtag{} [``The repository's c.e.\ clock-cone construction'' is \cref{lbh:thm:ce-spectrum}.]\par
The repository's c.e.\ clock-cone construction extends substantially
without assuming that an arbitrary limit-computable set can compute the
settling time of its approximation. First matching stages supply a
recursive singleton tree of exactly the desired degree. Tree exploration
then turns any bound on that unique path into an adequate progress clock,
and conversely any adequate clock into enough information to recover the
path. Deferred finite oracle checks extend the construction to explicit
jump hierarchies. Disjoint sums produce nonprincipal spectra; leastness,
when present, forces a self-modulus and hence a hyperarithmetic degree.

The arithmetic realization is equally explicit at the finite level:
quadratic natural zero sets encode the labelled computations with unique
helpers and all deadlines retained. The same clocks occur in exact
rational affine dynamics through a stepwise encoding. The distinction
between a finite polynomial witness and an infinite shared clock is not
an obstacle to hide; it is the organizing principle that makes the
representation faithful.

\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~12's two appendices follow as the last two sections of Part~VI, so that this report's appendix lettering is unchanged.}
\section{Pseudocode for the two tree constructions (source 12's Appendix A)\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:app:trees}

The first-matching-stage checker is particularly short. Here
\code{Approx(s,n)} returns the first $n$ bits of stage $s$.
\begin{lstlisting}
FirstMatchNode(s[1..k]):
    previous_word := empty
    for n := 1,...,k:
        if s[n] < n: reject
        word := Approx(s[n], n)
        if prefix(word, n-1) != previous_word: reject
        for t := n,...,s[n]-1:
            if Approx(t, n) == word: reject
        previous_word := word
    accept
\end{lstlisting}
No query to the limit $A$ is made by this checker. The approximation's
convergence is used only in the proof that its infinite path is unique.

The deferred hierarchy checker has a different structure:
\begin{lstlisting}
HierarchyNode(sigma):
    L := length(sigma)
    for each coordinate i < L:
        (a,n) := unpair(i)
        if a not in D:
            if sigma[i] != 0: reject
            continue
        partial_oracle(b,m):
            if b not in D or not (b <_L a): return 0
            j := pair(b,m)
            if j >= L: return UNKNOWN
            return indicator(sigma[j] > 0)
        if sigma[i] > 0:
            t := sigma[i]-1
            simulate machine n on n up to first halt or time t
            if UNKNOWN is queried: defer this coordinate
            else if first halt time is not exactly t: reject
        else:
            simulate machine n on n for L steps
            if UNKNOWN is queried: defer this coordinate
            else if a halt is witnessed: reject
    accept
\end{lstlisting}
For a positive claim, ``defer'' is permitted only when the simulation
actually reaches an unavailable query before its verification is complete.
An already observed wrong halting time must be rejected. In both cases
already established rejection remains valid under extension.

\section{An audit checklist for the proofs and implementation (source 12's Appendix B)\srctag{12}}\label{lbh:cs:app:audit}

The domain is always $\N$ in the finite polynomial theorem. The residuals
are affine, and no hidden multiplication of two residual variables occurs
inside $D$. Each selected rule is constrained by both its source and target
state equations. Every inactive rule has canonical sign helpers. Empty
stack tests use a sum of two natural codes, not a signed-integer shortcut.
Tick counts include only transitions entering designated accepting states,
not the initial state or arbitrary simulator states. Every finite progress
test includes all earlier deadlines. The tree-to-clock conversion takes
finite maxima of total simulations and never assumes a computable path.
The unique-path recovery takes finite bounded levels and uses compactness
only to prove that its search terminates. The jump checker defers unknown
oracle queries and does not assume that lower rows occur earlier in the
coordinate enumeration. The least-degree argument proves a nonuniform
self-modulus and invokes the modulus theorem only for hyperarithmeticity.
Finally, horizon-dependent unique finite witnesses are not promoted to a
single-fold fixed-arity MRDP representation.

under those names: this report's \path{article.tex} and \path{article.pdf} replace the manuscript,
this report's README replaces the delivery README, and the ledger is shipped as
\path{10-progress-deadlines-source_ledger.md}; the programs and artifacts are shipped under the
names of \cref{lbh:sec:provenance}. Rerun the programs on a copy with the delivered layout, as the
README describes; in this repository's shell \code{py} replaces \code{python3} and
\code{python}.}
\section{Reproduction guide\srctag{03}}\label{lbh:sec:app-qd-reproduction}

From the package directory, run:
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py
python code/quadratic_compiler.py examples/finite_visits.json \
  --output examples/finite_visits_polynomial.json
latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The Python implementation requires Python 3.10 or later and no third-party
packages. The PDF uses standard pdfLaTeX packages listed in its preamble.
The JSON polynomial format lists variables in source-state order followed
by target-state order. A monomial is a sorted tuple of variable indices;
the empty tuple denotes a constant. Repeated indices denote a square.

The verification script regenerates the polynomial examples, sample finite
runs, and its report. It intentionally does not implement an oracle for the
diagonal trees or attempt to test an infinite-path theorem by bounded
search. Its complete source states every finite range and sampling choice.
\srcnote{\wtag{} The listing keeps source~03's delivered layout; its programs are shipped as
\path{code/03-quadratic-recurrence-quadratic_compiler.py} and
\path{code/03-quadratic-recurrence-verify.py}, its examples and reports under
\path{data/03-quadratic-recurrence-*}. Rerun on a copy with the delivered layout (README);
in this repository's shell \code{py} replaces \code{python}.}
\section{The explicit eight-dimensional budget example\srctag{11}}
\label{lbh:ql:app:example}
Order the seven edges as follows:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{clll}
\toprule
Index & Source & Target & Effect or guard\\
\midrule
0 & $*$ & $G$ & dummy no-op\\
1 & $G$ & $I$ & no-op\\
2 & $G$ & $D$ & no-op\\
3 & $I$ & $G$ & increment $x$\\
4 & $D$ & $A$ & decrement $x$\\
5 & $D$ & $H$ & test $x=0$\\
6 & $A$ & $D$ & no-op\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The current state is $(x,b_0,\ldots,b_6)$ and the next state is
$(y,c_0,\ldots,c_6)$. The exact quadratic polynomial, in residual form, is
\begin{align*}
 P_2={}&\left(\sum_{j=0}^6b_j-1\right)^2
       +\left(\sum_{j=0}^6c_j-1\right)^2+c_0^2\\
 &+(b_0+b_3-c_1-c_2)^2+(b_1-c_3)^2\\
 &+(b_2+b_6-c_4-c_5)^2+(b_4-c_6)^2+b_5^2\\
 &+(y-x-c_3+c_4)^2+c_5x.
\end{align*}
Replace the final $c_5x$ by $c_5^2x^2$ for $P_4$. The marker is $A(b)=b_4$,
and the initial state is $(0,1,0,0,0,0,0,0)$. This complete example can be
checked directly without trusting any program generator.

The $j$th marked visit after choosing budget $n\geq1$ occurs at
\[
 \tau_j=2n+2j,\qquad 1\leq j\leq n.
\]
Indeed choosing the budget takes $2n$ transitions, entering $D$ takes one,
and its first decrement takes one more to reach $A$. Subsequent marked visits
are separated by two transitions. Hence the last visit is at time $4n$.
This proves the finite-prefix assertion for all $n$, beyond the tested range.

\section{A minimal proof checklist for reuse\srctag{11}}\label{lbh:sec:app-ql-checklist}
A future application of this report's normal form should discharge all of the
following obligations in its own semantics:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item Its state encoding is injective on the intended finite configurations.
\item Every emitted counter edge has one of the permitted effects and guards.
\item Dummy initialization cannot occur as a later transition.
\item Incoming-edge identity is retained when different choices would otherwise
lead to the same machine configuration.
\item Marked events correspond to the intended observations, rather than to
unbounded internal work.
\item A claimed computable deadline is total, and its time unit is specified.
\item Finite-prefix certificates enforce all earlier deadlines simultaneously.
\item Any macro simulation used for an infinite property has terminating
internal routines or a proof that internal divergence cannot satisfy the
observation.
\end{enumerate}
Omitting one of these conditions can preserve ordinary finite reachability
while invalidating a recurrence or witness-spectrum conclusion.
\srcnote{\wtag{} Source~11's reproduction commands are in \cref{lbh:ql:sec:verification}; its
programs and data are shipped with the prefix \path{11-quadratic-laws-} (README).}
\clearpage
\section{A compact theorem-dependency ledger}\label{lbh:app:dependencies}
\begin{longtable}{@{}p{0.27\linewidth}p{0.38\linewidth}p{0.27\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
Endpoint & Principal ingredients & Verification status\\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Endpoint & Principal ingredients & Verification status\\
\midrule
\endhead
Coherent compactification & Finite run semantics; computable binary tree; K\"onig compactness & Conventional proof.\\
Clock/closed/HIF equivalence & Compactification; uniform bounds on a compact class; classical HIF basis & Conventional proof; basis attributed.\\
C.e.-degree spectrum & Bounded enumeration tree; unary cost; enumeration modulus & Conventional explicit construction.\\
Hierarchy upper bounds & Finite decidability; common program index; analytic schedule quantifier & Conventional proof.\\
Hierarchy lower bounds & Unary witness selection; decidable search; recursive-tree normal form & Conventional reductions; recurrence endpoint classical.\\
Finite quartic bijection & One-hot indicators; canonical sentinel stacks; inactive slack equations & Conventional proof and finite exact tests.\\
Compact affine realization & Rational push/pop identities; finite linear guards & Conventional proof and exact identity tests.\\
Initial-parameter hierarchy & Cantor digit coding; rational periodicity; schedule-degree equivalence & Conventional proof.\\
Fixed-polynomial forms & MRDP; polynomial parameter pairing; quadratic circuit lifting & Classical backend; no general extractor implemented.\\
No-go theorem & Diophantine sets are c.e.; strict hierarchy and analytic hardness & Conventional consequence.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
